Acceleration or finite speed propagation in integro-differential equations with logarithmic Allee effect
Emeric Bouin, Jérôme Coville, Xi Zhang

Abstract
This paper is devoted to studying propagation phenomena in integro-differential equations with a weakly degenerate non-linearity. The reaction term can be seen as an intermediate between the classical logistic (or Fisher-KPP) non-linearity and the standard weak Allee effect one. We study the effect of the tails of the dispersal kernel on the rate of expansion. When the tail of the kernel is sub-exponential, the exact separation between existence and non-existence of travelling waves is exhibited. This, in turn, provides the exact separation between finite speed propagation and acceleration in the Cauchy problem. Moreover, the exact rates of acceleration for dispersal kernels with sub-exponential and algebraic tails are provided. Our approach is generic and covers a large variety of dispersal kernels including those leading to convolution and fractional Laplace operators. Numerical simulations are provided to illustrate our results.



Keywords:
travelling wave, acceleration, singular integral operator, fractional Laplacian, weakly degenerate equation

Mathematics Subject Classification:
35B40, 35C07, 35K57, 35R09, 35R11, 45G05, 47G10, 92D25

1
Introduction

This paper sheds light on propagation phenomena arising in semilinear equations of the form

\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}(t,x)=\mathcal{D}[u](t,x)+f(u(t,x)),\hskip 9.24994pt(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R},

(1.1)

where
f\in C^{1}([0,1],{\mathbb{R}}^{+})
is a monostable function such that
f(0)=f(1)=0,f^{\prime}(1)<0
and
{\mathcal{D}}
a nonlocal operator defined as
1
1

1

The notation P.V. indicates that the integral is understood in the sense of the Cauchy principal value.

\mathcal{D}[u](t,x):=\text{P.V.}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Big(u(t,x-y)-u(t,x)\Big)J(y)dy,

with
J
a non-negative kernel.

This type of equation arises naturally in population dynamics when long-distance dispersal events are considered. In such a context, the value
u(t,x)
represents an adimensional density of individuals of a species at time
t
and spatial location
x
. The operator
{\mathcal{D}}
corresponds to the description of the movement of the population modelled by a jump process of probability density
J
, while the nonlinearity
f
describes the demography.

In recent decades, propagation phenomena in reaction-diffusion and integro-differential equations have been the focus of considerable research efforts, starting from the seminal contributions of Kolmogorov, Petrovskii and Piskunov
[
30
]
and Fisher
[
22
]
.

When the growth rate per capita is maximal at small densities, i.e.
f
satisfies the so-called Fisher-KPP condition,

0<f(z)\leq f^{\prime}(0)z\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }z\in(0,1),

(1.2)

a solution to (
1.1
) exhibits some propagation phenomena. That is, starting with some initial data with nonnegative nontrivial compact support, the corresponding solution
u
converges to
1
, its stable steady state, for large times, locally uniformly in space. This is referred as the
hair trigger effect

[
4
]
.

A substantial body of research has been dedicated to classifying this invasion phenomenon according to the properties of
J
and
f
, specifically investigating the propagation speed of solutions to (
1.1
) when it arises. The dispersal kernel plays a significant role in influencing the propagation speed.
When it is exponentially bounded, in the sense,

\exists\varepsilon>0,\hskip 9.24994pt\int_{\mathbb{R}}J(y)e^{\varepsilon|y|}dy<\infty,

(1.3)

travelling wave solutions exist and the solution to the corresponding Cauchy problem propagates at finite speed
[
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
,
31
,
33
,
35
,
36
]
.
Having an exponential moment for
J
, i.e. condition (
1.3
), is actually a necessary condition for the existence of travelling wave solution when
f
is such that
f^{\prime}(0)>0
, see
[
36
]
.

When the kernel
J
is exponentially unbounded, that is (
1.3
) is not met, and
f
is monostable with
f^{\prime}(0)>0
, Garnier
[
23
]
proved the existence of accelerating solutions and gave the first estimate of rates of propagation.
From his results, we learn that if the kernel
J
is sub-exponential, that is,
J\propto e^{-\mu|\cdot|^{\beta}}
with
\beta\in(0,1)
and
\mu>0
, then the propagation is algebraic, while if the kernel
J
is algebraic, that is
J\propto|\cdot|^{-(1+2s)}
for
s>\frac{1}{2}
, then it is exponential. At the same time, Cabré and Roquejoffre
[
12
]
obtain similar results for the fractional Laplacian:
\ln|X_{\lambda}(t)|\sim_{t\to\infty}\frac{f^{\prime}(0)}{1+2s}t
, whenever
u(t,X_{\lambda}(t))=\lambda
, for all
t>0
.

Later, for integrable kernels
J
, the bounds obtained by Garnier were improved by Finkelshtein and collaborators
[
20
,
21
]
and by the first author with Garnier, Henderson and Patout
[
10
]
. The latter, by constructing a pair of precise sub- and super- solutions, obtained the following sharp estimate for
t
large enough:
X_{\lambda}(t)\asymp_{\lambda}J^{-1}(e^{-t})
.
Although this estimate was obtained for an integrable kernel
J
, the arguments used do not require such a specific assumption and can be adapted to the fractional Laplacian, thus extending the scope of validity of this estimate.
For such a monostable nonlinearity
f
, a clear dichotomy in the propagation behaviour can then be observed: if the dispersal kernel
J
is exponentially bounded, finite speed propagation occurs; otherwise, it leads to acceleration.

When the growth per capita does not imply an exponential growth at low density,
i.e.

f^{\prime}(0)=0
, propagation still occurs but its study is more subtle. When (
1.1
) is set with a monostable nonlinearity
f
satisfying:
f(z)\sim_{z\to 0^{+}}rz^{\alpha+1}
with
r>0
, Gui and Huan
[
26
]
proved existence of travelling front solutions for (
1.1
) when
\alpha>1
and
{\mathcal{D}}
is the fractional Laplace operator with
2>2s\geq 1+\frac{1}{\alpha}
. They also showed that this condition is sharp, in the sense that no front solution can exist when
2s<\min\left\{1+\tfrac{1}{\alpha},2\right\}
. Later, for an integrable kernel
J
such that
J\propto|\cdot|^{-(1+2s)}
the second author with Alfaro
[
2
]
showed that for any
\alpha>0
, the value
2s=1+\frac{1}{\alpha}
is the sharp threshold for the existence of travelling fronts. More precisely, for
J
having a bounded first moment and that satisfies

J(z)\lesssim|z|^{-\left(2+\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }z\geq 1,

(1.4)

they proved that travelling waves exist and that the solution to the corresponding Cauchy problem propagates at a constant speed, whereas if the kernel
J
does not enjoy such a decay no travelling waves can exist. In addition, they provided first estimates of the invasion rate for solutions to the associated Cauchy problem with front-like initial data
u_{0}
, showing a polynomial invasion rate
2
2

2

Explicitly, their result say that for any
\lambda\in(0,1)
and any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
large enough,
t^{1/2s\alpha}\leq_{\lambda}X_{\lambda}(t)\leq_{\lambda}t^{1/2s\alpha+1/2s}
, where
u(t,X_{\lambda}(t))=\lambda
.
.
Subsequently, the second author, with Gui and Zhao, considered in
[
17
]
the case of
{\mathcal{D}}
being a fractional Laplacian and derived similar acceleration estimates of polynomial type when
2s<\min\left\{1+\tfrac{1}{\alpha},2\right\}
.
More recently, the first two authors with Legendre
[
7
]
established the following sharp estimate for generic algebraically decaying kernel
J
:

X_{\lambda}(t)\asymp_{\lambda}t^{\frac{\alpha+1}{2s\alpha}},

with
1+\frac{1}{\alpha}>2s
. It is worth mentioning that a parallel work by Zhang and Zlatoš
[
37
]
obtains similar estimates when
{\mathcal{D}}
is the fractional Laplacian and
2s<\min\left\{1+\tfrac{1}{\alpha},2\right\}
, using a different construction.

It is worth mentioning that an acceleration phenomenon also occurs in classical reaction-diffusion equations, especially with fat-tailed initial data. We refer to
[
1
,
3
,
27
]
and references therein for more details.

These two dichotomies show that the description of propagation phenomena in (
1.1
) is governed by a strong interplay between the property of the nonlinearity
f
near zero and the decay of the dispersal kernel
J
at infinity. The behaviour of
f
near zero seems critical in determining the right decay of
J
for which we can observe propagation at a finite speed. While these two classical types of monostable non-linearities encompass a wide range of growth models, there are scenarios where they do not model growth adequately. This raises the question of how sensitive the above results are to the degeneracy of the nonlinearity
f
.

The primary goal of this paper is to complement the knowledge on how the degeneracy of
f
at zero affects different transitions, particularly aiming to gain fresh insights on the dichotomy between accelerated and finite speed regimes, depending on the behaviour of the nonlinearity

f
near zero. To do so, we examine a class of monostable non-linearities that we call ”weakly degenerate”, which lie between
f(z)\sim_{z\to 0^{+}}rz
and
f(z)\sim_{z\to 0^{+}}rz^{\alpha+1}
for
\alpha>0
. More precisely, the non-linearities
f
satisfy the following conditions:

Hypothesis 1.1
.

The function
f
is of class
C^{1}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
, with

f(0)=f(1)=0>f^{\prime}(1),\hskip 9.24994ptf(z)>0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for any }z\in(0,1),

and there exist
\xi_{0}\in(0,1)
,
K\geq 0
,
\alpha>0
and
r>0
such that

f(z)\leq\frac{rz}{(1+\lvert\ln z\rvert)^{\alpha}}\hskip 18.49988pt\text{for all }z\in(0,1),

and

f(z)\geq\frac{rz(1-Kz)}{(1+\lvert\ln z\rvert)^{\alpha}}\hskip 18.49988pt\text{for all }z\in(0,\xi_{0}],

(See Fig.
1
).

0
z
\left.\frac{f(z)}{z}\right|_{z\approx 0}
r
\tfrac{r}{\lvert\ln z\rvert^{\alpha}}
rz^{\alpha}
0
1
z
f(z)
rz(1-z)
r\frac{z(1-z)}{(1+\lvert\ln z\rvert)^{\alpha}}
rz^{\alpha+1}(1-z)

Figure 1:
Comparison of the size of three types of non-linearities with the positive parameters
r
and
\alpha
.

The weakly degenerate non-linearity serves as a framework to examine the transition between degeneracy and non-degeneracy.
The introduction of logarithmic corrections is not only mathematically interesting but also supported by extensive literature modelling specific effects, such as the Gompertz growth rate in population dynamics
[
25
]
and in quantum physics
[
5
]
. Here, the logarithmic correction in growth can be interpreted as an Allee effect induced by non-trivial factors, in line with Dennis’s work
[
18
]
. As a motivating curiosity, non-linearities with logarithmic terms naturally emerge from non-local Fisher-KPP equations, and phenomena with non-trivial propagation features have been studied
[
10
,
11
]
. In a previous paper
[
9
]
, we have investigated rates of propagation with a local dispersal operator (a laplacian) under a wide class of initial data. For sub-exponential initial data, finite speed propagation may happen or not, while for sub-exponentially unbounded initial data, acceleration necessarily occurs. Some precise rates of invasion were given. One may connect the present contribution to the former one by having in mind that a nonlocal Cauchy problem such as (
1.1
) starting for a Heaviside initial data gives immediately (that is, at
t>0
) to
u(t,\cdot)
tails that are comparable to the decay of
J
at infinity. It is thus natural that the local version studied in
[
9
]
also yielded discussions related to sub-exponential or algebraic tails of
u_{0}
.

Let us now make precise the dispersal kernels
J
we will consider all along this paper. We will always assume that
J
satisfies:

Hypothesis 1.2
.

The kernel
J
is nonnegative, symmetric and satisfies the following conditions: there exist positive constants
\mathcal{J}_{0}
,
\mathcal{J}_{1}
and
R_{0}>1
, along with a decaying function
\mathcal{Z}\in L^{1}([1,+\infty);\mathbb{R}^{+})
, such that

\int_{|z|\leq 1}z^{2}J(z)dz\leq 2\mathcal{J}_{1}\hskip 18.49988pt\text{and}\hskip 18.49988pt\mathcal{J}_{0}^{-1}\mathcal{Z}(\cdot)\mathds{1}_{\{|z|\geq R_{0}\}}(\cdot)\leq J(\cdot)\leq\mathcal{J}_{0}\mathcal{Z}(\cdot)\hskip 9.24994pt\text{in }[1,+\infty).

There are two common cases covered by the above hypothesis: the standard convolution operators
J*u-u
with integrable kernels and the fractional Laplacian
(-\triangle)^{s}
.

The tails of the dispersal kernel play a key role on the propagation phenomena in equation (
1.1
). Our first result is about existence or non-existence of travelling wave solutions.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
\alpha>0
and
\beta>0
be such that
\beta\geq\frac{1}{\alpha+1}.
Assume that the dispersal kernel
J
satisfies Hypothesis
1.2
with a sub-exponentially bounded tail, that is,

\mathcal{Z}(z)\leq e^{-z^{\beta}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }z\geq 1,

(1.5)

and that the non-linearity
f
satisfies Hypothesis
1.1
.
Then there exists
c^{*}>0
such that for any
c\geq c^{*}
equation (
1.1
) admits travelling wave solutions
(c,u)
, whereas, for all
c<c^{*}
equation (
1.1
) does not admit travelling wave solutions.

Sub-exponential kernels turn out to be critical for the existence of travelling waves, in the following sense,

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
\alpha>0
and
\beta>0
be such that
\beta<\frac{1}{\alpha+1}.
Assume that the dispersal kernel
J
satisfies Hypothesis
1.2
with

\mathcal{Z}(z)\geq e^{-z^{\beta}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }z\geq 1,

and that the non-linearity
f
satisfies Hypothesis
1.1
.
Then there is no travelling wave solution to (
1.1
).

We shall now consider the Cauchy problem associated to (
1.1
) with the following initial condition:

Hypothesis 1.5
.

The initial condition
u_{0}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
is of class
C^{1}
and satisfies

0\leq u_{0}\leq 1\ \text{on}\ \mathbb{R},\hskip 9.24994pt\liminf_{x\to-\infty}u_{0}>0\text{ and }u_{0}\equiv 0\text{ on }[a,+\infty)\text{ for some }a.

Usually, existence of travelling waves leads to finite speed propagation. When the kernel of the integral operator
\mathcal{D}[\cdot]
has sub-exponential tails with
\beta<\frac{1}{\alpha+1}
, an acceleration phenomenon occurs, and we are able to provide the rate of invasion. In order to estimate this accelerated rate of invasion, we introduce some notations.

For any
\lambda\in(0,1)
, the (lower) level set of
u(t,\cdot)
at time
t
is defined by

E_{\lambda}(t):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}:u(t,x)\leq\lambda\}.

Let
X_{\lambda}(t)
be the smallest element of the level set of
u(t,\cdot)
defined by
3
3

3

When the initial data
u_{0}
is non-increasing, this element can also be defined by

X_{\lambda}(t)=\sup\left\{x\in{\mathbb{R}},\,u(t,x)\geq\lambda\right\}.

X_{\lambda}(t):=\inf E_{\lambda}(t).

Our first result closes the case of sub-exponential tails.

Theorem 1.6

(Algebraic propagation)
.

Assume
0<\beta<\frac{1}{\alpha+1}
and that
J
satisfies Hypothesis
1.2
with a sub-exponential tail, that is,

\mathcal{Z}(z)=e^{-z^{\beta}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }z\geq 1,

(1.6)

and that the non-linearity
f
and the initial data
u_{0}
satisfy Hypothesis
1.1
and Hypothesis
1.5
, respectively. Then, for any
\lambda\in(0,1)
, we have
4
4

4

The notation
X\asymp_{\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2},\dots}Y
means that there exists a positive constant
C_{\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2},\dots}
, depending only on some parameters
\Lambda_{1}
,
\Lambda_{2}
,…, such that
C_{\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2},\dots}Y\leq X\leq C^{-1}_{\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2},\dots}Y
.

X_{\lambda}(t)\asymp_{\lambda}t^{\frac{1}{\beta(\alpha+1)}}.

To have a more clear picture of our results, we summarize them in the diagram below (
Figure

2
).

➋

Figure 2:
The rate of propagation in the
(\beta,\alpha)
plane for sub-exponential kernels
J
, that is,
J(z)\asymp e^{-|z|^{\beta}}
for
z\geq 1
. In the green zone
➊
, including the critical curve, the solution to (
1.1
) propagates at a finite speed, as shown in Theorem
1.3
. In the red zone
➋
, acceleration occurs:
X_{\lambda}(t)\asymp t^{\frac{1}{\beta(\alpha+1)}}
, see Theorem
1.6
.

Let us now consider kernels with algebraic tails.

Theorem 1.7

(Exponential propagation)
.

Assume
s>0
. Suppose that
J
satisfies Hypothesis
1.2
with an algebraic tail, that is,

\mathcal{Z}(z)=\frac{1}{z^{1+2s}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }z\geq 1,

(1.7)

and that the non-linearity
f
and the initial data
u_{0}
satisfy Hypothesis
1.1
and Hypothesis
1.5
, respectively.
Then, for any
\lambda\in(0,1)
, we have

\ln X_{\lambda}(t)\sim\frac{1}{2s}[r(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{as }t\to\infty.

Interestingly, when
\alpha=0
and
0<s<1
, one recovers the rate of the Fisher-KPP equation with fractional diffusion
[
12
]
, and Theorem
1.7
also fills a (small) gap for
\alpha=0
and
s\geq 1
.

Let us comment on our strategies to prove Theorem
1.3
, Theorem
1.4
, Theorem
1.6
and Theorem
1.7
.

[
16
]
.
In the proof of Theorem
1.3
, that is, existence of travelling wave solutions, we rely on methods designed in
[
2
,
6
,
15
]
.
We identify a clever form for a super-solution, a sub-exponential with a polynomial prefactor, the latter appearing to be of crucial importance in the proof of the critical case
\beta=\frac{1}{\alpha+1}
.
Theorem
1.4
can be regarded as a direct corollary of Theorem
1.6
; therefore, we will not provide a special proof in this paper.
The proofs of Theorem
1.6
and Theorem
1.7
are
mainly based on the sub- and super-solutions technique.
We build on and extend a paper by the two first authors and Legendre
[
7
]
.
For the algebraic propagation, that is, Theorem
1.6
, we construct a super-solution by using the solution to the ordinary differential equation
u_{t}=\rho\frac{u}{(1-\ln u)^{\alpha}}
with a free parameter
\rho
. To obtain a precise estimate of the invasion rate, it is necessary to incorporate an algebraic correction into the (initial) profile, as the weakly degenerate nonlinearity introduces a logarithmic correction that significantly impacts the asymptotic behaviour.
In the exponential propagation, much more precise sub- and super- solutions have to be developed to get sharp estimates on the propagation speed.
On the one hand, we incorporate a logarithmic correction into the profiles.
On the other hand, since the accelerating solution to the Cauchy problem of (
1.1
) flattens with time
[
7
,
24
]
, it is essential to take the flattening effect into consideration to get exact rates of invasion.
For the construction of sub-solutions in the exponential propagation regime (Theorem
1.7
), the key to get a precise sub-solution is the solution to the full ordinary differential equation
u_{t}=f(u)
, which causes a lot of technical difficulties since its solution is not explicit. We point out that some steps of both algebraic and exponential proofs are similar but with so significantly different computations that redoing some of them entirely is justified. It is worth mentioning that we prove a flattening estimate of independent interest, which plays a crucial role in proving the existence of sub-solutions at later times.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we shall prove the existence of travelling waves, that is, Theorem
1.3
. In Section
3
, we prove Theorem
1.6
. In Section
4
, we prove Theorem
1.7
. In Section
5
, we provide the discretisation of the singular integral operator
\mathcal{D}[\cdot]
that lead to the numerical simulations.

2
Existence of travelling waves: proof of Theorem
1.3

In this section, we construct travelling wave solutions to (
1.1
) in the regime
\beta\geq\frac{1}{\alpha+1}
, by using the strategy developed in
[
15
]
for integrable kernel
J
and in
[
6
]
for non-integrable kernel
J
. We introduce the following perturbation of the travelling wave equation,

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\varepsilon\phi^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{D}[\phi]+c\phi^{\prime}+f(\phi)=0&\text{in }\mathbb{R},\\
&\phi(-\infty)=1,\hskip 9.24994pt\phi(\infty)=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.1)

The key step in these two approaches is to build a non trivial super-solution independent of
\varepsilon
of the above problem (
2.1
). To construct our super-solution we proceed as follows.

Let
w
be a decreasing smooth function, at least
C^{2}
, defined on
\mathbb{R}
by

w(x):=\begin{cases}1-e^{x},&x\leq-1,\\
C_{L}x^{p}e^{-x^{\beta}},&x\geq L,\end{cases}

Figure 3:
Plot of the function
w
.

where
C_{L}=\frac{1}{2}\left(1-e^{-1}\right)L^{-p}e^{L^{\beta}}
and
L>L_{0}:=\max\left\{1,\left(\frac{p}{\beta}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\right\}
, so that
w
is decreasing on
[L,+\infty)
, and
p>0
has to be determined, see Figure
3
.

Some direct calculations show that for
x\geq L
,

w^{\prime}(x)=C_{L}\left(px^{-\beta}-\beta\right)x^{p+\beta-1}e^{-x^{\beta}},

and

w^{\prime\prime}(x)=C_{L}\left(p(p-1)x^{-2\beta}-\beta(2p+\beta-1)x^{-\beta}+\beta^{2}\right)x^{p+2\beta-2}e^{-x^{\beta}}.

Thus, there exists
L_{1}>L_{0}
such that for any
x\geq L
and
L\geq L_{1}
, we have

w^{\prime}(x)\asymp-x^{p+\beta-1}e^{-x^{\beta}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}\hskip 9.24994pt0\leq w^{\prime\prime}(x)\lesssim x^{p+2\beta-2}e^{-x^{\beta}}.

(2.2)

We shall now prove that
w
is a super-solution to the travelling wave equation (
2.1
) when selecting an appropriate parameter
p
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\alpha>0
and
\beta>0
be such that
\frac{1}{\alpha+1}\leq\beta<1.
Assume that Hypothesis
1.2
holds, where the function
\mathcal{Z}
is sub-exponentially bounded, that is,

\mathcal{Z}(z)\leq e^{-z^{\beta}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ for all }z\geq 1,

and that the non-linearity
f
satisfies Hypothesis
1.1
. If
p\geq 2-2\beta
, then, for any
0\leq\varepsilon\leq 1
, there is
c_{0}>0
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\varepsilon w^{\prime\prime}(x)+\mathcal{D}[w](x)+c_{0}w^{\prime}(x)+f(w(x))\leq 0.

(2.3)

Before proving the lemma, let us explain how to use
w
. Equipped with the super-solution
w
to (
2.1
), similarly to the proof of Theorem 1.3 in
[
15
]
, one may obtain existence of travelling waves, that is, there is a minimum speed
c^{*}\leq c_{0}
such that, for all
c\geq c_{*}
, equation (
1.1
) admits travelling waves
(c,u)
, whereas, for all
c<c^{*}
, equation (
1.1
) does not admit any travelling wave solutions.

In view of
[
2
, Lemma 3.2]
, one may get that the minimum speed
c^{*}
is positive. More precisely, we have the following lemma, which proof is standard: we omit it here and send the reader to
[
2
]
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Assume Hypothesis
1.1
and Hypothesis
1.5
hold. Let
(c,u)
be a travelling wave. Then the speed
c
is positive. Moreover, so is the minimum speed
c^{*}
.

Let us complete the proof of Theorem
1.3
by proving Lemma
2.1
.

Proof of Lemma
2.1
.

We prove the lemma by dividing it into three zones of positions
x
:
x\leq-2
,
x\geq 2L
and
-2<x<2L
.

Zone 1:

x\leq-2
. Now, let us briefly estimate
\mathcal{D}[w]
for any
x\leq-2
. Since
w
is decreasing, by the definition of
\mathcal{D}[w]
, we have

\mathcal{D}[w]\leq P.V.\int_{-1}^{1}J(y)\Big(w(x-y)-w(x)\Big)dy+\int_{1}^{+\infty}J(y)\Big(w(x-y)-w(x)\Big)dy.

(2.4)

Repeating the same steps as for the derivation of (
2.6
), we have

P.V.\int_{-1}^{1}J(y)\Big(w(x-y)-w(x)\Big)dy\leq-\mathcal{C}_{7}e^{x},

for some positive constant
\mathcal{C}_{7}
. For the second integral of (
2.4
), since
w\leq 1
and
y\mapsto J(y)\in L^{1}([1,+\infty))
, we obtain

\int_{1}^{+\infty}J(y)\Big(w(x-y)-w(x)\Big)dy=\int_{1}^{+\infty}J(y)\Big(w(x-y)-1\Big)dy+e^{x}\int_{1}^{+\infty}J(y)dy\leq e^{x}\int_{1}^{+\infty}J(y)dy.

As a result, for
x\leq-2
, we have

\mathcal{D}[w]\leq-\mathcal{C}_{7}e^{x}+e^{x}\int_{1}^{+\infty}J(y)dy.

Therefore, for
x\leq-2
, since
f(w)\leq\frac{rw}{(1-\ln w)^{\alpha}}(1-w)\leq rw(1-w)
for
w\in(0,1)
, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon w^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{D}[w]+c_{0}w^{\prime}+f(w)

\displaystyle\leq-\varepsilon e^{x}-\mathcal{C}_{7}e^{x}+e^{x}\int_{1}^{+\infty}J(y)dy-c_{0}e^{x}+r(1-e^{x})e^{x}

\displaystyle\leq-\left(c_{0}-\int_{1}^{+\infty}J(y)dy-r\right)e^{x}\leq 0,

as long as
c_{0}>c_{left}:=\int_{1}^{+\infty}J(y)dy+r
.

Zone 2:

-2<x<2L
. By the definition of
\mathcal{D}[w]
, since
w
is decreasing and
0<w<1
, we write

\mathcal{D}[w]\leq P.V.\int_{-1}^{1}\Big(w(x-y)-w(x)\Big)J(y)dy+\int_{1}^{+\infty}J(y)dy.

Repeating the same steps as for the derivation of (
2.6
), since
w\in C^{2}
and
y\mapsto J(y)\in L^{1}([1,+\infty))
, we have, for all
x\in(-2,2L)
,

\mathcal{D}[w](x)\leq\mathcal{C}_{8},

for some positive constant
\mathcal{C}_{8}
.
It follows from
\varepsilon\leq 1
and
f(w)\leq r
that, for any
x\in(-2,2L)
, we have

\varepsilon w^{\prime\prime}(x)+\mathcal{D}[w](x)+c_{0}w^{\prime}(x)+f(w(x))\leq\varepsilon w^{\prime\prime}(x)+\mathcal{C}_{8}+c_{0}w^{\prime}(x)+r\leq 0,

as soon as
c_{0}>c_{middle}:=\frac{\left(\max_{x\in[-2,2L]}w^{\prime\prime}(x)\right)^{+}+\mathcal{C}_{8}+r}{\min_{x\in[-2,2L]}|w^{\prime}(x)|}
.

Zone 3:
x\geq 2L
. Let us first check (
2.3
) when
x\geq 2L
. By the definition of
\mathcal{D}[w]
, we write

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[w]=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{-1}J(y)\Big(w(x-y)-w(x)\Big)dy+P.V.\int_{-1}^{1}J(y)\Big(w(x-y)-w(x)\Big)dy

\displaystyle+\int_{1}^{\frac{x}{2}}J(y)\Big(w(x-y)-w(x)\Big)dy+\int_{\frac{x}{2}}^{x-L}J(y)\Big(w(x-y)-w(x)\Big)dy

\displaystyle+\int_{x-L}^{+\infty}J(y)\Big(w(x-y)-w(x)\Big)dy:=I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}+I_{4}+I_{5}.

#
For
I_{1}
,
it follows from the fact that
w
is decreasing for
x\geq L
that

I_{1}\leq 0.

(2.5)

#
Let us now estimate
I_{2}
.
Since
J
is symmetric, we write

I_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}J(y)\Big(w(x+y)+w(x-y)-2w(x)\Big)dy.

It follows from Taylor’s theorem and (
2.2
) that for all
y\in[-1,1]
,

w(x+y)+w(x-y)-2w(x)\leq y^{2}\sup_{|z|\leq 1}w^{\prime\prime}(x+z)\lesssim y^{2}\sup_{|z|\leq 1}\left[(x+z)^{p+2(\beta-1)}e^{-(x+z)^{\beta}}\right].

Thus, since
0<\beta<1
, by Hypothesis
1.2
, we get

I_{2}\leq\mathcal{C}_{1}x^{p+2\beta-2}e^{-x^{\beta}},

(2.6)

for some positive constant
\mathcal{C}_{1}
.

#
Let us estimate
I_{3}
.
By the Taylor theorem, we get

I_{3}=-\int_{1}^{\frac{x}{2}}\int_{0}^{1}yJ(y)w^{\prime}(x-\tau y)d\tau dy.

Since
w
is convex for
x\geq 2L
and
L\geq L_{1}
, by (
2.2
) and Hypothesis
1.2
, we get

I_{3}\leq-\int_{1}^{\frac{x}{2}}yJ(y)w^{\prime}(x-y)dy\lesssim\int_{1}^{\frac{x}{2}}y(x-y)^{p+\beta-1}e^{-y^{\beta}-(x-y)^{\beta}}dy.

By using the change of variables
z=\frac{y}{x}
and assuming
p\geq 1-\beta
, we obtain, for
all
x\geq 2L
,

\displaystyle I_{3}\lesssim x^{p+\beta+1}\int_{\frac{1}{x}}^{\frac{1}{2}}z(1-z)^{p+\beta-1}e^{-x^{\beta}\left[z^{\beta}+(1-z)^{\beta}\right]}dz\lesssim x^{p+\beta+1}e^{-x^{\beta}}\int_{\frac{1}{x}}^{\frac{1}{2}}ze^{-x^{\beta}\left[z^{\beta}+(1-z)^{\beta}-1\right]}dz.

(2.7)

There is
L_{2}>\max\{L_{0},L_{1}\}
such that for any
L\geq L_{2}
,

\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{3})}{x}\in\left(\frac{1}{x},\frac{1}{2}\right)\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }x\geq 2L.

Then, we split the above integral into two parts and obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{1}{x}}^{\frac{1}{2}}ze^{-x^{\beta}\left[z^{\beta}+(1-z)^{\beta}-1\right]}dz=

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{3})}{x}}^{\frac{1}{2}}ze^{-x^{\beta}\left[z^{\beta}+(1-z)^{\beta}-1\right]}dz+\int_{\frac{1}{x}}^{\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{3})}{x}}ze^{-x^{\beta}\left[z^{\beta}+(1-z)^{\beta}-1\right]}dz.

(2.8)

Notice that since
\beta\in(0,1)
and
z\mapsto z^{\beta}+(1-z)^{\beta}
is increasing on
\left[0,\frac{1}{2}\right]
, one has for all
z\in\left[\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{3})}{x},\frac{1}{2}\right]
,

z^{\beta}+(1-z)^{\beta}\geq\frac{\ln(x^{3})}{x^{\beta}}+\left(1-\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{3})}{x}\right)^{\beta}\geq\frac{\ln(x^{3})}{x^{\beta}}+1-\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{3})}{x}.

Thus, since
\beta\in(0,1)
, for the first integral in (
2.8
), we have

\int_{\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{3})}{x}}^{\frac{1}{2}}ze^{-x^{\beta}\left[z^{\beta}+(1-z)^{\beta}-1\right]}dz\leq e^{-\ln(x^{3})+\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{3})}{x^{1-\beta}}}=o_{x\to+\infty}\left(\frac{1}{x^{2}}\right).

For the second integral in (
2.8
), we have

\int_{\frac{1}{x}}^{\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{3})}{x}}ze^{-x^{\beta}\left[z^{\beta}+(1-z)^{\beta}-1\right]}dz\leq\exp\left\{x^{\beta}\left[1-\left(1-\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{3})}{x}\right)^{\beta}\right]\right\}\int_{\frac{1}{x}}^{\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{3})}{x}}ze^{-x^{\beta}z^{\beta}}dz.

Since
0<\beta<1
, by using the inequality
1-(1-y)^{\beta}\leq y
for all
y\in(0,1)
, we have for all
x\geq 2L
and
L\geq L_{2}
, up to enlarging
L_{2}
if necessary,

\exp\left\{x^{\beta}\left[1-\left(1-\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{3})}{x}\right)^{\beta}\right]\right\}=\exp\left\{\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{3})}{x^{1-\beta}}\right\}\leq 2.

On the other hand, since
y\mapsto ye^{-y^{\beta}}\in L^{1}([1,+\infty))
, by using the change of variables
y=zx
, we obtain

\int_{\frac{1}{x}}^{\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{3})}{x}}ze^{-x^{\beta}z^{\beta}}dz=\frac{1}{x^{2}}\int_{1}^{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{3})}ye^{-y^{\beta}}dy\leq\frac{1}{x^{2}}\int_{1}^{+\infty}ye^{-y^{\beta}}dy.

We then arrive at

\int_{\frac{1}{x}}^{\frac{1}{2}}ze^{-x^{\beta}\left[z^{\beta}+(1-z)^{\beta}-1\right]}dz\leq\frac{2}{x^{2}}\int_{1}^{+\infty}ye^{-y^{\beta}}dy+o_{x\to+\infty}\left(\frac{1}{x^{2}}\right).

As a result, for all
x\geq 2L
and
L>L_{2}
, up to enlarging
L_{2}
if necessary, we achieve, recalling (
2.7
),

I_{3}\leq\mathcal{C}_{3}x^{p+\beta-1}e^{-x^{\beta}},

(2.9)

for some positive constant
\mathcal{C}_{3}
.

#
Now, let us estimate
I_{4}
.
Since
w\geq 0
, one may directly get

I_{4}\leq\int_{\frac{x}{2}}^{x-L}J(y)w(x-y)dy.

By using the change of variables
z=1-\frac{y}{x}
, the definition of
w
and Hypothesis
1.2
, we have

I_{4}\leq\mathcal{J}_{0}x^{p+1}e^{-x^{\beta}}\int^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\frac{1}{x}}z^{p}e^{-x^{\beta}\left[z^{\beta}+(1-z)^{\beta}-1\right]}dz.

There is
L_{3}\geq\max\{L_{0},L_{1}\}
such that for
L\geq L_{3}
,

\frac{1}{x}\leq\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{p+2})}{x}\leq\frac{1}{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }x\geq 2L.

As for the estimate of
I_{4}
, the above integral can be split into two parts:

\displaystyle\int^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\frac{1}{x}}z^{p}e^{-x^{\beta}\left[z^{\beta}+(1-z)^{\beta}-1\right]}dz

\displaystyle=\int^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{p+2})}{x}}z^{p}e^{-x^{\beta}\left[z^{\beta}+(1-z)^{\beta}-1\right]}dz+\int^{\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{p+2})}{x}}_{\frac{1}{x}}z^{p}e^{-x^{\beta}\left[z^{\beta}+(1-z)^{\beta}-1\right]}dz.

By repeating the steps of the estimate of
I_{4}
, we get

\int^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{p+2})}{x}}z^{p}e^{-x^{\beta}\left[z^{\beta}+(1-z)^{\beta}-1\right]}dz=O_{x\to+\infty}\left(\frac{1}{x^{p+2}}\right),

and

\int^{\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{p+2})}{x}}_{\frac{1}{x}}z^{p}e^{-x^{\beta}\left[z^{\beta}+(1-z)^{\beta}-1\right]}dz\leq\frac{1}{x^{p+1}}\int_{1}^{+\infty}y^{p}e^{-y^{\beta}}dy.

As a result, for all
L\geq L_{3}
up to enlarging
L_{3}
if necessary, we have

I_{4}\leq\mathcal{J}_{0}\left[\int_{1}^{+\infty}y^{p}e^{-y^{\beta}}dy+O_{x\to+\infty}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)\right]e^{-x^{\beta}}\leq\mathcal{C}_{4}e^{-x^{\beta}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for any }x\geq 2L,

(2.10)

for some positive constant
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.

#
Let us now estimate
I_{5}
.
Since
0<w\leq 1
, by Hypothesis
1.2
, we have

I_{5}=\int_{x-L}^{+\infty}J(y)\Big(w(x-y)-w(x)\Big)dy\leq\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{x-L}^{+\infty}e^{-y^{\beta}}dy.

It follows from L’Hôpital’s rule that

\lim_{x\to+\infty}\frac{\int_{x-L}^{+\infty}e^{-y^{\beta}}dy}{\frac{1}{\beta}x^{1-\beta}e^{-x^{\beta}}}=1,

whence there exists some constant
\mathcal{C}_{2}>0
such that for all
x>2L
, we have

I_{5}\leq\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{x-L}^{+\infty}e^{-y^{\beta}}dy\leq\mathcal{C}_{2}x^{1-\beta}e^{-x^{\beta}}.

(2.11)

Collecting (
2.5
), (
2.6
), (
2.9
), (
2.10
) and (
2.11
), for all
x\geq 2L
and
L\geq\tilde{L}:=\max\{L_{2},L_{3}\}
, we have

\mathcal{D}[w](x)\leq x^{p+\beta-1}e^{-x^{\beta}}\left(\mathcal{C}_{1}x^{\beta-1}+\mathcal{C}_{2}x^{2(1-\beta)-p}+\mathcal{C}_{3}+\mathcal{C}_{4}x^{1-\beta-p}\right).

In view of Hypothesis
1.1
, by the definition of
w
, there is a positive constant
\mathcal{C}_{5}
such that we have for all
x\geq 2L
,

f(w)\leq\frac{rw}{(1-\ln w)^{\alpha}}=\frac{rC_{L}x^{p}e^{-x^{\beta}}}{(1-\ln C_{L}+x^{\beta}-p\ln x)^{\alpha}}\leq\mathcal{C}_{5}x^{p-\alpha\beta}e^{-x^{\beta}}.

By (
2.2
), there are two positive constants
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
such that

w^{\prime}(x)\leq-\omega_{1}x^{p+\beta-1}e^{-x^{\beta}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}\hskip 9.24994ptw^{\prime\prime}(x)\leq\omega_{2}x^{p+2\beta-2}e^{-x^{\beta}}.

Therefore, for any
0\leq\varepsilon\leq 1
,
x\geq 2L
and
L\geq\tilde{L}
, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon w^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{D}[w]+c_{0}w^{\prime}+f(w)

\displaystyle\leq x^{p+\beta-1}e^{-x^{\beta}}\Big((\varepsilon\omega_{2}+\mathcal{C}_{1})x^{\beta-1}+\mathcal{C}_{2}x^{2-2\beta-p}+\mathcal{C}_{3}+\mathcal{C}_{4}x^{1-\beta-p}-c_{0}\omega_{1}+\mathcal{C}_{5}x^{1-\beta-\alpha\beta}\Big).

Since
\frac{1}{\alpha+1}\leq\beta<1
, by selecting
p\geq 2-2\beta
,

\varepsilon w^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{D}[w]+c_{0}w^{\prime}+f(w)\leq(-c_{0}\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\mathcal{C}_{1}+\mathcal{C}_{2}+\mathcal{C}_{3}+\mathcal{C}_{4}+\mathcal{C}_{5})x^{p+\beta-1}e^{-x^{\beta}},

and then, by choosing
c_{0}>c_{right}:=\frac{1}{\omega_{1}}(\omega_{2}+\mathcal{C}_{1}+\mathcal{C}_{2}+\mathcal{C}_{3}+\mathcal{C}_{4}+\mathcal{C}_{6}),

one achieves, for any
0\leq\varepsilon\leq 1
and
L\geq\tilde{L}:=\max\{L_{2},L_{3}\}
,

\varepsilon w^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{D}[w]+c_{0}w^{\prime}+f(w)\leq 0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }x\geq 2L.

Let us finish the proof of the lemma. By choosing a speed

c_{0}>\max\left\{c_{left},c_{middle},c_{right}\right\}
,
it follows from the above computations that, for any
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
, we have

\forall x\in\mathbb{R},\hskip 9.24994pt\varepsilon w^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{D}[w]+c_{0}w^{\prime}+f(w)\leq 0.

This completes the proof.

\Box

3
Algebraic propagation: proof of Theorem
1.6

In this section, we present the sub- and super- solution process that leads to the proof of Theorem
1.6
, the latter being obtained at the end of the section.

3.1
The upper bound

Here, we prove the upper bound in Theorem
1.6
. To do so, we shall construct an adequate super-solution.

3.1.1
Definition of the super-solution and preliminary computations

Let us define

v_{0}(x):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &1,&x<L,\\
&C_{L}x^{p}e^{-x^{\beta}},&x\geq L,\end{aligned}\right.

(3.1)

where
p>0
has to be determined,
C_{L}:=L^{-p}e^{L^{\beta}}
and
L\geq\max\{(\frac{p}{\beta})^{\frac{1}{\beta}},1,a\}
, so that
v_{0}
is continuous and decreasing,
v_{0}\leq 1
and
C_{L}
is greater than
e
. Here,
a
comes from Hypothesis
1.5
, and thus, one may notice that

v_{0}\geq u_{0}.

(3.2)

Solving the Cauchy problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{\partial w}{\partial t}(t,x)=\rho\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}},&t>0,x\in\mathbb{R},\\
&w(0,\cdot)=v_{0}\geq 0,\end{aligned}\right.

leads to defining

w(t,x):=\exp{\left\{1-\left[(1-\ln v_{0}(x))^{\alpha+1}-\rho(\alpha+1)t\right]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}\right\}},

where
\rho\geq r
has to be chosen later. Observe though that
w
is not defined for all times: for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
w(t,x)
is defined only for
t\in\left[0,\frac{(1-\ln v_{0}(x))^{\alpha+1}}{\rho(\alpha+1)}\right)
. For later use, we state below several properties related to
v_{0}
and
w
, the proofs of which can be found in Appendix
A.1
.

Lemma 3.1
.

(i).
For any
y\in(0,1)
, denoting by
v_{0}^{-1}
the inverse of
v_{0}
on
[L,+\infty)
,

\left[\ln\frac{C_{L}}{y}+\frac{p}{\beta}\ln\ln\frac{e^{L^{\beta}}}{y}\right]^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\leq v_{0}^{-1}(y)\leq\left[\ln\frac{C_{L}}{y}+\left(\frac{p}{\beta}+\gamma\right)\ln\ln\frac{e^{L^{\beta}}}{y}\right],

(3.3)

for some positive constant
\gamma>0
, and

(v_{0}^{\prime}\circ v_{0}^{-1})(y)\gtrsim-\lvert\ln y\rvert^{\frac{\beta-1}{\beta}}y.

(3.4)

(ii).
For any
t\geq t^{\star}:=\frac{1}{r(\alpha+1)}
, define

x_{\Lambda}(t):=v_{0}^{-1}\left\{\exp\left\{1-\left[\rho(\alpha+1)t+(1-\ln\Lambda)^{\alpha+1}\right]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}\right\}\right\}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }\Lambda\in(0,2].

Then, we have
w(t,x_{\Lambda}(t))=\Lambda
,

x_{\Lambda}(t)\asymp_{\Lambda}t^{\frac{1}{\beta(\alpha+1)}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }t\in[t^{\star},\infty)\text{ and }\Lambda\in(0,2],

(3.5)

and for any
0<\Lambda_{1}<\Lambda_{2}\leq 2
,

x_{\Lambda_{1}}(t)-x_{\Lambda_{2}}(t)\gtrsim_{\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}}t^{\frac{1}{\beta(\alpha+1)}-1}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }t\in[t^{\star},\infty).

(3.6)

For all
t\in[t^{\star},\infty)
, let us now define the following function
m
as our prototype of a super-solution to (
1.1
):

m(t,x):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &1,&x\leq x_{1}(t),\\
&w(t,x),&x>x_{1}(t),.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.7)

We will see that for the right choice of
p
and
\rho
, the function
m
is indeed a super-solution to (
1.1
). In order to prove that, we first collect in the next two lemmas some additional useful properties of
w
. We first have

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\Lambda\in(0,2]
. For all
(t,x)\in[t^{\star},\infty)\times[x_{\Lambda}(t),+\infty)
, the function
w
is well-defined and non-increasing in
x
, and for any
t\in[t^{\star},\infty)
, the function
w(t,\cdot)
is convex and log-convex on
[x_{\Lambda}(t),+\infty)
. Moreover, setting
\varphi_{0}=-\ln v_{0}
, for all
(t,x)\in[t^{\star},\infty)\times[x_{\Lambda}(t),+\infty)
, we have

w(t,x)\geq v_{0}(x),

(3.8)

\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}=-\frac{w}{(1-\ln w)^{\alpha}}\varphi_{0}^{\prime}(1+\varphi_{0})^{\alpha},

(3.9)

and

0\leq\frac{\partial^{2}w}{\partial x^{2}}(t,x)\leq\mathcal{C}_{0}\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}},

(3.10)

for some positive constant
\mathcal{C}_{0}
.

Last, in order to estimate
\mathcal{D}[w]
later, we establish the following.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\alpha>0
and
0<\beta<1
. For all

z\in\left(\frac{1}{x},1-\frac{x_{1}(t)}{x}\right)
and
(t,x)\in[t^{\star},\infty)\times[x_{1}(t)+1,+\infty)
, one has

\frac{w(t,x(1-z))}{w(t,x)}\leq\exp\left\{\beta x^{\alpha\beta+\beta}z+C_{\alpha,\beta}x^{2\alpha\beta+2\beta}z^{2}\right\},

and

\frac{1-\ln w(t,x(1-z))}{1-\ln w(t,x)}\geq 1-\beta x^{\alpha\beta+\beta}z-C_{\alpha,\beta}x^{2\alpha\beta+2\beta}z^{2},

where
C_{\alpha,\beta}:=1-\beta+\alpha(\alpha+1)>0
.

With the above lemmas at hand, we are now ready to show that
m
is a super-solution to (
1.1
) when the constants
\rho>0
and
p>0
are chosen adequately. Unless otherwise specified, throughout the next subsections, we assume that
t\in[t^{\star},\infty)
.
We start by proving an estimate for
\mathcal{D}[m]
.

3.1.2
Estimation of
\mathcal{D}[m](t,\cdot)
on
(x_{1}(t),+\infty)

Lemma 3.4
.

There exists a time
t_{0}\geq t^{\star}
such that, for all
t\geq t_{0}
and
x>x_{1}(t)
, we have

\mathcal{D}[m](t,x)\leq\mathcal{C}_{1},

where
\mathcal{C}_{1}:=\max\left\{\frac{2\mathcal{J}_{1}\mathcal{C}_{0}}{(1-\ln 2)^{\alpha}},2\int_{|y|\geq 1}J(y)dy\right\}>0
.

Proof of Lemma
3.4
.

Let us denote
\tilde{J}(y)=J(y)\mathds{1}_{|y|\geq 1}(y)
. Since
J
is symmetric, we write

\mathcal{D}[m](t,x)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}J(y)\Big(m(t,x+y)+m(t,x-y)-2m(t,x)\Big)dy+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\tilde{J}(y)\Big(m(t,x-y)-m(t,x)\Big)dy:=I+II.

#
Let us estimate the integral
I
. In view of (
3.6
), there is a time
t_{0}\geq t^{\star}
such that
x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t)\geq 1
. By definition of
m
for all
t>t^{\star},x\geq x_{2}(t)
we have
m(t,x)\leq w(t,x)
.
Therefore, for all
t>0
and
x>x_{1}(t)
, we have

\displaystyle I\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}J(y)\Big(w(t,x+y)+w(t,x-y)-2w(t,x)\Big)dy.

Recall that for
t\geq t_{0}
we have
x_{1}(t)>x_{2}(t)+1
. From this observation and since
w(t,\cdot)\in C^{2}
on
[x_{2}(t),+\infty)
, by Taylor’s theorem, we get for all
(t,x)\in[t_{0},\infty)\times(x_{1}(t),+\infty)
,

I\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\partial^{2}w}{\partial x^{2}}(t,x+\tau\theta y)\theta y^{2}d\tau d\theta dy\leq\frac{1}{2}\sup_{|z|\leq 1}\frac{\partial^{2}w}{\partial x^{2}}(t,x+z)\int_{-1}^{1}y^{2}J(y)dy.

Thus, by Hypothesis
1.2
and (
3.10
), since
w(t,\cdot)
is non-increasing and less than
2
on
[x_{2}(t),+\infty)
, for all
(t,x)\in[t_{0},\infty)\times(x_{1}(t),+\infty)
, we have

I\leq\mathcal{J}_{1}\mathcal{C}_{0}\sup_{|z|\leq 1}\frac{w(t,x+z)}{(1-\ln w(t,x+z))^{\alpha}}\leq\mathcal{J}_{1}\mathcal{C}_{0}\frac{w(t,x-1)}{(1-\ln w(t,x-1))^{\alpha}}\leq\frac{2\mathcal{J}_{1}\mathcal{C}_{0}}{(1-\ln 2)^{\alpha}}.

#

Let us estimate the integral
II
. Since
0<m\leq 1
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
\tilde{J}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, we get

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\tilde{J}(y)\Big(m(t,x-y)-m(t,x)\Big)dy\leq 2\int_{\mathbb{R}}\tilde{J}(y)dy.

Therefore, combining the estimates of
I
and
II
, for all
(t,x)\in[t_{0},\infty)\times(x_{1}(t),+\infty)
, we achieve

\mathcal{D}[m](t,x)\leq\mathcal{C}_{1}.

where
\mathcal{C}_{1}=\max\left\{\frac{2\mathcal{J}_{1}\mathcal{C}_{0}}{(1-\ln 2)^{\alpha}},2\int_{|y|\geq 1}J(y)dy\right\}
.

\Box

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\tilde{p}:=\frac{2}{\beta(\alpha+1)-1}
and
\varrho\in(0,1)
.
Then there is a time
t^{\ddagger}\geq t^{\star}
such that for all
t\geq t^{\ddagger}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
, we have

\mathcal{D}[m](t,x)\leq\mathcal{C}_{2}\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\frac{p+\beta-1}{\beta}}}+\mathcal{C}_{3}\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}+\mathcal{C}_{4}\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\frac{p-\tilde{p}}{\beta}}}.

for some positive constants
\mathcal{C}_{2}
,
\mathcal{C}_{3}
and
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.

Proof of Lemma
3.5
.

By (
3.6
), we get

x_{\varrho}(t)-x_{1}(t)\to+\infty\hskip 9.24994pt\text{as }t\to+\infty.

(3.11)

Thus, there exists
t_{1}\geq t^{\star}
, such that for all
t\geq t_{1}
, we have

x_{\varrho}(t)-x_{1}(t)\geq\max\{L,2\},

where the constant
L
is defined in (
3.1
).
Since
0<m\leq 1
,
m(t,x)=w(t,x)
on
[t^{\star},+\infty)\times[x_{1}(t),+\infty)
and
m(t,\cdot)
is non-increasing on
\mathbb{R}
, for all
t\geq t_{1}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
, we write

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[m](t,x)

\displaystyle\leq\int^{+\infty}_{x-x_{1}(t)}J(y)dy+\int^{x-x_{1}(t)}_{1}J(y)\Big(w(t,x-y)-w(t,x)\Big)dy

\displaystyle+P.V.\int_{-1}^{1}J(y)\Big(w(t,x-y)-w(t,x)\Big)dy:=I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}.

\#

Let us estimate
I_{1}
.
By Hypothesis
1.2
and L’Hôpital’s rule, we have

I_{1}\leq\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{x-x_{1}(t)}^{+\infty}e^{-y^{\beta}}dy\lesssim(x-x_{1}(t))^{1-\beta}e^{-(x-x_{1}(t))^{\beta}}.

Let us denote
V_{0}(x):=(1-\ln v_{0}(x))^{\alpha+1}-1
on
(L,+\infty)
. By a direct calculation, for
x\in(L,+\infty)
, we get

V_{0}^{\prime}(x)=-(\alpha+1)(1-\ln v_{0}(x))^{\alpha}\frac{v_{0}^{\prime}}{v_{0}}(x),

and

V_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x)=(\alpha+1)(1-\ln v_{0}(x))^{\alpha-1}\left[\alpha\left(\frac{v_{0}^{\prime}}{v_{0}}\right)^{2}(x)-(1-\ln v_{0}(x))\left(\frac{v_{0}^{\prime}}{v_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(x)\right].

Since
\frac{v_{0}^{\prime}}{v_{0}}=-\frac{\beta}{x^{1-\beta}}+\frac{p}{x}
and
\left(\frac{v_{0}^{\prime}}{v_{0}}\right)^{\prime}=\frac{\beta(1-\beta)}{x^{2-\beta}}-\frac{p}{x^{2}}
for
x\in(L,+\infty)
, it follows that for
x\in(L,+\infty)
,

V_{0}^{\prime}(x)>0,

and, by
\alpha\beta+\beta-1<0
, there is
\tilde{L}\geq L
such that for all
x\geq\tilde{L}
, we have

\displaystyle\alpha\left(\frac{v_{0}^{\prime}}{v_{0}}\right)^{2}(x)-(1-\ln v_{0}(x))\left(\frac{v_{0}^{\prime}}{v_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta}{x^{2-2\beta}}\left[\alpha\beta\left(1-\frac{p}{\beta}\frac{1}{x^{\beta}}\right)^{2}-(1-\beta)\left(1+\frac{1-\ln C_{L}-p\ln x}{x^{\beta}}\right)\left(1-\frac{p}{\beta(1-\beta)}\frac{1}{x^{\beta}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta}{x^{2-2\beta}}\left(\alpha\beta+\beta-1+O_{x\to+\infty}\left(\frac{\ln x}{x^{\beta}}\right)\right)<0,

whence, for all
x\geq\tilde{L}
, we have

V_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x)\leq 0.

By (
3.11
), there is
\tilde{t}\geq t^{\star}
such that
x_{\varrho}(t)-x_{1}(t)\geq\tilde{L}
and
x_{1}(t)\geq\tilde{L}
for all
t\geq\tilde{t}
,
and thus, for all
t\geq\tilde{t}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
, we have

V_{0}(x-x_{1}(t))+V_{0}(x_{1}(t))\geq V_{0}(x).

By the definition of
x_{1}
, we have

V_{0}(x_{1}(t))=[1-\ln v_{0}(x_{1}(t))]^{\alpha+1}-1=\rho(\alpha+1)t.

Thus, it follows from
V_{0}
being strictly increasing on
(L,+\infty)
that, for all
t\geq\tilde{t}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
, we have

\displaystyle x-x_{1}(t)\geq V_{0}^{-1}\{V_{0}(x)-V_{0}(x_{1}(t))\}

\displaystyle=V_{0}^{-1}\{(1-\ln v_{0}(x))^{\alpha+1}-\rho(\alpha+1)t-1\}

(3.12)

\displaystyle=V_{0}^{-1}\{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha+1}-1\}

\displaystyle=v_{0}^{-1}\{w(t,x)\}

\displaystyle\geq\left(\ln\frac{C_{L}}{w(t,x)}+\frac{p}{\beta}\ln\ln\frac{e^{L^{\beta}}}{w(t,x)}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta}},

thanks to (
3.3
).
By (
3.11
), one has
x-x_{1}(t)\geq x_{\varrho}(t)-x_{1}(t)\geq\left(\frac{1}{\beta}-1\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta}}
for all
t\geq\tilde{t}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
, up to enlarge
\tilde{t}
if necessary. It follows from the fact that the function
y\mapsto y^{1-\beta}e^{-y^{\beta}}
is decreasing for
y\geq\left(\frac{1}{\beta}-1\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta}}
and
w(t,\cdot)<1
on
[x_{\varrho}(t),+\infty)
that for all
t\geq\max\{\tilde{t},t_{1}\}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
,

I_{1}\leq\mathcal{C}_{2}(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\frac{1-\beta-p}{\beta}}w(t,x),

(3.13)

for some constant
\mathcal{C}_{2}>0
.

\#

Let us estimate
I_{2}
.
By using the change of variables
z=\frac{y}{x}
,
I_{2}
can be written as

I_{2}=x\int^{\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{x}}_{\frac{1}{x}}J(xz)\Big(w(t,x(1-z))-w(t,x)\Big)dz.

We claim that there is a time
t_{2}>0
such that for all
t\geq t_{2}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
,

\frac{1}{x}\leq\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{q})}{x}\leq\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{x},

for
q>1
to be determined. Indeed, since
w(t,\cdot)
is non-increasing and convex on
[x_{1}(t),+\infty)
, we have

1=w(t,x_{1}(t))\geq w\left(t,x_{1}(t)+2\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x_{1}^{q}(t))\right)\geq 1+2\left[\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x_{1}^{q}(t))\right]\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}(t,x_{1}(t)).

By (
3.9
) and the facts that
\alpha\beta+\beta-1<0
and
\lim_{t\to\infty}x_{1}(t)=+\infty
, we notice that

\left[\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x_{1}^{q}(t))\right]\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}(t,x_{1}(t))=-\left[\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x_{1}^{q}(t))\right]x_{1}^{\alpha\beta+\beta-1}(t)\left[1+O_{t\to+\infty}\left(\frac{\ln x_{1}(t)}{x_{1}^{\beta}(t)}\right)\right]\to 0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{as }t\to\infty.

It follows that

w\left(t,x_{1}(t)+2\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x_{1}^{q}(t))\right)\to 1\hskip 9.24994pt\text{as }t\to\infty.

Thus, since
0<\varrho<1
, there is
t_{2}>t^{\star}
such that for all
t\geq t_{2}
,

w\left(t,x_{1}(t)+2\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x_{1}^{q}(t))\right)\geq\varrho=w(t,x_{\varrho}(t)),

which implies that for
t\geq t_{2}
,

x_{\varrho}(t)\geq x_{1}(t)+2\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x_{1}^{q}(t)),

since
w(t,\cdot)
is non-increasing on
[x_{1}(t),+\infty)
. Thus, for all
t\geq t_{2}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
, up to enlarging
t_{2}
if necessary, we have

\frac{1}{x}\leq\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{q})}{x}\leq\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{x}.

Therefore, since
w>0
, denoting
B=\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{q})}{x}
, for all
t\geq t_{2}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
, we write

\displaystyle I_{2}\leq x\int^{B}_{\frac{1}{x}}J(xz)\Big(w(t,x(1-z))-w(t,x)\Big)dz+x\int^{\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{x}}_{B}J(xz)w(t,x(1-z))dz:=I_{4}+I_{5}.

\#\#

Let us estimate
I_{4}
.

Before estimating
I_{4}
, we introduce a bounded function
H_{t,x}
. Recall
C_{\alpha\beta}=1-\beta+\alpha(\alpha+1)>0
in Lemma
3.3
. Since
\alpha\beta+\beta-1<0
, one may notice that there is
t_{3}>t_{2}
such that

1-\beta x^{\alpha\beta+\beta-1}\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{q})-C_{\alpha,\beta}x^{2\alpha\beta+2\beta-2}\ln^{\frac{2}{\beta}}(x^{q})>\frac{1}{2},

for all
t\geq t_{3}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
.
Let
H_{t,x}
be defined on
\left[0,\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{q})}{x}\right]
as

H_{t,x}(z):=\frac{\exp\left\{\beta x^{\alpha\beta+\beta}z+C_{\alpha,\beta}x^{2\alpha\beta+2\beta}z^{2}\right\}}{\left(1-\beta x^{\alpha\beta+\beta}z-C_{\alpha,\beta}x^{2\alpha\beta+2\beta}z^{2}\right)^{\alpha}},

where
t\geq t_{3}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
. Then, for all
t\geq t_{3}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
, since
H_{t,x}
is increasing on
\left[0,\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{q})}{x}\right]
, we get, for
z\in\left[0,\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{q})}{x}\right]
,

0<H_{t,x}(z)\leq\frac{\exp\left\{\beta\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{q})}{x^{1-\alpha\beta-\beta}}+C_{\alpha,\beta}\frac{\ln^{\frac{2}{\beta}}(x^{q})}{x^{2-2\alpha\beta-2\beta}}\right\}}{\left[1-\left(\beta\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{q})}{x^{1-\alpha\beta-\beta}}+C_{\alpha,\beta}\frac{\ln^{\frac{2}{\beta}}(x^{q})}{x^{2-2\alpha\beta-2\beta}}\right)\right]^{\alpha}}\to 1\hskip 9.24994pt\text{as }x\to+\infty,

and it follows that
H_{t,x}
is bounded uniformly on
\left[0,\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{q})}{x}\right]
for all
t\geq t_{3}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
, up to enlarging
t_{3}
if necessary.

In view of
I_{4}
, by Taylor’s theorem and the convexity of
w(t,\cdot)
on
[x_{1}(t),+\infty)
,
I_{4}
can be written as

\displaystyle I_{4}\leq-x^{2}\int^{B}_{\frac{1}{x}}\int_{0}^{1}zJ(xz)\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\Big(t,x(1-\tau z)\Big)d\tau dz\leq-x^{2}\int^{B}_{\frac{1}{x}}zJ(xz)\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\Big(t,x(1-z)\Big)dz.

By (
1.6
) and (
3.9
), we write

-zJ(xz)\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\Big(t,x(1-z)\Big)\leq\mathcal{J}_{0}ze^{-x^{\beta}z^{\beta}}\frac{w(t,x(1-z))}{[1-\ln w(t,x(1-z))]^{\alpha}}\varphi_{0}^{\prime}(x(1-z))[1+\varphi_{0}(x(1-z))]^{\alpha}.

Since
L\geq\left(\frac{e}{C_{L}}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}
and
\varphi_{0}(x)=-\ln v_{0}(x)=x^{\beta}-p\ln x-\ln C_{L}
for all
x>L
, one has
\varphi_{0}^{\prime}(x)=\beta x^{\beta-1}-px^{-1}\leq\beta x^{\beta-1}
and
[1+\varphi_{0}(x)]^{\alpha}\leq x^{\alpha\beta}
for all
x>L
, and it follows from the definition of
w
that

\displaystyle-zJ(xz)\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\Big(t,x(1-z)\Big)\leq\mathcal{J}_{0}\beta x^{\alpha\beta+\beta-1}\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}ze^{-x^{\beta}z^{\beta}}\frac{w(t,x(1-z))}{w(t,x)}\left(\frac{1-\ln w(t,x)}{1-\ln w(t,x(1-z))}\right)^{\alpha}.

By Lemma
3.3
, we have

\displaystyle-zJ(xz)\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\Big(t,x(1-z)\Big)\leq\mathcal{J}_{0}\beta\frac{x^{\alpha\beta+\beta-1}w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}ze^{-x^{\beta}z^{\beta}}H_{t,x}(z).

Thus, since
H_{t,x}
is bounded uniformly on
\left[0,B\right]
for all
t\geq t_{3}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
, we get for all
t\geq t_{3}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
,

\displaystyle I_{4}\leq\mathcal{J}_{0}\beta\frac{x^{\alpha\beta+\beta+1}w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}\int_{\frac{1}{x}}^{B}ze^{-x^{\beta}z^{\beta}}H_{t,x}(z)dz

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{x^{\alpha\beta+\beta+1}w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}\int_{\frac{1}{x}}^{B}ze^{-x^{\beta}z^{\beta}}dz.

Then, since
y\mapsto ye^{-y^{\beta}}\in L^{1}([1,+\infty))
, by using the change of variables
y=xz
, for all
t\geq t_{3}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
, we have

I_{4}\leq C_{4}x^{\alpha\beta+\beta-1}\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}},

(3.14)

for some positive constant
C_{4}
.

\#\#

Let us estimate
I_{5}
.
By (
1.6
) and the definition of
w
, we write

\displaystyle I_{5}=x\int_{B}^{\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{x}}J(xz)w(t,x(1-z))dz\leq\mathcal{J}_{0}x\int_{B}^{\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{x}}\exp\left\{-x^{\beta}z^{\beta}+\ln w(t,x(1-z))\right\}dz.

By
\beta\in(0,1)
and Lemma
3.2
, for all
x\geq x_{1}(t)
and
z\in\left(\frac{1}{x},\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{x}\right)
, one may get

\beta(1-\beta)x^{\beta}z^{\beta-2}+x^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x}[\ln w(t,x(1-z))]\geq 0,

it follows that, for fixed
(t,x)\in[t^{\star},\infty)\times[x_{1}(t),+\infty)
,
z\mapsto-x^{\beta}z^{\beta}+\ln w(t,x(1-z))
is convex on
\left(\frac{1}{x},\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{x}\right)
. Thus, for all
z\in\left[B,\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{x}\right]
, it follows that

-x^{\beta}z^{\beta}+\ln w(t,x(1-z))\leq\max\left\{-x^{\beta}B^{\beta}+\ln w(t,x(1-B)),-(x-x_{1}(t))^{\beta}\right\}.

By Lemma
3.3
and
B=\frac{\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{q})}{x}
, we obtain

-x^{\beta}B^{\beta}+\ln w(t,x(1-B))\leq-\ln(x^{q})+\ln w(t,x)+\beta x^{\alpha\beta+\beta-1}\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(x^{q})+C_{\alpha,\beta}x^{2\alpha\beta+2\beta-2}\ln^{\frac{2}{\beta}}(x^{q}),

and thus, since
\alpha\beta+\beta-1<0
and
q>1
, there is
t_{4}>t_{2}
such that for all
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
and
t\geq t_{4}
, we have

x\exp\left\{-x^{\beta}B^{\beta}+\ln w(t,x(1-B))\right\}\lesssim x^{1-q}w(t,x)\lesssim\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\frac{q-1}{\beta}}}.

(3.15)

On the other hand, for
t
large enough and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
, we have

x\leq(x-x_{1}(t))^{\tilde{p}},

where
\tilde{p}=\frac{2}{1-\beta-\alpha\beta}>2
.
Indeed, since
x\mapsto x-(x-x_{1}(t))^{\tilde{p}}
is decreasing for
t
large enough and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
, by (
3.5
) and (
3.6
), there is
t_{5}>t_{2}
such that for all
t\geq t_{5}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
,

x-(x-x_{1}(t))^{\tilde{p}}\leq x_{\varrho}(t)-(x_{\varrho}(t)-x_{1}(t))^{\tilde{p}}\leq C^{\prime}t^{\frac{1}{\beta(\alpha+1)}}-C^{\prime\prime}t^{\frac{2}{\beta(\alpha+1)}}\leq 0,

for some positive constants
C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime}
.
It follows from (
3.12
) that for all
t\geq t_{5}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
, up to enlarge
t_{5}
if necessary,

x\exp\left\{-x^{\beta}B^{\beta}+\ln w(t,x(1-B))\right\}\leq(x-x_{1}(t))^{\tilde{p}}e^{-(x-x_{1}(t))^{\beta}}\lesssim\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\frac{p-\tilde{p}}{\beta}}}.

(3.16)

As a result, collecting (
3.15
) and (
3.16
) and taking
q=\alpha\beta+1
, for all
t\geq\max\{t_{4},t_{5}\}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
, we achieve

I_{5}\leq C_{5}\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}+C_{6}\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\frac{p-\tilde{p}}{\beta}}},

(3.17)

for some positive constant
C_{5}
and
C_{6}
.

Therefore, collecting (
3.14
) and (
3.17
), since
\alpha\beta+\beta-1<0
, for all
t\geq\max\{t_{3},t_{4},t_{5}\}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
, we arrive at

I_{2}\leq(C_{4}+C_{5})\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}+C_{6}\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\frac{p-\tilde{p}}{\beta}}}.

(3.18)

\#

Let us estimate
I_{3}
.
Since
J
is symmetric and
w(t,\cdot)
is
C^{2}
on
[x_{1}(t),+\infty)
, we have

\displaystyle I_{3}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}J(y)\Big(w(t,x+y)+w(t,x-y)-2w(t,x)\Big)dy

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\partial^{2}w}{\partial x^{2}}(t,x+\tau\theta y)\theta y^{2}J(y)d\tau d\theta dy

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sup_{|z|\leq 1}\frac{\partial^{2}w}{\partial x^{2}}(t,x+z)\int_{-1}^{1}y^{2}J(y)dy,

and it follows from Hypothesis
1.2
, Lemma
3.3
and (
3.10
) that, for all
t\geq t^{\star}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
,

I_{3}\leq\mathcal{J}_{1}\mathcal{C}_{0}\sup_{|z|\leq 1}\frac{w(t,x+z)}{(1-\ln w(t,x+z))^{\alpha}}\leq\mathcal{J}_{1}\mathcal{C}_{0}\frac{w(t,x-1)}{(1-\ln w(t,x-1))^{\alpha}}\leq C_{3}\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}},

(3.19)

for some constant
C_{3}>0
,

Let us denote
t^{\ddagger}:=\max\{t^{\star},t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4},t_{5},\tilde{t}\}
. Therefore, owing to (
3.13
), (
3.18
) and (
3.19
), for all
t\geq t^{\ddagger}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
, we achieve

\mathcal{D}[m](t,x)\leq\mathcal{C}_{2}\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\frac{p+\beta-1}{\beta}}}+\mathcal{C}_{3}\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}+\mathcal{C}_{4}\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\frac{p-\tilde{p}}{\beta}}}.

where
\mathcal{C}_{3}:=C_{3}+C_{4}+C_{5}
and
\mathcal{C}_{4}:=C_{6}
.

\Box

3.1.3
Proof of the upper bound in Theorem
1.6

Proposition 3.6
.

Assume
p\geq\tilde{p}+\alpha\beta
. There is
\bar{\rho}\geq r
such that, for all
\rho\geq\bar{\rho}
, we have

\frac{\partial m}{\partial t}(t,x)-\mathcal{D}[m](t,x)-f(m(t,x))\geq 0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }t\geq\bar{t}:=\max\{t_{0},t^{\ddagger}\}\text{ and }x>x_{1}(t).

(3.20)

Proof of Proposition
3.6
.

By Hypothesis
1.1
and the definition of
m
, for
x>x_{1}(t)
, we have

\frac{\partial m}{\partial t}(t,x)=\frac{\rho w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}\hskip 9.24994ptf(m(t,x))\leq\frac{rw(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}.

(3.21)

Choosing
\rho>\rho_{0}:=r+2(1+\ln 2)^{\alpha}\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\varrho=\frac{1}{2}
, we have

\frac{\varrho}{(1-\ln\varrho)^{\alpha}}=\frac{1}{2(1+\ln 2)^{\alpha}}\geq\frac{\mathcal{C}_{1}}{\rho-r},

where
\mathcal{C}_{1}
is defined in Lemma
3.4
. By definition of
x_{\varrho}
, we obtain
w(t,x_{\varrho}(t))=\varrho
and
\varrho\leq w(t,x)\leq 1
for all
x_{1}(t)\leq x\leq x_{\varrho}(t)
. Thus, since the function
y\mapsto\frac{y}{(1-\ln y)^{\alpha}}
is increasing on
y\in(0,1)
, we have, for
x_{1}(t)\leq x\leq x_{\varrho}(t)
,

\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}\geq\frac{w(t,x_{\varrho}(t))}{(1-\ln w(t,x_{\varrho}(t)))^{\alpha}}=\frac{\varrho}{(1-\ln\varrho)^{\alpha}}\geq\frac{\mathcal{C}_{1}}{\rho-r}.

As a result, by (
3.21
) and Lemma
3.4
, for all
t\geq t_{0}
and
x_{1}(t)<x\leq x_{\varrho}(t)
, we have

\frac{\partial m}{\partial t}(t,x)-\mathcal{D}[m](t,x)-f(m(t,x))\geq(\rho-r)\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}-\mathcal{C}_{1}\geq(\rho-r)\frac{\mathcal{C}_{1}}{\rho-r}-\mathcal{C}_{1}=0.

(3.22)

On the other hand, in view of Lemma
3.5
, by (
3.21
), for all
t\geq t^{\ddagger}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial m}{\partial t}(t,x)-\mathcal{D}[m](t,x)-f(m(t,x))

\displaystyle\geq\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}\left(\rho-r-\mathcal{C}_{2}(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\frac{\alpha\beta-\beta+1-p}{\beta}}-\mathcal{C}_{3}-\mathcal{C}_{4}(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\frac{\tilde{p}+\alpha\beta-p}{\beta}}\right).

As a result, recalling that
\tilde{p}=\frac{2}{1-\beta-\alpha\beta}
, by assuming
p\geq\tilde{p}+\alpha\beta\geq\alpha\beta-\beta+1
and choosing
\rho\geq r+\mathcal{C}_{2}+\mathcal{C}_{3}+\mathcal{C}_{4}
, we have for all
t\geq t^{\ddagger}
and
x\geq x_{\varrho}(t)
,

\frac{\partial m}{\partial t}(t,x)-\mathcal{D}[m](t,x)-f(m(t,x))\geq 0.

(3.23)

Collecting (
3.22
) and (
3.23
), for all
\rho\geq\bar{\rho}:=\max\{\rho_{0},r+\mathcal{C}_{2}+\mathcal{C}_{3}+\mathcal{C}_{4}\}
,
m
is then a super-solution to (
1.1
) for all
t\geq\bar{t}=\max\{t_{0},t^{\ddagger}\}
and
x>x_{1}(t)
.

\Box

Let us denote

\tilde{m}(t,x)=m(t+\bar{t},x)\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}.

Thus, when
p=\tilde{p}+\alpha\beta
and
\rho=\bar{\rho}
, for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
x\in(x_{1}(t+\bar{t}),+\infty)
, by Proposition
3.6
, the function
\tilde{m}
is a super-solution to (
1.1
).
Now, we are ready to prove the upper bound in Theorem
1.6
.

In view of Hypothesis
1.5
, by (
3.2
), (
3.8
) and the definition of
m
, one has that
u_{0}\leq v_{0}\leq m(\bar{t},\cdot)=\tilde{m}(0,\cdot)
.
Thus, since
u(t,x)\leq 1=\tilde{m}(t,x)
for all
t>0
and
x\leq x_{1}(t+\bar{t})
, and
\tilde{m}
is a super-solution to (
1.1
) in
(x_{1}(t+\bar{t}),+\infty)
, the comparison principle yields that
u(t,x)<\tilde{m}(t,x)
for all
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}
. Thus, for any
\lambda\in(0,1)
, we have

u(t,x_{\lambda}(t+\bar{t}))<\tilde{m}(t,x_{\lambda}(t+\bar{t}))=\lambda,\hskip 9.24994pt\forall t>0.

Therefore, for
t>0
, we have

X_{\lambda}(t)\leq x_{\lambda}(t+\bar{t})\lesssim_{\lambda}t^{\frac{1}{\beta(\alpha+1)}},

which completes the proof of the upper bound of the level set.

3.2
The lower bound

Here, we prove the lower bound in Theorem
1.6
. This is achieved by deriving a smooth sub-solution to (
1.1
).

3.2.1
Definition of the sub-solution and basic computations

Let us define

v_{0}(x):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &1,&x<0,\\
&e^{-x^{\beta}},&x\geq 0.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.24)

As in the previous subsection, let us define

w(t,x):=\exp{\left\{1-\left[(1-\ln v_{0}(x))^{\alpha+1}-\rho(\alpha+1)t\right]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}\right\}},

which solves the Cauchy problem

\ \left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{\partial w}{\partial t}(t,x)=\rho\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}},&(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R},\\
&w(0,x)=v_{0}(x),&x\in\mathbb{R},\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\end{aligned}\right.

where
\rho>0
is to be determined.
Again,
w
is not defined for all times: for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
w(t,x)
is only defined in
t\in\left[0,\frac{(1-\ln v_{0}(x))^{\alpha+1}}{\rho(\alpha+1)}\right)
. As for the construction of the super-solution, we first collect some useful technical results. We summarize everything we need in the following Lemma, whose proof can be found in Appendix
A.2
.

Lemma 3.7
.

For all
t\geq 0
, define

x_{\Lambda}(t):=\left\{-1+\left[(1-\ln\Lambda)^{\alpha+1}+\rho(\alpha+1)t\right]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}\right\}^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for any }\Lambda\in(0,1).

(3.25)

(i).

For any
\Lambda\in\left(0,e^{-1}\right)
,

x_{\Lambda}(t)>1\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }t\geq 0.

(ii).

For any
0<\Lambda_{1}<\Lambda_{2}<1
, we have

x_{\Lambda_{1}}(t)-x_{\Lambda_{2}}(t)\to+\infty\hskip 9.24994pt\text{as }t\to\infty.

(iii).

For any
\Lambda\in(0,1)
and
t\geq 0
, the function
w(t,\cdot)
is well-defined, non-increasing and convex on
[x_{\Lambda}(t),+\infty)
. Moreover, denoting
\varphi_{0}=-\ln v_{0}
, we have

\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}=-\frac{w}{(1-\ln w)^{\alpha}}\varphi_{0}^{\prime}(1+\varphi_{0})^{\alpha},

(3.26)

and

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}(t,x_{\Lambda}(t))=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\text{uniformly on }\Lambda\in(0,1).

(3.27)

(iv).

For any
\Lambda\in(0,1)
, we have the following estimate for
w
:

w(t,x)\leq e^{-x^{\beta}+[\rho(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times[x_{\Lambda}(t),+\infty).

(3.28)

To construct a sub-solution to equation (
1.1
), one may want to cut the function
w
at the value
\varepsilon
. However, to handle a possible singularity of
J
at zero, one requires the sub-solution to (
1.1
) to be at least of class
C^{2}
in
x
.
As in
[
7
]
, we introduce the function

g_{\varepsilon}:y\mapsto 3\left(1-\frac{y}{\varepsilon}+\frac{y^{2}}{3\varepsilon^{2}}\right)y,\hskip 9.24994pt\text{on }[0,\varepsilon],

for some
\varepsilon>0
. Some direct calculations show that

g_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(y)=3\left(1-\frac{y}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}\hskip 9.24994ptg_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(y)=-\frac{6}{\varepsilon}\left(1-\frac{y}{\varepsilon}\right),\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for }y\in[0,\varepsilon].

Notice that for
y\in[0,\varepsilon]
, we have

y\leq g_{\varepsilon}(y)\leq 3y.

Equipped with the above preparations, for any fixed
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{0}:=\min\left\{e^{-1},\xi_{0}\right\}\right)
and
t\geq 0
, let us define

v(t,x):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\varepsilon,&x<x_{\varepsilon}(t),\\
&g_{\varepsilon}(w(t,x)),&x\geq x_{\varepsilon}(t).\end{aligned}\right.

Notice that the function
v
has a good regularity, that is,
v(\cdot,x)
is
C^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
v(t,\cdot)
is
C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
for all
t>0
. Unless otherwise specified, throughout the subsection, we assume that
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

Some direct calculations show that for all
x<x_{\varepsilon}(t)
, we have

\frac{\partial v}{\partial t}(t,x)=\frac{\partial v}{\partial x}(t,x)=\frac{\partial^{2}v}{\partial x^{2}}(t,x)=0,

(3.29)

while for all
x\geq x_{\varepsilon}(t)
,

\frac{\partial v}{\partial t}(t,x)=g_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(w)\frac{\partial w}{\partial t}=3\rho\left(1-\frac{w(t,x)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}\leq 3\rho\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}},

(3.30)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial v}{\partial x}(t,x)=g_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(w)\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}

\displaystyle=-3\left(1-\frac{w(t,x)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}(1-\varphi_{0}(x))^{\alpha}\varphi_{0}^{\prime}(x)

(3.31)

\displaystyle\geq-3\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}(1-\varphi_{0}(x))^{\alpha}\varphi_{0}^{\prime}(x),

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}v}{\partial x^{2}}=g_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(w)\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)^{2}+g^{\prime}(w)\frac{\partial^{2}w}{\partial x^{2}}

\displaystyle=3\left(1-\frac{w}{\varepsilon}\right)\left[-\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)^{2}+\left(1-\frac{w}{\varepsilon}\right)\frac{\partial^{2}w}{\partial x^{2}}\right]\geq-\frac{6}{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)^{2}.

(3.32)

Let us investigate the convexity of the function
v
.

Lemma 3.8
.

For all
t>0
,
v(t,\cdot)
is convex on
\left(x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t),+\infty\right)
.

Proof of Lemma
3.8
.

By (
3.26
), for all
t>0
and
x>x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t)
, we get

\frac{\partial^{2}w}{\partial x^{2}}=\frac{w}{(1-\ln w)^{\alpha}}\Big\{(\varphi_{0}^{\prime})^{2}(1+\varphi_{0})^{2\alpha}\Big((1-\ln w)^{-\alpha}+\alpha(1-\ln w)^{-(\alpha+1)}-\alpha(1+\varphi_{0})^{-(\alpha+1)}\Big)-\varphi_{0}^{\prime\prime}(1+\varphi_{0})^{\alpha}\Big\}.

It then follows from (
3.26
) and (
3.32
) that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}v}{\partial x^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq 3\left(1-\frac{w}{\varepsilon}\right)(\varphi^{\prime}_{0})^{2}(1+\varphi_{0})^{2\alpha}\frac{w}{(1-\ln w)^{2\alpha}}\left(1-\frac{3}{\varepsilon}w\right)\geq 0

as soon as
0<w\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{3}
. Therefore, for all
x>x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t)
, we have
\frac{\partial^{2}v}{\partial x^{2}}\geq 0
.

\Box

3.2.2
Estimation of
\mathcal{D}[v](t,\cdot)
on
\mathbb{R}

Now, let us estimate the dispersal term
\mathcal{D}[v]
.
We split space into three space sub-zones, as follows.

Proposition 3.9
.

Assume
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
. For any
t>0
, we have the following estimate for
\mathcal{D}[v](t,\cdot)
.

I.

When
x\in(-\infty,x_{\varepsilon}(t))
, for all
B_{1}>1
, we have

\mathcal{D}[v]\geq-\varepsilon\mathcal{C}_{1}B_{1}^{1-\beta}e^{-B_{1}^{\beta}}-\frac{\mathcal{C}_{2}}{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)^{2}(t,x_{\varepsilon}(t)),

(3.33)

where
\mathcal{C}_{1}>0
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}:=6\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{1}^{+\infty}z^{2}e^{-z^{\beta}}dz\right)
.

II.

When
x\in\left[x_{\varepsilon}(t),x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t)+1\right]
, for all
B_{2}>1
, we have

\mathcal{D}[v]\geq-\frac{\mathcal{C}_{2}}{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)^{2}(t,x_{\varepsilon}(t))-\mathcal{C}_{3}\varepsilon B_{2}^{1-\beta}e^{-B_{2}^{\beta}},

(3.34)

where
\mathcal{C}_{3}>0
.

III.

When
x\in\left(x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t)+1,+\infty\right)
, we have

\mathcal{D}[v]\geq\mathcal{C}_{4}\frac{\partial v}{\partial x}(t,x),

(3.35)

where
\mathcal{C}_{4}:=\int_{1}^{+\infty}zJ(z)dz
.

Proof of Proposition
3.9
.

#
Start with I:
x<x_{\varepsilon}(t)
.
Since
v(t,\cdot)=\varepsilon
on
(-\infty,x_{\varepsilon}(t))
, we write

\mathcal{D}[v]=\int_{x_{\varepsilon}(t)-x}^{+\infty}J(z)\Big(v(t,x+z)-\varepsilon\Big)dz.

In view of Hypothesis
1.2
, one may estimate the above integral on
x\in(-\infty,x_{\varepsilon}(t)-B_{1}]
and
x\in(x_{\varepsilon}(t)-B_{1},x_{\varepsilon}(t))
for some
B_{1}>1
, respectively.

##
Part 1:
x\in(-\infty,x_{\varepsilon}(t)-B_{1}]
.

By the hypothesis on
J
and L’Hôpital’s rule, we get

\mathcal{D}[v]\geq-\varepsilon\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{B_{1}}^{+\infty}e^{-z^{\beta}}dz\geq-\varepsilon\mathcal{C}_{1}B_{1}^{1-\beta}e^{-B_{1}^{\beta}},

(3.36)

for some
\mathcal{C}_{1}>0
.

##
Part 2:
x\in(x_{\varepsilon}(t)-B_{1},x_{\varepsilon}(t))
.

We write

\mathcal{D}[v]=\int_{x_{\varepsilon}(t)-x+B_{1}}^{+\infty}J(z)\Big(v(t,x+z)-\varepsilon\Big)dz+\int_{x_{\varepsilon}(t)-x}^{x_{\varepsilon}(t)-x+B_{1}}J(z)\Big(v(t,x+z)-\varepsilon\Big)dz:=I_{1}+I_{2}.

###
Let us estimate
I_{1}
.
Similarly to calculations in (
3.36
), one may get

I_{1}\geq-\varepsilon\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{x_{\varepsilon}(t)-x+B_{1}}^{+\infty}e^{-z^{\beta}}dz\geq-\varepsilon\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{B_{1}}^{+\infty}e^{-z^{\beta}}dz\geq-\varepsilon\mathcal{C}_{1}B_{1}^{1-\beta}e^{-B_{1}^{\beta}},

where
\mathcal{C}_{1}>0
.

###
Let us estimate
I_{2}
.
By the fundamental theorem of calculus, since
v(t,x)=\varepsilon
for
x<x_{\varepsilon}(t)
, we have

I_{2}=\int_{0}^{x_{\varepsilon}(t)-x+B_{1}}J(z)\Big(v(t,x+z)-v(t,x)\Big)dz=\int_{0}^{x_{\varepsilon}(t)-x+B_{1}}\int_{0}^{1}zJ(z)\frac{\partial v}{\partial x}(t,x+\tau z)dz.

Since
\frac{\partial v}{\partial x}(t,x)=0
for
x<x_{\varepsilon}(t)
, by (
3.32
) and the convexity of
w
, we obtain

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{x_{\varepsilon}(t)-x+B_{1}}\int_{0}^{1}zJ(z)\Big(\frac{\partial v}{\partial x}(t,x+\tau z)-\frac{\partial v}{\partial x}(t,x)\Big)dz

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{x_{\varepsilon}(t)-x+B_{1}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\tau z^{2}J(z)\frac{\partial^{2}v}{\partial x^{2}}(t,x+\sigma\tau z)d\sigma d\tau dz

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{6}{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)^{2}(t,x_{\varepsilon}(t))\int_{0}^{x_{\varepsilon}(t)-x+B_{1}}z^{2}J(z)dz.

It follows from Hypothesis
1.2
and
z\mapsto z^{2}J(z)\in L^{1}([1,+\infty))
that

\int_{0}^{x_{\varepsilon}(t)-x+B_{1}}z^{2}J(z)dz\leq\int_{0}^{1}z^{2}J(z)dz+\int_{1}^{+\infty}z^{2}J(z)dz\leq\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{1}^{+\infty}z^{2}e^{-z^{\beta}}dz,

and thus, we have

I_{2}\geq-\frac{6}{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)^{2}(t,x_{\varepsilon}(t))\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{1}^{+\infty}z^{2}e^{-z^{\beta}}dz\right).

Therefore, collecting the estimates of
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
, one obtains

\mathcal{D}[v]\geq-\varepsilon\mathcal{C}_{1}B_{1}^{1-\beta}e^{-B_{1}^{\beta}}-\frac{\mathcal{C}_{2}}{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)^{2}(t,x_{\varepsilon}(t)),

where
\mathcal{C}_{2}:=6\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{1}^{+\infty}z^{2}e^{-z^{\beta}}dz\right)
.

#
Continue with II:
x_{\varepsilon}(t)\leq x\leq x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t)+1
.

By the definition of
\mathcal{D}[v]
, since
v(t,\cdot)
is non-increasing on
\mathbb{R}
, for some
B_{2}>1
, we write

\mathcal{D}[v](t,x)\geq P.V.\int_{-B_{2}}^{B_{2}}J(z)\Big(v(t,x+z)-v(t,x)\Big)dz+\int_{B_{2}}^{+\infty}J(z)\Big(v(t,x+z)-v(t,x)\Big)dz:=II_{1}+II_{2}.

##
Let us estimate
II_{1}
.
Since
v(t,\cdot)
is
C^{2}
on
\mathbb{R}
and
\frac{\partial^{2}v}{\partial x^{2}}(t,\cdot)=0
on
(-\infty,x_{\varepsilon}(t))
, by using the symmetry of
J
and Taylor’s theorem, we get

\displaystyle II_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-B_{2}}^{B_{2}}J(z)\Big(v(t,x+z)+v(t,x-z)-2v(t,x)\Big)dz

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-B_{2}}^{B_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}\tau z^{2}J(z)\frac{\partial^{2}v}{\partial x^{2}}(t,x+\sigma\tau z)d\sigma d\tau dz

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\xi|\leq B_{2}\\
x+\xi\geq x_{\varepsilon}(t)\end{subarray}}\frac{\partial^{2}v}{\partial x^{2}}(t,x+\xi)\int_{-B_{2}}^{B_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\tau z^{2}J(z)d\tau dz.

By (
3.32
) and the convexity of
w(t,\cdot)
on
(x_{\varepsilon}(t),+\infty)
, for
x+\xi\geq x_{\varepsilon}(t)
, one gets

\frac{\partial^{2}v}{\partial x^{2}}(t,x+\xi)\geq-\frac{3}{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)^{2}(t,x+\xi)\geq-\frac{3}{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)^{2}(t,x_{\varepsilon}(t)).

It then follows from Hypothesis
1.2
and (
1.6
) that

\displaystyle II_{1}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{3}{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)^{2}(t,x_{\varepsilon}(t))\left(\int_{|z|\leq 1}z^{2}J(z)dz+\int_{1\leq|z|\leq B_{2}}z^{2}J(z)dz\right)

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{6}{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)^{2}(t,x_{\varepsilon}(t))\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{1}^{+\infty}z^{2}e^{-z^{\beta}}dz\right).

##
Now, let us estimate
II_{2}
.
By (
1.6
), L’Hôpital’s rule and
v\leq\varepsilon
, we have

II_{2}\geq-\mathcal{J}_{0}v(t,x)\int_{B_{2}}^{+\infty}e^{-z^{\beta}}dz\geq-\mathcal{C}_{3}\varepsilon B_{2}^{1-\beta}e^{-B_{2}^{\beta}}.

for some constant
\mathcal{C}_{3}>0
.
Therefore, collecting the above estimates, we arrive at

\mathcal{D}[v]\geq-\frac{\mathcal{C}_{2}}{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)^{2}(t,x_{\varepsilon}(t))-\mathcal{C}_{3}\varepsilon B_{2}^{1-\beta}e^{-B_{2}^{\beta}},

where
\mathcal{C}_{2}=6\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{1}^{+\infty}z^{2}e^{-z^{\beta}}dz\right)
.

#
Finally focus on III:
x>x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t)+1
.

By the definition of
\mathcal{D}[v]
, since
v(t,\cdot)
is non-increasing on
\mathbb{R}
, we write

\mathcal{D}[v](t,x)\geq P.V.\int_{-1}^{1}J(z)\Big(v(t,x+z)-v(t,x)\Big)dz+\int_{1}^{+\infty}J(z)\Big(v(t,x+z)-v(t,x)\Big)dz:=III_{1}+III_{2}.

##
Let us estimate
III_{1}
. Since
J
is symmetric, by Taylor’s theorem, we write

III_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}J(z)\Big(v(t,x+z)+v(t,x-z)-2v(t,x)\Big)dz=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}\tau z^{2}J(z)\frac{\partial^{2}v}{\partial x^{2}}(t,x+\sigma\tau z)d\sigma d\tau dz.

It follows from the convexity of
v(t,\cdot)
on
\left(x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t),+\infty\right)
that for all
x>x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t)+1
,

III_{1}\geq 0.

(3.37)

##
Let us estimate
III_{2}
.
By the fundamental theorem of calculus, we get

III_{2}=\int_{1}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{1}zJ(z)\frac{\partial v}{\partial x}(t,x+\tau z)d\tau dz.

Since
v(t,\cdot)
is convex on
\left(x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t),+\infty\right)
and
\tau z>0
for
z>0
and
\tau>0
, one has
\frac{\partial v}{\partial x}(t,x+\tau z)>\frac{\partial v}{\partial x}(t,x)
. Thus,

III_{2}\geq\frac{\partial v}{\partial x}(t,x)\int_{1}^{+\infty}zJ(z)dz.

Therefore, collecting the above estimates, for all
x>x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t)+1
, we obtain

\mathcal{D}[v]\geq\mathcal{C}_{4}\frac{\partial v}{\partial x}(t,x),

where
\mathcal{C}_{4}:=\int_{1}^{+\infty}zJ(z)dz
.

\Box

3.2.3
The function
v
is a sub-solution to (
1.1
) at later times

Proposition 3.10
.

For all
0<\varepsilon\leq\underline{\varepsilon}:=\min\{\frac{1}{2K},\varepsilon_{0}\}
and
0<\rho\leq\frac{r}{12}
, there is a time
\tilde{t}>0
such that for all
t\geq\tilde{t}
, we have

\frac{\partial v}{\partial t}(t,x)-\mathcal{D}[v](t,x)-f(v(t,x))\leq 0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }x\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.38)

Proof of Proposition
3.10
.

Before proving the proposition, we estimate
\frac{\partial v}{\partial t}
,
f(v)
and
\mathcal{D}[v]
. For
\frac{\partial v}{\partial t}
, by the definition of
v
and (
3.30
), we obtain

\frac{\partial v}{\partial t}(t,x)\leq\left\{\begin{aligned} &0,&x<x_{\varepsilon}(t),\\
&3\rho\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}},&x\geq x_{\varepsilon}(t).\end{aligned}\right.

(3.39)

Recalling that
f(z)\geq\frac{rz}{(1-\ln z)^{\alpha}}(1-Kz)
for
z\in(0,\xi_{0})
, when
x<x_{\varepsilon}(t)
, we have

f(v(t,x))\geq r\frac{v(t,x)}{(1-\ln v(t,x))^{\alpha}}(1-Kv(t,x))=r(1-K\varepsilon)\frac{\varepsilon}{(1-\ln\varepsilon)^{\alpha}},

thanks to
v(t,x)=\varepsilon
for all
x<x_{\varepsilon}(t)
.
When
x\geq x_{\varepsilon}(t)
, it follows from
w(t,x)\leq v(t,x)\leq\varepsilon
for
x\geq x_{\varepsilon}(t)
that

f(v(t,x))\geq r(1-K\varepsilon)\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}.

As a result, we have

f(v(t,x))\geq\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{r(1-K\varepsilon)\varepsilon}{(1-\ln\varepsilon)^{\alpha}},&x<x_{\varepsilon}(t),\\
&\frac{r(1-K\varepsilon)w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}},&x\geq x_{\varepsilon}(t).\end{aligned}\right.

(3.40)

One may find that there is
B_{0}>1
, such that for all
z\geq B_{0}
, we have

z^{1-\beta}e^{-z^{\beta}}\leq z^{-1}.

(3.41)

To prove the proposition, we split into three space zones:
\left(-\infty,x_{\varepsilon}(t)\right)
,
\left[x_{\varepsilon}(t),x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t)+1\right]
and
\left(x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t)+1,+\infty\right)
.

#
Zone 1:
x\in\left(-\infty,x_{\varepsilon}(t)\right)
.

In view of (
3.33
), let us choose
B_{1}:=\frac{2\mathcal{C}_{1}}{r}\frac{(1-\ln\varepsilon)^{\alpha}}{1-K\varepsilon}+B_{0}
. Then, by (
3.41
), for all
x<x_{\varepsilon}(t)
, one gets

\mathcal{D}[v]\geq-\frac{r}{2}\frac{(1-K\varepsilon)\varepsilon}{(1-\ln\varepsilon)^{\alpha}}-\frac{\mathcal{C}_{2}}{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)^{2}(t,x_{\varepsilon}(t)).

In view of (
3.27
), there is a time
t_{1}>0
, depending on
\varepsilon
, such that for all
t\geq t_{1}
, we have

\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)^{2}(t,x_{\varepsilon}(t))\leq\frac{r}{2\mathcal{C}_{2}}\frac{(1-K\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{2}}{(1-\ln\varepsilon)^{\alpha}},

and then we conclude

\mathcal{D}[v]\geq-\frac{r(1-K\varepsilon)\varepsilon}{(1-\ln\varepsilon)^{\alpha}}.

As a result, by (
3.29
) and (
3.40
), we arrive at

\frac{\partial v}{\partial t}(t,x)-\mathcal{D}[v](t,x)-f(v(t,x))\leq 0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }t\geq t_{1}\text{ and }x\leq x_{\varepsilon}(t).

(3.42)

#
Zone 2:
x\in\left[x_{\varepsilon}(t),x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t)+1\right]
.

We claim that there is a time
t^{\#}>0
, such that for all
t\geq t^{\#}
and
x\leq x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t)+1
, we have

w(t,x)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{6}.

Indeed, by (
3.7
), there is a time
t^{\#}>0
such that for
t\geq t^{\#}
,

x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{6}}(t)-x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t)>1.

In view of (
3.34
) and (
3.41
), let us choose
B_{2}:=\frac{3\mathcal{C}_{3}}{\rho}\left(1-\ln\frac{\varepsilon}{6}\right)^{\alpha}+B_{0}
such that

\mathcal{C}_{3}\varepsilon B_{2}^{1-\beta}e^{-B_{2}^{\beta}}\leq\mathcal{C}_{3}\varepsilon B_{2}^{-1}\leq\frac{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}\rho}{\left(1-\ln\frac{\varepsilon}{6}\right)^{\alpha}}\leq 2\rho\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ for all }t\geq t^{\#}\text{ and }x\leq x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t)+1.

On the other hand, in view of (
3.27
), there is
t^{*}>0
such that for
t\geq t^{*}
we have

\frac{\mathcal{C}_{2}}{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)^{2}(t,x_{\varepsilon}(t))\leq\rho\frac{\frac{\varepsilon}{6}}{(1-\ln\frac{\varepsilon}{6})^{\alpha}}\leq\rho\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }x\leq x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t)+1.

Thus, by (
3.34
), for all
t\geq t_{2}:=\max\{t^{\#},t^{*}\}
and
x_{\varepsilon}(t)\leq x\leq x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t)+1
, we arrive at

\mathcal{D}[v]\geq-3\rho\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}.

As a result, by collecting (
3.39
) and (
3.40
), for all
t\geq t_{2}
and
x_{\varepsilon}(t)\leq x\leq x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t)+1
, we achieve

\Big(\frac{\partial v}{\partial t}-\mathcal{D}[v]-f(v)\Big)(t,x)\leq\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}\Big(3\rho+3\rho-r(1-K\varepsilon)\Big)\leq 0,

(3.43)

as soon as
\rho\leq\frac{r}{12}
and
\varepsilon\leq\min\{\frac{1}{2K},\varepsilon_{0}\}
.

#
Zone 3:
x\in\left(x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t)+1,+\infty\right)
.

In view of (
3.31
) and (
3.35
), since
\alpha\beta+\beta-1<0
, then

(1+\varphi_{0}(x))^{\alpha}\varphi^{\prime}_{0}(x)=x^{\alpha\beta+\beta-1}\left(1+\frac{1}{x^{\beta}}\right)^{\alpha}\to 0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{as }x\to+\infty,

and it follows that there is a time
t_{3}>0
such that for all
t\geq t_{3}
and
x\geq x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t)+1
, we have

\mathcal{C}_{4}(1+\varphi_{0}(x))^{\alpha}\varphi^{\prime}_{0}(x)\leq\rho.

As a result, collecting (
3.39
) and (
3.40
), for all
t\geq t_{3}
and
x>x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}(t)+1
, we achieve

\left(\frac{\partial v}{\partial t}-\mathcal{D}[v]-f(v)\right)(t,x)\leq\frac{w(t,x)}{(1-\ln w(t,x))^{\alpha}}\Big(3\rho+3\rho-r(1-K\varepsilon)\Big)\leq 0,

(3.44)

as soon as
\rho\leq\frac{r}{12}
and
\varepsilon\leq\underline{\varepsilon}:=\min\{\frac{1}{2K},\varepsilon_{0}\}
.

Now, let us denote
\tilde{t}:=\max\{t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\}
. Therefore, by collecting (
3.42
), (
3.43
) and (
3.44
), for all
0<\rho\leq\frac{r}{12}
and
0<\varepsilon\leq\underline{\varepsilon}
, the inequality (
3.38
) holds for all
t\geq\tilde{t}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

\Box

3.2.4
A flattening estimate

By Proposition
3.10
, the function
v
is a sub-solution to (
1.1
) for all
t\geq\tilde{t}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
. To validate the comparison principle, one would like to prove that there exist
t_{1}\geq 0
and
R>0
such that

u(t_{1},\cdot-R)\geq v(\tilde{t},\cdot)\hskip 9.24994pt\text{on }\mathbb{R}.

To do so, we derive a flattening estimate for the solution to (
1.1
). Let
\underline{u}
be the solution to the equation

\begin{cases}\frac{\partial\underline{u}}{\partial t}(t,x)-\mathcal{D}[\underline{u}](t,x)=0,&t>0,\ x\in\mathbb{R},\\
\underline{u}(0,x)=u_{0}(x),&x\in\mathbb{R},\end{cases}

(3.45)

where
u_{0}
satisfies Hypothesis
1.5
. Since
f\geq 0
, the comparison principle implies that
\underline{u}\leq u
. As in
[
8
]
, we have the following

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
\underline{u}
be the solution to (
3.45
). Then, for any positive constant
\kappa
, we have

\lim_{x\to+\infty}e^{x^{\beta}}\underline{u}(t,x)\geq\kappa t\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ for all }t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}.

Proof of Proposition
3.11
.

For all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, let us define

w(t,x):=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2},&x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{+},\\
