\frac{\kappa t}{e^{x^{\beta}}+2\kappa t},&x\in\mathbb{R}^{+},\end{cases}

where
\kappa>0
. Some direct calculations show that for all
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

\frac{\partial w}{\partial t}(t,x)=\frac{\kappa e^{x^{\beta}}}{(e^{x^{\beta}}+2\kappa t)^{2}}>0,

\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}(t,x)=-\frac{\beta\kappa tx^{\beta-1}e^{x^{\beta}}}{(e^{x^{\beta}}+2\kappa t)^{2}}<0,

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}w}{\partial x^{2}}(t,x)=\frac{\beta\kappa tx^{2\beta-2}e^{x^{\beta}}}{(e^{x^{\beta}}+2\kappa t)^{2}}\left[\beta-\frac{4\beta\kappa t}{e^{x^{\beta}}+2\kappa t}+(1-\beta)x^{-\beta}\right].

One may notice that, for all
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
, the function
w
is increasing in
t
and decreasing in
x
, and that, for
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\left[\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(2\kappa t+1),+\infty\right)
, the function
w(t,\cdot)
is convex.

Let us estimate
\mathcal{D}[w]
. Let
B>1
to be chosen later. In view of Hypothesis
1.2
, for all
x\geq R_{0}+B
, we write

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[w](t,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{B}^{+\infty}J(z)\Big(w(t,x+z)-w(t,x)\Big)dz+\frac{1}{2}\int_{-B}^{B}J(z)\Big(w(t,x+z)+w(t,x-z)-2w(t,x)\Big)dz

\displaystyle+\int_{-\infty}^{-B}J(z)\Big(w(t,x+z)-w(t,x)\Big)dz

\displaystyle\geq-w(t,x)\int_{B}^{+\infty}J(z)dz+\frac{1}{2}\int_{-B}^{B}J(z)\Big(w(t,x+z)+w(t,x-z)-2w(t,x)\Big)dz

\displaystyle+\int_{-x}^{-B}J(z)\Big(w(t,x+z)-w(t,x)\Big)dz+\int_{-\infty}^{-x}J(z)\Big(w(t,x+z)-w(t,x)\Big)dz

\displaystyle\geq-\mathcal{J}_{0}w(t,x)\int_{B}^{+\infty}e^{-z^{\beta}}dz+\frac{1}{2}\int_{-B}^{B}J(z)\Big(w(t,x+z)+w(t,x-z)-2w(t,x)\Big)dz

\displaystyle+\mathcal{J}_{0}^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}-w(t,x)\right)\int_{x}^{+\infty}e^{-z^{\beta}}dz.

For any
\mathcal{C}>0
, we define
t_{\kappa}:=\frac{2\mathcal{C}}{\kappa}
. Since, for all
t\in(0,t_{\kappa})
, the function
w(t,\cdot)
is convex on
\left[\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(4\mathcal{C}+1),+\infty\right)
, then, letting
B\geq\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(4\mathcal{C}+1)
,
for all
(t,x)\in(0,t_{\kappa})\times[R_{0}+B,+\infty)
, we get

\int_{-B}^{B}J(z)\Big(w(t,x+z)+w(t,x-z)-2w(t,x)\Big)dz=\int_{-B}^{B}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}z^{2}J(z)\tau\frac{\partial^{2}w}{\partial x^{2}}(t,x+\sigma\tau z)d\sigma d\tau dz\geq 0.

By using L’Hôpital’s rule, one may find that

\int_{y}^{+\infty}e^{-z^{\beta}}dz\asymp y^{1-\beta}e^{-y^{\beta}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }y>0,

and it follows that, for all
(t,x)\in(0,t_{\kappa})\times[R_{0}+B,+\infty)
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[w](t,x)

\displaystyle\geq-\mathcal{C}_{1}w(t,x)B^{1-\beta}e^{-B^{\beta}}+\mathcal{C}_{2}\Big(\frac{1}{2}-w(t,x)\Big)x^{1-\beta}e^{-x^{\beta}}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{C}_{1}B^{1-\beta}e^{-B^{\beta}}\frac{\kappa t}{e^{x^{\beta}}+2\kappa t}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{C}_{2}\frac{x^{1-\beta}}{e^{x^{\beta}}+2\kappa t},

for some positive constants
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
. Now, let us check, for all
(t,x)\in(0,t_{\kappa})\times[R_{0}+B,+\infty)
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial w}{\partial t}-\mathcal{D}[w]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{e^{x^{\beta}}+2\kappa t}\left(\frac{\kappa e^{x^{\beta}}}{e^{x^{\beta}}+2\kappa t}+\mathcal{C}_{1}B^{1-\beta}e^{-B^{\beta}}\kappa t-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{C}_{2}x^{1-\beta}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{e^{x^{\beta}}+2\kappa t}\left(\kappa+\mathcal{C}_{1}B^{1-\beta}e^{-B^{\beta}}\kappa t-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{C}_{2}B^{1-\beta}\right).

Notice that there is some
B_{0}>1
such that for all
B\geq B_{0}
, we have

B^{1-\beta}e^{-B^{\beta}}\leq B^{-1}.

We choose
B\geq B_{c}:=\left(\frac{2(\kappa+1)}{\mathcal{C}_{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\beta}}+2\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}_{1}+B_{0}+\ln^{\frac{1}{\beta}}(4\mathcal{C}+1)
, so that, for all
t\in(0,t_{\kappa})
,

\displaystyle\kappa+\mathcal{C}_{1}B^{1-\beta}e^{-B^{\beta}}\kappa t-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{C}_{2}B^{1-\beta}\leq\kappa+2\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}_{1}B^{-1}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{C}_{2}B^{1-\beta}\leq\kappa+1-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{C}_{2}B^{1-\beta}\leq 0.

Thus, we arrive at

\frac{\partial w}{\partial t}-\mathcal{D}[w]\leq 0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }t\in(0,t_{\kappa})\text{ and }x\in[R_{0}+B_{c},+\infty).

(3.46)

In view of Hypothesis
1.5
, denoting
M:=\liminf_{x\to-\infty}u_{0}(x)
, there is
b\in\mathbb{R}
such that
u_{0}(x)\geq\frac{M}{2}
for all
x\leq b
. One may notice that
\underline{u}=\frac{M}{2}
is a sub-solution to (
3.45
) on
[0,\infty)\times(-\infty,b]
. Thus, the comparison principle implies that

\underline{u}(t,x)\geq\frac{M}{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }t>0\text{ and }x\leq b.

Since
\underline{u}(t,\cdot)
is decreasing for each
t>0
, by the definition of
w
, we get
\underline{u}(t,x-R_{0}-B_{c}+b)\geq\frac{M}{2}\geq Mw(t,x)
for
t>0
and
x\leq R_{0}+B_{c}
. Denoting
\tilde{u}(t,x)=\underline{u}(t,x-R_{0}-B_{c}+b)
, it follows that we have

\begin{cases}\frac{\partial\tilde{u}}{\partial t}(t,x)-\mathcal{D}[\tilde{u}](t,x)=0,&\text{for all }t\in(0,t_{\kappa})\text{ and }x\in\mathbb{R},\\
\tilde{u}(t,x)\geq Mw(t,x),&\text{for all }t\in[0,t_{\kappa}]\text{ and }x\leq R_{0}+B_{c},\\
\tilde{u}(0,x)>Mw(0,x),&\text{for all }x\in\mathbb{R}.\end{cases}

Thus, combining (
3.46
) and using an adapted comparison principle from
[
8
, Theorem
2.2
]
, we get
\tilde{u}(t,x)\geq Mw(t,x)
for
t\in(0,t_{\kappa})
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, and it follows from the definition of
t_{\kappa}
that, for all
x\geq-R_{0}-B_{c}+b
,

\underline{u}\left(\frac{t_{\kappa}}{2},x\right)\geq Mw\left(\frac{t_{\kappa}}{2},x+R_{0}+B_{c}-b\right)=\frac{M\mathcal{C}}{e^{(x+R_{0}+B_{c}-b)^{\beta}}+2\mathcal{C}}.

Thus, we have

\lim_{x\to+\infty}e^{x^{\beta}}\underline{u}\left(\frac{t_{\kappa}}{2},x\right)\geq\lim_{x\to+\infty}\frac{M\mathcal{C}e^{x^{\beta}}}{e^{(x+R_{0}+B_{c}-b)^{\beta}}+2\mathcal{C}}=M\mathcal{C},

or, equivalently, that, for all positive real number
\mathcal{C}
,

\lim_{x\to+\infty}e^{x^{\beta}}\underline{u}\left(\frac{\mathcal{C}}{\kappa},x\right)\geq M\mathcal{C}.

Therefore, we conclude that

\lim_{x\to+\infty}e^{x^{\beta}}\underline{u}(t,x)\geq M\kappa t\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }t\in(0,+\infty).

This completes the proof.

\Box

3.2.5
Proof of the lower bound in Theorem
1.6

Now, with Proposition
3.11
, it follows from the comparison principle that
\lim_{x\to+\infty}e^{x^{\beta}}u(t,x)\geq t
for all
t>0
. Then, there is
\xi_{1}>0
such that, for all
x\geq\xi_{1}
, we have

u(t,x)\geq\frac{1}{2}te^{-x^{\beta}}.

In view of the definition of
v
, by (
3.28
), we have

v(t,x)\leq 3w(t,x)\leq 3e^{[\rho(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}e^{-x^{\beta}}.

Thus, for all
t\geq t_{1}^{\prime}:=6e^{[\rho(\alpha+1)\tilde{t}]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}
, we have

u(t,x)\geq\frac{1}{2}te^{-x^{\beta}}\geq 3e^{[\rho(\alpha+1)\tilde{t}]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}e^{-x^{\beta}}\geq v(\tilde{t},x)\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }x\geq\xi_{1}.

(3.47)

Now, since propagation in integro-differential equations with Allee effect occurs
[
2
]
, it follows from the comparison principle that there are
t_{1}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
\xi_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

u(t,\xi_{2})\geq\varepsilon\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }t\geq t_{1}^{\prime\prime},

and thus, since
u(t,\cdot)
is non-increasing, one gets

u(t,x)\geq\varepsilon\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }t\geq t_{1}^{\prime\prime}\text{ and }x\leq\xi_{2}.

It follows from the definition of
v
that

u(t,x)\geq\varepsilon\geq v(\tilde{t},x)\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }t\geq t_{1}^{\prime\prime}\text{ and }x\leq\xi_{2}.

Thus, by (
3.47
) and denoting
t_{1}:=\max\{t_{1}^{\prime},t_{2}^{\prime\prime}\}
, one obtains

u(t_{1},x-|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|)\geq v(\tilde{t},x)\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }x\in\mathbb{R}.

As a result, the comparison principle implies

u(t,x-|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|)\geq v(t+\tilde{t}-t_{1},x)\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }t\geq t_{1}\text{ and }x\in\mathbb{R}.

With the above inequality, let us give a lower bound when
\lambda
is small, that is
\lambda\in(0,\varepsilon]
. Since
v(t,x_{\varepsilon}(t))=\varepsilon
, we have

u(t,x-|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|)\geq\varepsilon\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }t\geq t_{1}\text{ and }x\leq x_{\varepsilon}(t+\tilde{t}-t_{1}).

(3.48)

Therefore, by (
3.25
), for any
0<\lambda\leq\varepsilon
, there is a time
\underline{T}_{1}>t_{1}
such that we have

X_{\lambda}(t)\geq x_{\varepsilon}(t+\tilde{t}-t_{1})-|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|\geq\underline{C}_{1}t^{\frac{1}{\beta(\alpha+1)}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ for all }t\geq\underline{T}_{1},

where
\underline{C}_{1}:=\left[\frac{\rho(\alpha+1)}{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{\beta(\alpha+1)}}
. This gives a lower bound to the level set
E_{\lambda}(t)
for
\lambda\in(0,\varepsilon]
.

Now, we upgrade the previous bound to
\lambda\in(\varepsilon,1)
. Let
w
be the solution to (
1.1
) with the following initial data

w_{0}(x):=\begin{cases}\varepsilon,&x\leq-1,\\
-\varepsilon x,&-1<x<0,\\
0,&x\geq 0.\end{cases}

(3.49)

Since propagation occurs
[
2
]
, the comparison principle implies that there exists a time
t_{\lambda}>0
such that

w(t_{\lambda},x)>\lambda\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for any }x\leq 0.

(3.50)

On the other hand, it follows from (
3.48
) and (
3.49
) that

u(t,x-|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|)\geq w_{0}(x-x_{\varepsilon}(t+\tilde{t}-t_{1}))\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for any }t\geq t_{1}\text{ and }x\in\mathbb{R}.

Thus, by the comparison principle, we have

u(T+t,x-|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|)\geq w(T,x-x_{\varepsilon}(t+\tilde{t}-t_{1}))\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ for any }\ T\geq 0,t\geq t_{1}\text{ and }x\in\mathbb{R},.

Then, by (
3.50
) and letting
T=t_{\lambda}
, we obtain

u(t+t_{\lambda},x-|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|)>\lambda\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for any }t\geq t_{1}\text{ and }x\leq x_{\varepsilon}(t+\tilde{t}-t_{1}).

As a result, there exists a time
\underline{T}_{2}>\max\{t_{\lambda},t_{1}\}
and a constant
\underline{C}_{2}>0
such that, for
t\geq\underline{T}_{2}
, we have

X_{\lambda}(t)\geq x_{\varepsilon}(t+\tilde{t}-t_{1}-t_{\lambda})-|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|\geq\underline{C}_{2}t^{\frac{1}{\beta(\alpha+1)}},

which gives the lower bound of the level set for any
\lambda\in(\varepsilon,1)
.
Therefore, taking
\underline{C}:=\min\{\underline{C}_{1},\underline{C}_{2}\}
and
\underline{T}=\max\{\underline{T}_{1},\underline{T}_{2}\}
, we get the lower bound in Theorem
1.6
for all
t\geq\underline{T}
.

4
Exponential propagation: proof of Theorem
1.7

In this section, we prove the sharp exponential rate stated in Theorem
1.7
. In the polynomial acceleration regime, the growth parameter
\rho
, used as a free parameter, does not change the acceleration rate. In the regime of exponential acceleration, it is not the case anymore: small perturbations in the growth rate have a significant impact on the estimates of the acceleration rate. In such a situation, using the sub- and super-solutions considered in the previous section would only provide rough estimates on the exponential acceleration rate.

To obtain much sharper estimates, we shall construct much more precise sub- and super-solutions where the growth parameter
\rho=r
is now fixed by the form of
f
. One way to get such precise sub- and super- solutions is to add a degree of freedom to the construction adding a logarithmic correction to the tails. Such an approach requires performing most of the previous calculations again with higher precision.

4.1
The upper bound

Here, we prove the upper bound in Theorem
1.7
. To do so, we construct an adequate super-solution.

4.1.1
Definition of the super-solution and preliminary computations

Let us define

v_{0}(x):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &1,&x<L,\\
&C_{L}\frac{\ln^{q}x}{x^{2s}},&x\geq L,\end{aligned}\right.

where
q>0
,
C_{L}:=\frac{L^{2s}}{\ln^{q}L}
and
L\geq\max\{e,e^{\frac{q}{2s}},a\}+1
large enough, so that
v_{0}
is decreasing, continuous and less than or equal to
1
. Here,
a
comes from Hypothesis
1.5
, and thus, one may notice that

v_{0}\geq u_{0}.

(4.1)

Let us define

\psi(t,x)=\exp{\left\{1-\left\{\left[1-\ln[(1+\kappa t^{p})v_{0}(x)]\right]^{\alpha+1}-r(\alpha+1)t\right\}^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}\right\}},

where
p\geq 0
and
\kappa\geq e
are to be determined. The domain of
\psi
will be given later. For later use, we state several properties related to
v_{0}
and
\psi
, the proofs of which can be found in Appendix
B.1
. We first obtain:

Lemma 4.1
.

I.

For
y\in(0,1)
, we have, denoting by
v_{0}^{-1}
the inverse of
v_{0}
on
[L,+\infty)
,

v_{0}^{-1}(y)\asymp\frac{\left(\ln L-\frac{1}{2s}\ln y\right)^{\frac{q}{2s}}}{y^{\frac{1}{2s}}},

(4.2)

and

(v^{\prime}\circ v^{-1})(y)\asymp-y^{1+\frac{1}{2s}}(\ln y^{-1})^{-\frac{q}{2s}}.

(4.3)

II.

For
t\geq t^{\star}:=\frac{1}{r(\alpha+1)}
, define

x_{\Lambda}(t):=v_{0}^{-1}\left\{(1+\kappa t^{p})^{-1}\exp\left\{1-\left[r(\alpha+1)t+(1-\ln\Lambda)^{\alpha+1}\right]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}\right\}\right\}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for any }\Lambda\in(0,2].

Then, for all
t\geq t^{\star}
, we have

x_{\Lambda}(t)\asymp_{\Lambda}t^{\frac{1}{2s}\left(p+\frac{q}{\alpha+1}\right)}\exp\left\{\frac{1}{2s}[r(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}\right\},

(4.4)

and for any
0<\Lambda_{1}<\Lambda_{2}\leq 2
,

x_{\Lambda_{1}}(t)-x_{\Lambda_{2}}(t)\asymp_{\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}+\frac{1}{2s}\left(\frac{q}{\alpha+1}+p\right)}e^{\frac{1}{2s}[r(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}.

(4.5)

Let us now summarize in the next two lemmas some basic properties for
\psi
that we will constantly use.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\Lambda\in(0,2]
.
For
(t,x)\in[t^{\star},\infty)\times[x_{\Lambda}(t),+\infty)
, the function
\psi
is well defined, non-decreasing in
t
and non-increasing in
x
, and for any
t\in[t^{\star},\infty)
,
\psi(t,\cdot)
is convex and log-convex on
[x_{\Lambda}(t),+\infty)
.
In addition, there exist
t_{0}>t^{\star}
,
\gamma_{1}>0
and
\gamma_{2}>0
, such that for all
t\geq t_{0}
and
x\geq x_{\Lambda}(t)
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial t}\geq\left(\gamma_{1}t^{-\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}+r\right)\frac{\psi}{(1-\ln\psi)^{\alpha}},

(4.6)

and

-\gamma_{2}^{-1}\frac{\ln^{\alpha}x}{x}\frac{\psi}{(1-\ln\psi)^{\alpha}}\leq\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial x}\leq-\gamma_{2}\frac{t^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}}}{x}\frac{\psi}{(1-\ln\psi)^{\alpha}}.

(4.7)

Moreover, there is some positive constant
\mathcal{C}_{0}
, such that for all
t\geq t^{\star}
and
x\geq x_{\Lambda}(t)
, we have

0\leq\frac{\partial^{2}\psi}{\partial x^{2}}(t,x)\leq\frac{\mathcal{C}_{0}}{x}\frac{\psi(t,x)}{(1-\ln\psi(t,x))^{\alpha}}.

(4.8)

Lemma 4.3
.

There is a time
t^{\dagger}\geq t^{\star}
such that, for all
z\in\left(\frac{1}{x},\min\left\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{x}\right\}\right)
and
(t,x)\in[t^{\dagger},\infty)\times[x_{1}(t)+1,+\infty)
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1-\ln\psi(t,x(1-z))}{1-\ln\psi(t,x)}\geq 1-2(2s)^{\alpha+1}(\ln^{\alpha}x)z-C_{\alpha}(\ln^{2\alpha}x)z^{2},

and

\displaystyle\frac{\psi(t,x(1-z))}{\psi(t,x)}\leq\exp\left\{2(2s)^{\alpha+1}(\ln^{\alpha}x)z+C_{\alpha}(\ln^{2\alpha}x)z\right\},

for some positive constant
C_{\alpha}:=4\alpha(\alpha+1)(2s)^{2\alpha+2}
.

Now, we are ready to properly define the super-solution to (
1.1
) we need. Let us define, for
t\in[t^{\star},\infty)
,

\Psi(t,x)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &1,&x<x_{1}(t),\\
&\psi(t,x),&x\geq x_{1}(t).\end{aligned}\right.

(4.9)

Unless otherwise specified, throughout the subsection later, we assume that
t\in[t^{\star},\infty)
.

We start by proving an estimate for
\mathcal{D}[\Psi]
.

4.1.2
Estimation of
\mathcal{D}[\Psi](t,\cdot)
on
[x_{1}(t),+\infty)

Lemma 4.4
.

Denote
R(t):=\gamma_{3}t^{-1}x_{1}(t)
for some
\gamma_{3}>0
and
\bar{q}:=(2s+1)(\alpha+1)-q
. We have the following estimate for
\mathcal{D}[\Psi]
.

I.

When
x_{1}(t)\leq x<x_{1}(t)+R(t)
, there is a time
\tilde{t}_{1}\geq t^{\star}
such that, for all
t\geq\tilde{t}_{1}
, we have

\mathcal{D}[\Psi](t,x)\leq\mathcal{C}_{1}e^{-\frac{1}{2s}[r(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}\frac{\psi(t,x)}{(1-\ln\psi(t,x))^{\alpha}}-\mathcal{J}_{3}R(t)\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial x}(t,x_{1}(t)),

for some positive constant
\mathcal{C}_{1}
, where
\mathcal{J}_{3}:=\int^{\infty}_{1}J(z)dz
.

II.

When
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
, there is a time
\tilde{t}_{2}>t^{\star}
such that for all
t\geq\tilde{t}_{2}
and
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
, we have

\mathcal{D}[\Psi](t,x)\leq\left(\mathcal{C}_{3}\frac{\ln^{\alpha+1}x}{x^{\min\{2s,1\}}}+\mathcal{C}_{4}\frac{\ln^{\bar{q}}x}{x^{s}}+\mathcal{C}_{5}\frac{\kappa^{-1}t^{-p}}{(1-\ln\psi(t,x))^{2\alpha-q+1}}+\mathcal{C}_{6}e^{-\frac{1}{2s}[r(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}\right)\frac{\psi(t,x)}{(1-\ln\psi(t,x))^{\alpha}},

for some positive constants
\mathcal{C}_{3}
,
\mathcal{C}_{4}
,
\mathcal{C}_{5}
and
\mathcal{C}_{6}
.

Proof.

We divide the proof into two parts:
x_{1}(t)\leq x<x_{1}(t)+R(t)
and
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
.

#
Part I:
x_{1}(t)\leq x<x_{1}(t)+R(t)
.

By the definition of
\mathcal{D}[\Psi]
, since
\Psi(t,\cdot)
is non-increasing on
\mathbb{R}
, we write

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[\Psi]

\displaystyle\leq P.V.\int_{|z|<1}J(z)\Big(\Psi(t,x+z)-\Psi(t,x)\Big)dz+\int_{z\leq-1}J(z)\Big(\Psi(t,x+z)-\Psi(t,x)\Big)dz

\displaystyle:=I_{1}+I_{2}.

##
Estimating
I_{1}
.

In view of (
4.5
), there is a time
\tilde{t}_{1}\geq t^{\star}
such that
x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t)\geq 1
for all
t\geq\tilde{t}_{1}
.
Since
\Psi(t,x)=1\leq\psi(t,x)
for
x_{2}(t)\leq x\leq x_{1}(t)
and
\Psi(t,x)=\psi(t,x)
for
x\geq x_{1}(t)
, it follows from Taylor’s theorem that, for all
(t,x)\in[\tilde{t}_{1},\infty)\times[x_{1}(t),+\infty)
,

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{|z|<1}J(z)\Big(\psi(t,x+z)+\psi(t,x-z)-2\psi(t,x)\Big)dz\leq\mathcal{J}_{1}\sup_{|B|\leq 1}\frac{\partial^{2}\psi}{\partial x^{2}}(t,x+B).

As a result, by (
4.4
) and (
4.8
), for all
(t,x)\in[\tilde{t}_{1},\infty)\times[x_{1}(t),+\infty)
, we arrive at

I_{1}\leq\mathcal{J}_{1}\frac{\mathcal{C}_{0}}{x-1}\frac{\psi(t,x-1)}{(1-\ln\psi(t,x-1))^{\alpha}}\leq\mathcal{C}_{1}e^{-\frac{1}{2s}[r(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}\frac{\psi(t,x)}{(1-\ln\psi(t,x))^{\alpha}},

(4.10)

for some positive constant
\mathcal{C}_{1}
.

##
Let us estimate
I_{2}
.

Since
\psi(t,\cdot)
is non-increasing on
[x_{1}(t),+\infty)
and
J\in L^{1}((-\infty,-1])
, then for
x\in[x_{1}(t),x_{1}(t)+R(t))
, we have

I_{2}\leq\mathcal{J}_{3}[1-\psi(t,x_{1}(t)+R(t))],

where
\mathcal{J}_{3}=\int^{\infty}_{1}J(z)dz
.
By convexity of
\psi(t,\cdot)
, one may get

\psi(t,x_{1}(t)+R(t)\geq\psi(t,x_{1}(t))+R(t)\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial x}(t,x_{1}(t)),

and thus, since
\psi(t,x_{1}(t))=1
, one arrives at

I_{2}\leq-\mathcal{J}_{3}R(t)\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial x}(t,x_{1}(t)).

(4.11)

Therefore, collecting (
4.10
) and (
4.11
), for all
(t,x)\in[\tilde{t}_{1},\infty)\times[x_{1}(t),x_{1}(t)+R(t))
, we achieve

\mathcal{D}[\Psi](t,x)\leq\mathcal{C}_{1}e^{-\frac{1}{2s}[r(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}\frac{\psi(t,x)}{(1-\ln\psi(t,x))^{\alpha}}-\mathcal{J}_{3}R(t)\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial x}(t,x_{1}(t)).

#
Part II:
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
.
By the definition of
\mathcal{D}[\Psi]
, since
0<\Psi\leq 1
and
\Psi(t,\cdot)
is non-increasing, we write

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[\Psi]

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\infty}^{x_{1}(t)-x}J(z)dz+\int_{x_{1}(t)-x}^{-1}J(z)\Big(\psi(t,x+z)-\psi(t,x)\Big)dz

\displaystyle+P.V.\int_{-1}^{1}J(z)\Big(\psi(t,x+z)-\psi(t,x)\Big)dz:=II_{1}+II_{2}+II_{3}.

##
Let us estimate
II_{1}
.
By (
1.7
), we have

II_{1}\leq\int_{-\infty}^{x_{1}(t)-x}J(z)dz\leq\frac{\mathcal{J}_{0}}{2s}\frac{1}{[x-x_{1}(t)]^{2s}}.

For any fixed
t\in[t^{\star},+\infty)
, we denote
V_{t}(x):=\{1-\ln[(1+\kappa t^{p})v_{0}(x)]\}^{\alpha+1}-1
for
x>L
.
By some direct calculations, for all
x>L
, one may get

V_{t}^{\prime}(x)=-(\alpha+1)\{1-\ln[(1+\kappa t^{p})v_{0}(x)]\}^{\alpha}\frac{v_{0}^{\prime}}{v_{0}}(x),

and

V_{t}^{\prime\prime}(x)=(\alpha+1)\{1-\ln[(1+\kappa t^{p})v_{0}(x)]\}^{\alpha-1}\left[\alpha\left(\frac{v_{0}^{\prime}}{v_{0}}\right)^{2}(x)-\{1-\ln[(1+\kappa t^{p})v_{0}(x)]\}\left(\frac{v_{0}^{\prime}}{v_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(x)\right].

Since
\frac{v_{0}^{\prime}}{v_{0}}(x)=\left(-2s+\frac{q}{\ln x}\right)\frac{1}{x}<0
and
\left(\frac{v_{0}^{\prime}}{v_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(x)=\left(2s-\frac{q}{\ln x}-\frac{q}{\ln^{2}x}\right)\frac{1}{x^{2}}>0
for
x
large enough, there is
\tilde{L}\geq L
such that, for all
x\geq\tilde{L}
, we have

\displaystyle\alpha\left(\frac{v_{0}^{\prime}}{v_{0}}\right)^{2}(x)-\{1-\ln[(1+\kappa t^{p})v_{0}(x)]\}\left(\frac{v_{0}^{\prime}}{v_{0}}\right)^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left[\alpha\left(-2s+\frac{q}{\ln x}\right)^{2}-[r(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}\left(2s-\frac{q}{\ln x}-\frac{q}{\ln^{2}x}\right)\right]\frac{1}{x^{2}}\leq 0.

It follows that

V_{t}^{\prime}(x)>0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ and }\hskip 9.24994ptV_{t}^{\prime\prime}(x)\leq 0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }x\geq\tilde{L}.

Since
R(t)
and
x(t)
go to infinity as
t
to infinity, there is
t_{1}\geq t^{\star}
such for all
t\geq t_{1}
,
R(t)
and
x_{1}(t)
are greater than
\tilde{L}
, and thus, for all
t\geq t_{1}
and
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
, we get

\displaystyle V_{t}(x-x_{1}(t))\geq V_{t}(x)-V_{t}(x_{1}(t))

\displaystyle=\{1-\ln[(1+\kappa t^{p})v_{0}(x)]\}^{\alpha+1}-r(\alpha+1)t-1

\displaystyle=(1-\ln\psi)^{\alpha+1}-1

\displaystyle=\left\{1-\ln\left[(1+\kappa t^{p})v_{0}\left(v_{0}^{-1}\left(\left(1+\kappa t^{p}\right)^{-1}\psi\right)\right)\right]\right\}^{\alpha+1}-1,

and then, since
V_{t}
is strictly increasing on
[\tilde{L},+\infty)
, by (
4.2
), for all
t\geq t_{1}
and
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
, we achieve

x-x_{1}(t)\geq v_{0}^{-1}\left(\left(1+\kappa t^{p}\right)^{-1}\psi\right)\gtrsim\frac{\left\{\ln L-\frac{1}{2s}\ln[(1+\kappa t^{p})^{-1}\psi]\right\}^{\frac{q}{2s}}}{[\kappa^{-1}t^{-p}\psi]^{\frac{1}{2s}}}.

(4.12)

It follows that for all
t\geq t_{1}
and
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
,
5
5

5

One may notice from here that it is necessary to have the factor
\ln^{q}x
in
v_{0}
. Indeed, if
v_{0}
is defined without
\ln^{q}x
, then we get
I_{1}\leq\mathcal{C}_{2}\kappa^{-1}t^{-p}\psi
as an estimate of
I_{1}
, which is much larger than
f(\psi)
. This is not what we expect.

II_{1}\leq\mathcal{C}_{2}\frac{\kappa^{-1}t^{-p}\psi}{\left\{2s\ln L-\ln[(1+\kappa t^{p})^{-1}\psi]\right\}^{q}}\leq\mathcal{C}_{2}\frac{\kappa^{-1}t^{-p}\psi}{(1-\ln\psi)^{q}},

(4.13)

for some
\mathcal{C}_{2}>0
.

##
Let us estimate
II_{2}
.

We denote
B:=\frac{\gamma_{4}}{\ln^{\alpha+1}x}
where
\gamma_{4}>0
is small enough such that

1-2(2s)^{\alpha+1}\gamma_{4}-C_{\alpha}\gamma_{4}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}.

(4.14)

By taking
\gamma_{4}<\frac{1}{2}
, one may notice that
x\mapsto x-\frac{\gamma_{4}x}{\ln^{\alpha+1}x}
is increasing for all
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
, it follows that, for all
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
,

x-\frac{\gamma_{4}x}{\ln^{\alpha+1}x}\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)-\gamma_{4}\frac{x_{1}(t)+R(t)}{\ln^{\alpha+1}(x_{1}(t)+R(t))}.

Recalling
R(t)=\gamma_{3}t^{-1}x_{1}(t)
and using (
4.4
), one has
R(t)-\gamma_{4}\frac{x_{1}(t)+R(t)}{\ln^{\alpha+1}(x_{1}(t)+R(t))}\geq 0
for all
t\geq t^{\star}
by taking
\gamma_{4}
small enough, and thus, one achieve

x-\frac{\gamma_{4}x}{\ln^{\alpha+1}x}\geq x_{1}(t).

Therefore, there is a time
t_{2}>t^{\star}
and constant
\gamma_{4}>0
such that for all
t\geq t_{2}
and
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
, we have

\frac{1}{x}\leq\frac{\gamma_{4}}{\ln^{\alpha+1}x}\leq\min\left\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{x}\right\}.

By using the change of variables
u=-\frac{z}{x}
, for all
t\geq t_{2}
and
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
, we have

\displaystyle II_{2}

\displaystyle\leq x\int^{\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{x}}_{\frac{1}{x}}J(xu)\Big(\psi(t,x(1-u))-\psi(t,x)\Big)du

\displaystyle\leq x\int^{B}_{\frac{1}{x}}J(xu)\Big(\psi(t,x(1-u))-\psi(t,x)\Big)du+x\int^{\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{x}}_{B}J(xu)\psi(t,x(1-u))du:=II_{4}+II_{5}.

###
Let us estimate
II_{4}.
By Taylor’s theorem and the convexity of
\psi(t,\cdot)
on
[x_{1}(t),+\infty)
,
II_{4}
can be written as

II_{4}=-x^{2}\int^{B}_{\frac{1}{x}}\int_{0}^{1}uJ(xu)\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial x}\Big(t,x(1-\tau u)\Big)d\tau du\leq-x^{2}\int^{B}_{\frac{1}{x}}uJ(xu)\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial x}\Big(t,x(1-u)\Big)du.

Since
y\mapsto\frac{y}{(1-\ln y)^{\alpha}}
is non-decreasing on
(0,1)
and
\psi(t,\cdot)
is non-increasing on
[x_{1}(t),+\infty)
, by (
4.7
), for all
(t,x)\in[\max\{t_{0},t_{2}\},\infty)\times[x_{1}(t),+\infty)
, we get

\displaystyle II_{4}

\displaystyle\leq-x^{2}\int^{B}_{\frac{1}{x}}uJ(xu)\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial x}\Big(t,x(1-u)\Big)du

\displaystyle\leq\gamma_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{J}_{0}x^{-2s}\int^{B}_{\frac{1}{x}}\frac{\ln^{\alpha}x(1-u)}{u^{2s}(1-u)}\frac{\psi(t,x(1-u))}{[1-\ln\psi(t,x(1-u))]^{\alpha}}du

\displaystyle\leq\gamma_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{J}_{0}x^{-2s}(\ln^{\alpha}x)(1-B)^{-1}\frac{\psi(t,x(1-B))}{[1-\ln\psi(t,x(1-B))]^{\alpha}}\int^{B}_{\frac{1}{x}}u^{-2s}du.

On the one hand, by Lemma
4.3
, recalling
B=\frac{\gamma_{4}}{\ln^{\alpha+1}x}
, we have

\frac{\psi(t,x(1-B))}{[1-\ln\psi(t,x(1-B))]^{\alpha}}\leq\frac{\psi(t,x)}{[1-\ln\psi(t,x)]^{\alpha}}\frac{\exp\left\{2(2s)^{\alpha+1}\frac{\gamma_{4}}{\ln x}+C_{\alpha}\frac{\gamma_{4}^{2}}{\ln^{2}x}\right\}}{\left[1-2(2s)^{\alpha+1}\frac{\gamma_{4}}{\ln x}-C_{\alpha}\frac{\gamma_{4}^{2}}{\ln^{2}x}\right]^{\alpha}}.

Thus, by (
4.14
), for all
t\geq\max\{t_{0},t_{2}\}
and
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
, we obtain

\frac{\psi(t,x(1-B))}{[1-\ln\psi(t,x(1-B))]^{\alpha}}\lesssim\frac{\psi(t,x)}{[1-\ln\psi(t,x)]^{\alpha}}.

On the other hand, we have

\int_{\frac{1}{x}}^{B}u^{-2s}du=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{1-2s}\left(B^{1-2s}-\frac{1}{x^{1-2s}}\right),&0<s<\frac{1}{2},\\
\frac{1}{2s-1}\left(x^{2s-1}-\frac{1}{B^{2s-1}}\right),&s>\frac{1}{2},\\
\ln(Bx),&s=\frac{1}{2}.\end{cases}

It follows from
B=\frac{\gamma_{4}}{\ln^{\alpha+1}x}
that

\displaystyle(1-B)^{-1}x^{-2s}\ln^{\alpha}x\int_{\frac{1}{x}}^{B}u^{-2s}du

\displaystyle\lesssim\begin{cases}x^{-2s}\ln^{2s(\alpha+1)-1}x,&0<s<\frac{1}{2},\\
x^{-1}\ln^{\alpha}x,&s>\frac{1}{2},\\
x^{-1}\ln^{\alpha+1}x,&s=\frac{1}{2},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\leq O(x^{-\min\{2s,1\}}\ln^{\alpha+1}x)\hskip 9.24994pt\text{as }x\to+\infty.

As a result, for all
t\geq\max\{t_{0},t_{2}\}
and
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
, we arrive at

II_{4}\leq\mathcal{C}_{3}\frac{\ln^{\alpha+1}x}{x^{\min\{2s,1\}}}\frac{\psi(t,x)}{[1-\ln\psi(t,x)]^{\alpha}},

(4.15)

for some positive constant
\mathcal{C}_{3}
.

###
Let us estimate
II_{5}
.
By (
1.7
), we have

II_{5}\leq\mathcal{J}_{0}x\int^{\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{x}}_{B}\exp\left\{-(2s+1)\ln(xu)+\ln\psi(t,x(1-u))\right\}du.

Since
\psi(t,\cdot)
is log-convex on
[x_{1}(t),+\infty)
by Lemma
4.2
, we have that, for any
x\in[x_{1}(t)+R(t),+\infty)
,
u\mapsto-(2s+1)\ln(xu)+\ln\psi(t,x(1-u))
is convex on
\left(\frac{1}{x},\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{x}\right)
.
It follows that for any
u\in\left(B,\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{x}\right)
,

x\exp\left\{-(2s+1)\ln(xu)+\ln\psi(t,x(1-u))\right\}\leq\frac{x}{(xB)^{2s+1}}\psi(t,x(1-B))+\frac{x}{(x-x_{1}(t))^{2s+1}}.

Let us estimate both terms in the r.h.s. of the above inequality. On the one hand, by Lemma
4.3
and
\psi(t,x)\geq v_{0}(x)=C_{L}\frac{\ln^{q}x}{x^{2s}}
, recalling
B=\frac{\gamma_{4}}{\ln^{\alpha+1}x}
, we get, for all
t
large enough, say
t\geq t_{3}
, and
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
,

\frac{x}{(xB)^{2s+1}}\psi(t,x(1-B))\lesssim x^{-2s}\left(\ln^{(\alpha+1)(2s+1)}x\right)\psi(t,x)\lesssim\frac{\ln^{\bar{q}}x}{x^{s}}\psi^{\frac{3}{2}}(t,x),

where
\bar{q}:=(2s+1)(\alpha+1)-q
. As a result, since
y\mapsto y^{\frac{1}{2}}(1-\ln y)^{\alpha}
is bounded on
(0,1)
, for all
t\geq t_{3}
and
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
, we have

\frac{x}{(xB)^{2s+1}}\psi(t,x(1-B))\leq\mathcal{C}_{4}\frac{\ln^{\bar{q}}x}{x^{s}}\frac{\psi(t,x)}{(1-\ln\psi(t,x))^{\alpha}},

for some positive constant
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.

On the other hand, recalling
R(t)=\gamma_{3}t^{-1}x_{1}(t)
, it follows from (
4.4
) that there is a positive constant
\gamma_{5}
such that for all
t\geq t_{3}
, up to enlarging
t_{3}
if necessary, we have

x_{1}(t)+R(t)-\gamma_{5}R(t)\ln^{\alpha+1}R(t)\leq 0.

One may notice that
x\mapsto x-\gamma_{5}(x-x_{1}(t))\ln^{\alpha+1}(x-x_{1}(t))
is decreasing on
[x_{1}(t)+R(t),+\infty)
for all
t\geq t_{3}
, up to enlarging
t_{3}
if necessary, and then for all
t\geq t_{3}
and
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
,

x-\gamma_{5}(x-x_{1}(t))\ln^{\alpha+1}(x-x_{1}(t))\leq x_{1}(t)+R(t)-\gamma_{5}R(t)\ln^{\alpha+1}R(t)\leq 0.

Thus, since
y\mapsto\frac{\ln^{\alpha+1}y}{y^{2s}}
is decreasing for
y
large enough, by (
4.2
) and (
4.12
), for all
t\geq t_{3}
and
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
, up to enlarging
t_{3}
if necessary,

\frac{x}{(x-x_{1}(t))^{2s+1}}\leq\gamma_{5}\frac{\ln^{\alpha+1}(x-x_{1}(t))}{(x-x_{1}(t))^{2s}}\leq\mathcal{C}_{5}\frac{\kappa^{-1}t^{-p}\psi}{[1-\ln\psi(t,x)]^{q-\alpha-1}},

for some positive constant
\mathcal{C}_{5}
. As a result, for all
t\geq t_{3}
and
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
, we arrive at

II_{5}\leq\mathcal{C}_{4}\frac{\ln^{\bar{q}}x}{x^{s}}\frac{\psi(t,x)}{(1-\ln\psi(t,x))^{\alpha}}+\mathcal{C}_{5}\frac{\kappa^{-1}t^{-p}\psi}{[1-\ln\psi(t,x)]^{q-\alpha-1}}.

(4.16)

##

Let us estimate
II_{3}
.

In view of (
4.10
), we have

II_{3}\leq\mathcal{C}_{6}e^{-\frac{1}{2s}[r(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}\frac{\psi(t,x)}{(1-\ln\psi(t,x))^{\alpha}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }(t,x)\in[\tilde{t}_{1},\infty]\times[x_{1}(t)+R(t),+\infty),

(4.17)

for some positive constant
\mathcal{C}_{6}
.

Owing to (
4.13
), (
4.17
), (
4.15
) and (
4.16
), for all
t\geq\tilde{t}_{2}:=\max\{t_{0},t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},\tilde{t}_{1}\}
and
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
, we have

\mathcal{D}[\Psi](t,x)\leq\left(\mathcal{C}_{3}\frac{\ln^{\alpha+1}x}{x^{\min\{2s,1\}}}+\mathcal{C}_{4}\frac{\ln^{\bar{q}}x}{x^{s}}+\mathcal{C}_{5}\frac{\kappa^{-1}t^{-p}}{(1-\ln\psi(t,x))^{2\alpha-q+1}}+\mathcal{C}_{6}e^{-\frac{1}{2s}[r(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}\right)\frac{\psi(t,x)}{(1-\ln\psi(t,x))^{\alpha}}.

\Box

4.1.3
The function
\Psi
is a super-solution to (
1.1
) at later times

Proposition 4.5
.

Assume
p=\frac{1}{\alpha+1}
,
q=2\alpha+1
and
\kappa=\bar{\kappa}:=\max\left\{e,\frac{2\mathcal{C}_{5}}{\gamma_{1}}\right\}
. There is a time
\bar{t}>t^{\star}
such that for all
t\geq\bar{t}
and
x>x_{1}(t)
, we have

\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial t}(t,x)-\mathcal{D}[\Psi](t,x)-f(\Psi(t,x))\geq 0.

Proof of Proposition
4.5
.

In view of Hypothesis
1.1
, since
\Psi(t,\cdot)=\psi(t,\cdot)
on
(x_{1}(t),+\infty)
, we obtain

f(\Psi(t,\cdot))\leq\frac{r\psi(t,\cdot)}{(1-\ln\psi(t,\cdot))^{\alpha}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{on }(x_{1}(t),+\infty).

(4.18)

We split the proof according to two space zones:
(x_{1}(t),x_{1}(t)+R(t))
and
[x_{1}(t)+R(t),+\infty)
.

#
Zone 1:
x\in(x_{1}(t),x_{1}(t)+R(t))
.

By (
4.6
), (
4.7
), (
4.18
) and part II in Lemma
4.4
, for all
t\geq\tilde{t}_{1}
and
x\in(x_{1}(t),x_{1}(t)+R(t))
, we arrive at

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial t}(t,x)-\mathcal{D}[\Psi](t,x)-f(\Psi(t,x))

\displaystyle\geq\gamma_{1}t^{-\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}\frac{\psi(t,x)}{(1-\ln\psi(t,x))^{\alpha}}-\mathcal{C}_{1}e^{-\frac{1}{2s}[r(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}\frac{\psi(t,x)}{(1-\ln\psi(t,x))^{\alpha}}+\mathcal{J}_{3}R(t)\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial x}(t,x_{1}(t))

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{1}t^{-\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}-\mathcal{C}_{1}e^{-\frac{1}{2s}[r(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}\right)\frac{\psi(t,x)}{(1-\ln\psi(t,x))^{\alpha}}+\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{1}t^{-\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}\frac{\psi(t,x)}{(1-\ln\psi(t,x))^{\alpha}}-\gamma_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{J}_{3}R(t)\frac{\ln^{\alpha}x_{1}(t)}{x_{1}(t)}.

We recall
R(t)=\gamma_{3}t^{-1}x_{1}(t)
for some
\gamma_{3}>0
to be chosen later.
By (
4.5
), we get

x_{\frac{1}{2}}(t)-x_{1}(t)\asymp t^{-\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}+\frac{1}{2s}\left(\frac{q}{\alpha+1}+p\right)}e^{\frac{1}{2s}[r(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}},

and it follows from (
4.4
) that there is
\bar{t}_{1}\geq\tilde{t}_{1}
such that for all
t\geq\bar{t}_{1}
, we have

x_{\frac{1}{2}}(t)\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t),

It follows from the fact that
\psi(t,\cdot)
is non-increasing on
(x_{1}(t),+\infty)
that, for all
t\geq\bar{t}_{1}
and
x\in(x_{1}(t),x_{1}(t)+R(t))
,

\frac{\psi(t,x)}{(1-\ln\psi(t,x))^{\alpha}}\geq\frac{\psi(t,x_{1}(t)+R(t))}{[1-\ln\psi(t,x_{1}(t)+R(t))]^{\alpha}}\geq\frac{\psi\left(t,x_{\frac{1}{2}}(t)\right)}{\left[1-\ln\psi\left(t,x_{\frac{1}{2}}(t)\right)\right]^{\alpha}}=\frac{1}{2(1+\ln 2)^{\alpha}}.

Then, by (
4.4
), for all
t\geq\bar{t}_{1}
and
x\in(x_{1}(t),x_{1}(t)+R(t))
, we achieve

\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{1}t^{-\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}\frac{\psi(t,x)}{(1-\ln\psi(t,x))^{\alpha}}-\gamma_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{J}_{3}R(t)\frac{\ln^{\alpha}x_{1}(t)}{x_{1}(t)}\geq t^{-\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}\left(\frac{\gamma_{1}}{4(1+\ln 2)^{\alpha}}-\mathcal{J}_{3}\gamma_{2}^{-1}\gamma_{3}\frac{\ln^{\alpha}x_{1}(t)}{t^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}}}\right)\geq 0,

by taking
\gamma_{3}
small enough, say
\gamma_{3}\leq\tilde{\gamma}_{3}
.
On the other hand, for
t\geq\bar{t}_{1}
up to enlarging
\bar{t}_{1}
if necessary, we have

\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{1}t^{-\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}-\mathcal{C}_{1}e^{-\frac{1}{2s}[r(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}\geq 0.

Therefore, for all
t\in[\bar{t}_{1},\infty)
and
x\in(x_{1}(t),x_{1}(t)+R(t))
, we achieve

\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial t}(t,x)-\mathcal{D}[\Psi](t,x)-f(\Psi(t,x))\geq 0.

(4.19)

#
Zone 2:
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
.
Let us estimate
\mathcal{D}[\Psi]
for
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
.
In view of (
4.6
) and Hypothesis
1.1
, therefore, we have, for all
t\geq\tilde{t}_{2}
and
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial t}(t,x)-\mathcal{D}[\Psi](t,x)-f(\Psi(t,x))

\displaystyle\geq t^{-\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}\left\{\gamma_{1}-\mathcal{C}_{5}\kappa^{-1}t^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}-p}(1-\ln\psi(t,x))^{2\alpha-q+1}+o_{t\to\infty}(1)\right\}\frac{\psi(t,x)}{(1-\ln\psi(t,x))^{\alpha}}.

To validate the above computations, we need
p\geq\frac{1}{\alpha+1}
,
q\geq 2\alpha+1
and
\kappa\geq\bar{\kappa}
. Thus, we can choose
p=\frac{1}{\alpha+1}
,
q=2\alpha+1
and
\kappa=\bar{\kappa}
.
As a result, for such
p
,
q
and
\kappa
, there is a time
\bar{t}_{2}>\tilde{t}_{2}
such that for all
t\geq\bar{t}_{2}
and
x\geq x_{1}(t)+R(t)
, we have

\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial t}(t,x)-\mathcal{D}[\Psi](t,x)-f(\Psi(t,x))\geq 0.

Therefore, collecting the estimates in the two zones, by taking
\bar{t}:=\max\{\bar{t}_{1},\bar{t}_{2}\}
,
p=\frac{1}{\alpha+1}
,
q=2\alpha+1
and
\kappa=\bar{\kappa}
, for all
t\geq\bar{t}
and
x>x_{1}(t)
, we achieve

\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial t}(t,x)-\mathcal{D}[\Psi](t,x)-f(\Psi(t,x))\geq 0.

\Box

4.1.4
Proof of the upper bound in Theorem
1.7

In view of Hypothesis
1.5
, by (
4.1
), one has
u_{0}\leq v_{0}\leq\Psi(0,\cdot)\leq\Psi(\bar{t},\cdot)
.
Thus, by proposition
4.5
and the fact that
u(t,x)\leq 1=\Psi(t+\bar{t},x)
for all
t>0
and
x\leq x_{1}(t+\bar{t})
, the comparison principle yields
u(t,x)\leq\Psi(t+\bar{t},x)
for all
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}
. Thus, for any
\lambda\in(0,1)
, we have

\lambda\leq u(t,X_{\lambda}(t))\leq\Psi(t+\bar{t},X_{\lambda}(t)),\hskip 9.24994pt\forall t>0.

Therefore, by (
4.2
), there exists
T_{\lambda}>0
such that, for
t>T_{\lambda}
, we have

\displaystyle X_{\lambda}(t)

\displaystyle\leq v^{-1}_{0}\left\{(1+\kappa(t+\bar{t})^{p})^{-1}\exp\left\{1-[(1-\ln\lambda)^{\alpha+1}+r(\alpha+1)(t+\bar{t})]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}\right\}\right\}\lesssim t^{\frac{1}{s}}e^{\frac{1}{2s}[r(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}.

The proof of the upper bound in Theorem
1.7
is now complete.

4.2
The lower bound

To obtain a precise lower bound on the level sets of the solution to (
1.1
), we need to construct a subtle sub-solution. For equation (
1.1
), it turns out that it is not enough to construct a suitable sub-solution by using the solution of the simplified ordinary differential equation
\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}=\frac{ru}{(1-\ln u)^{\alpha}}
. Therefore, we should use the full ordinary differential equation
\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}=\frac{ru}{(1-\ln u)^{\alpha}}(1-u^{p})
for
p\in(0,1)
. Unfortunately, its solution is not explicit, which causes some tedious difficulties in subsequent calculations.

4.2.1
Definition of the sub-solution and basic computations

Let us define

\varphi(t,x)=\exp{\left\{1-\left[\left(1+2s\ln x\right)^{\alpha+1}-r(\alpha+1)[t-h(t,x)]\right]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}\right\}},

where

h(t,x):=\kappa\frac{t^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}}e^{p[r(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}}{x^{2sp}},

for some constants
\kappa\in(0,+\infty)
and
p\in(0,1)
to be chosen later. Here, we list some useful lemmas that we prove in Appendix
B.2
. We first obtain:

Lemma 4.6
.

Assume
\kappa\leq\kappa_{0}:=\min\left\{\frac{[r(\alpha+1)]^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}}}{2^{\alpha+1}rpe^{2p}},\frac{1}{2e^{2p}[r(\alpha+1)]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}},\frac{1}{4\tilde{\gamma}_{1}e^{2p}},\frac{s[r(\alpha+1)]^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}}}{2^{\alpha}r\tilde{\gamma}_{2}}\right\}
, where
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}:=\frac{p}{\alpha+1}[r(\alpha+1)]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}
and
\tilde{\gamma}_{2}:=2sp(2sp+1)e^{2p}
. Let
x_{\Lambda}(t)
be the position of the level set of
\varphi
of height
\Lambda\in(0,e]
, that is,
\varphi(t,x_{\Lambda}(t))=\Lambda
.

(i).

For any
\Lambda\in(0,e)
, there is a time
\tilde{t}>0
such that, for any
t\geq\tilde{t}
and
x\geq x_{\Lambda}(t)
,
\varphi(t,x)
is well-defined, strictly increasing in
t
, and strictly decreasing and convex in
x
.

(ii).

For any
\Lambda\in(0,e)
, the position
x_{\Lambda}(t)
is strictly decreasing in
\Lambda
and unique such that
\varphi(t,x_{\Lambda}(t))=\Lambda
, and satisfies

x_{\Lambda}(t)>e^{-\frac{1}{s}}e^{\frac{1}{2s}\left[r(\alpha+1)t\right]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}.

(4.20)

To make the above lemma valid, we fix
\kappa=\kappa_{0}
in this subsection.
With the above lemma, an estimate of
\varphi
can be derived.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\Lambda\in(0,1)
. We have the following estimate for
\varphi
:

\frac{1}{x^{2s}}\leq\varphi(t,x)\leq\frac{e^{[r(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}}{x^{2s}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ for all }t>\tilde{t}\text{ and }x\geq x_{\Lambda}(t).

(4.21)

For all
t>\tilde{t}
and
x\geq x_{\Lambda}(t)
, we have

\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial t}\leq\frac{r\varphi}{(1-\ln\varphi)^{\alpha}}(1-\tilde{\gamma}_{1}\kappa\varphi^{p}),

(4.22)

where
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}=\frac{p}{\alpha+1}[r(\alpha+1)]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}
, and if
\varphi\leq\min\{e^{\frac{1}{2s}-\alpha},e\}
, then

\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\geq-2s\varphi^{1+\frac{1}{2s}}.

(4.23)

Moreover, we have

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}(t,x_{\Lambda}(t))=0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{uniformly for }\Lambda\in(0,1).

(4.24)

Lemma 4.8
.

For any
\Lambda\in(0,1)
, we have

x_{\Lambda}(t)\asymp_{\Lambda}e^{\frac{1}{2s}[r(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }t\geq\tilde{t},

and for any
0<\Lambda_{1}<\Lambda_{2}<1
, we have

x_{\Lambda_{1}}(t)-x_{\Lambda_{2}}(t)\to+\infty\hskip 9.24994pt\text{as }t\to\infty.

Lemma 4.9
.

For any
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\frac{2}{3}\right)
, there exist a function
g_{\varepsilon}\in C^{2}:[0,1]\rightarrow[0,\varepsilon]
such that

g_{\varepsilon}(y)=y\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }y\in\left[0,\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right]\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}\hskip 9.24994ptg_{\varepsilon}(y)=\varepsilon\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }y\in\left[\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon,1\right],

and for all
y\in(0,1)
,

g_{\varepsilon}(y)\leq y,\hskip 9.24994ptg_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(y)\frac{y}{(1-\ln y)^{\alpha}}\leq\frac{g_{\varepsilon}(y)}{(1-\ln g_{\varepsilon}(y))^{\alpha}},\hskip 9.24994pt0\leq g^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(y)\leq 1\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}\hskip 9.24994ptg_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(y)\geq-C_{\varepsilon}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for some }C_{\varepsilon}>0.

(4.25)

Fix any
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{0}:=\min\{\frac{1}{4},\xi_{0}\}\right)
, where
\xi_{0}
is defined in Hypothesis
1.1
, and let

p=\frac{1}{2}\min\left\{\frac{1}{2s},2s\right\}.

Let
g_{\varepsilon}
be a function given by Lemma
4.9
. For all
t\geq\tilde{t}
, we then define

\Phi(t,x):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\varepsilon,&x<x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t),\\
&g_{\varepsilon}(\varphi(t,x)),&x\geq x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t).\end{aligned}\right.

Observe that for any
t\geq\tilde{t}
,
\Phi(t,\cdot)
is at least
C^{2}
on
\mathbb{R}
.
It follows that for
x<x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)
, we have
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}=0
,
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x}=0
and
\frac{\partial^{2}\Phi}{\partial x^{2}}=0
, while for
x\geq x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)
, by the definition of
g_{\varepsilon}
and Lemma
4.6
, for all
t\geq\tilde{t}
, we have

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}=g_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\varphi)\frac{\varphi}{(1-\ln\varphi)^{\alpha}}\left[r-r\frac{\partial h}{\partial t}(t,x)\right]\leq\frac{r\varphi}{(1-\ln\varphi)^{\alpha}}(1-\tilde{\gamma}_{1}\kappa\varphi^{p}),

(4.26)

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x}=g_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\varphi)\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\geq\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x},

(4.27)

and

\frac{\partial^{2}\Phi}{\partial x^{2}}=g^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi)\left(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{2}+g^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi)\varphi_{xx}\geq-C_{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{2}.

(4.28)

Unless otherwise specified, throughout the subsection, we assume that
t\in[\tilde{t},\infty)
.

4.2.2
Estimation of
\mathcal{D}[\Phi](t,\cdot)
on
\mathbb{R}

Now, let us estimate
\mathcal{D}[\Phi]
. We split into three space zones, as follows.

Lemma 4.10
.

Assume
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
. For all
t\geq\tilde{t}
, we have the following estimate for
\mathcal{D}[\Phi]
.

I.

When
x\in\left(-\infty,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right)
: Given some quantity
B_{1}>1
, we have

\mathcal{D}[\Phi]\geq-\frac{\mathcal{J}_{0}}{2s}\frac{\varepsilon}{B_{1}^{2s}}-C_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{1}^{2B_{1}}y^{1-2s}dy\right)\left(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{2}\left(t,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right).

II.

When
x\in\left[x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t),x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)\right]
: Given some quantity
B_{2}>1
, we have

\mathcal{D}[\Phi](t,x)\geq-\frac{\mathcal{J}_{0}}{2s}\frac{\varepsilon}{B_{2}^{2s}}-C_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{1}^{B_{2}}y^{1-2s}dy\right)\left(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{2}\left(t,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right).

III.

When
x\in\left(x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t),+\infty\right):
For
s\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right]
, given some
B_{3}>1
, we have

\mathcal{D}[\Phi]\geq-\frac{\mathcal{J}_{0}}{2s}\frac{\varepsilon}{B_{3}^{2s}}+\mathcal{J}_{0}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}(t,x)\int_{1}^{B_{3}}y^{-2s}dy,

while, for
s\in\left(\frac{1}{2},+\infty\right)
, we have

\mathcal{D}[\Phi]\geq\frac{\mathcal{J}_{0}}{2s-1}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}(t,x).

Proof of Lemma
4.10
.

We divide the proof of the above lemma into three parts, corresponding to the three zones of positions:
x\in\left(-\infty,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right)
,
x\in\left[x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t),x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)\right]
and
x\in\left(x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t),+\infty\right)
.

#
Start with
I
:
x\in\left(-\infty,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right)
.

We split the interval
\left(-\infty,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right)
into two parts
\left(-\infty,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-B_{1}\right]
and
\left(x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-B_{1},x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right)
with some quantity
B_{1}>1
to be chosen later.

##

When
x\in\left(-\infty,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-B_{1}\right]
:
By the definition of
\mathcal{D}[\Phi]
and (
1.7
), since
\Phi(t,x)=\varepsilon
for
x\leq x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)
, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[\Phi]

\displaystyle=\int_{x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-x}^{+\infty}J(y)\Big(\Phi(t,x+y)-\varepsilon\Big)dy\geq-\varepsilon\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-x}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{y^{1+2s}}dy\geq-\frac{\varepsilon\mathcal{J}_{0}}{2s}\frac{1}{\left(x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-x\right)^{2s}}\geq-\frac{\varepsilon\mathcal{J}_{0}}{2s}\frac{1}{B_{1}^{2s}}.

##

When
x\in\left(x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-B_{1},x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right)
:
By the definition of
\mathcal{D}[\Phi]
, we write

\mathcal{D}[\Phi]=\int_{x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-x+B_{1}}^{+\infty}J(y)\Big(\Phi(t,x+y)-\Phi(t,x)\Big)dy+\int_{x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-x}^{x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-x+B_{1}}J(y)\Big(\Phi(t,x+y)-\Phi(t,x)\Big)dy:=I_{1}+I_{2}.

For the integral
I_{1}
, since
\Phi(t,\cdot)=\varepsilon
on
\left(-\infty,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right)
, by (
1.7
), we get

I_{1}\geq-\varepsilon\int_{x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-x+B_{1}}^{+\infty}J(y)dy\geq-\varepsilon\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-x+B_{1}}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{y^{1+2s}}dy\geq-\frac{\varepsilon\mathcal{J}_{0}}{2s}\frac{1}{B_{1}^{2s}}.

For the integral
I_{2}
, by Taylor’s theorem,
I_{2}
can be written as

I_{2}=\int_{x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-x}^{x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-x+B_{1}}J(y)\Big(\Phi(t,x+y)-\Phi(t,x)\Big)dy=\int_{x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-x}^{x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-x+B_{1}}\int_{0}^{1}yJ(y)\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x}(t,x+\tau y)d\tau dy.

Since
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x}(t,\cdot)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x}(t,\cdot)=0
on
\left(-\infty,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right)
, we get

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-x}^{x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-x+B_{1}}\int_{0}^{1}yJ(y)\left(\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x}(t,x+\tau y)-\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x}(t,x)\right)d\tau dy

\displaystyle=\int_{x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-x}^{x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-x+B_{1}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\tau y^{2}J(y)\frac{\partial^{2}\Phi}{\partial x^{2}}(t,x+\sigma\tau y)d\sigma d\tau dy.

It follows from
\frac{\partial^{2}\Phi}{\partial x^{2}}(t,\cdot)=0
on
\left(-\infty,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right)
that

I_{2}=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-x+B_{1}}\tau y^{2}J(y)\frac{\partial^{2}\Phi}{\partial x^{2}}(t,x+\sigma\tau y)dyd\sigma d\tau.

Since
x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)-x+B_{1}\leq 2B_{1}
, by the convexity of
\varphi(t,\cdot)
, using (
4.28
), we get

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle\geq-C_{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{2}\left(t,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right)\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{2B_{1}}J(y)y^{2}\tau dyd\sigma d\tau

\displaystyle\geq-C_{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{2}\left(t,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right)\left(\int_{0}^{1}J(y)y^{2}dy+\int_{1}^{2B_{1}}J(y)y^{2}dy\right)

\displaystyle\geq-C_{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{2}\left(t,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right)\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{1}^{2B_{1}}y^{1-2s}dy\right),

where the last inequality uses (
1.7
).
As a result, for all
t\geq\tilde{t}
and
x<x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)
, in both cases we obtain

\mathcal{D}[\Phi]\geq-\frac{\mathcal{J}_{0}}{2s}\frac{\varepsilon}{B_{1}^{2s}}-C_{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{2}\left(t,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right)\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{1}^{2B_{1}}y^{1-2s}dy\right).

#

Continue with
II
:

x\in\left[x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t),x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)\right].
Let us estimate
\mathcal{D}[\Phi]
on
\left[x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t),x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)\right]
.
Since
\Phi(t,\cdot)
is non-increasing on
\mathbb{R}
, by (
4.28
), for some
B_{2}>1
, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[\Phi]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{|y|\leq B_{2}}J(y)\Big(\Phi(t,x+y)-2\Phi(t,x)+\Phi(t,x-y)\Big)dy+\int_{|y|>B_{2}}J(y)\Big(\Phi(t,x+y)-\Phi(t,x)\Big)dy

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{|y|\leq B_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}J(y)y^{2}\tau\frac{\partial^{2}\Phi}{\partial x^{2}}(t,x+\sigma\tau y)dy+\int_{y>B_{2}}J(y)\Big(\Phi(t,x+y)-\Phi(t,x)\Big)dy

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{C_{\varepsilon}}{2}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\xi|<B_{2}\\
x+\xi\geq x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon(t)}\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{2}(t,x+\xi)\int_{|y|\leq B_{2}}J(y)y^{2}dy+\int_{y>B_{2}}J(y)\Big(\Phi(t,x+y)-\Phi(t,x)\Big)dy:=II_{1}+II_{2}.

For
II_{1}
, by (
1.7
) and the convexity of
\varphi(t,\cdot)
, we have

\displaystyle II_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{C_{\varepsilon}}{2}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\xi|<B_{2}\\
x+\xi\geq x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon(t)}\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{2}(t,x+\xi)\left(\int_{|y|\leq 1}J(y)y^{2}dy+\int_{1<|y|\leq B_{2}}J(y)y^{2}dy\right)

\displaystyle=-C_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{1}^{B_{2}}y^{1-2s}dy\right)\left(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{2}\left(t,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right),

while for
II_{2}
, since
0\leq\Phi\leq\varepsilon
, by (
1.7
), we have

II_{2}\geq-\Phi(t,x)\int_{y>B_{2}}J(y)dy=-\frac{\mathcal{J}_{0}}{2s}\frac{\varepsilon}{B_{2}^{2s}}.

Therefore, by collecting the estimates of
II_{1}
and
II_{2}
, we achieve

\mathcal{D}[\Phi]\geq-\frac{\mathcal{J}_{0}}{2s}\frac{\varepsilon}{B_{2}^{2s}}-C_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{1}^{B_{2}}y^{1-2s}dy\right)\left(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{2}\left(t,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right).

#

Finally, focus on
III
:

x\in\left(x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t),+\infty\right).

By the definition of
\mathcal{D}[\Phi]
, since
\Phi(t,\cdot)
is non-increasing, we write

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[\Phi]

\displaystyle\geq P.V.\int_{-1}^{1}J(y)\Big(\Phi(t,x+y)-\Phi(t,x)\Big)dy+\int_{1}^{+\infty}J(y)\Big(\Phi(t,x+y)-\Phi(t,x)\Big)dy:=III_{1}+III_{2}.

By (
1.7
) and the convexity of
\Phi(t,\cdot)
on
\left(x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t),+\infty\right)
, we get

III_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}J(y)y^{2}\tau\frac{\partial^{2}\Phi}{\partial x^{2}}(t,x+\sigma\tau y)d\sigma d\tau dy\geq 0.

To end the estimate of
\mathcal{D}[\Phi]
for
x>x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)
, let us consider two cases:
s\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right]
and
s\in\left(\frac{1}{2},+\infty\right)
.

##

Case
1
:
s\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
.
Let us estimate
III_{2}
. For some
B_{3}>1
, we write

III_{2}=\int_{1}^{B_{3}}J(y)\Big(\Phi(t,x+y)-\Phi(t,x)\Big)dy+\int_{B_{3}}^{+\infty}J(y)\Big(\Phi(t,x+y)-\Phi(t,x)\Big)dy:=III_{3}+III_{4}.

By (
4.27
), the convexity of
\varphi(t,\cdot)
and (
1.7
), we have

\displaystyle III_{3}=\int_{1}^{B_{3}}\int_{0}^{1}J(y)y\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x}(t,x+\tau y)d\tau dy\geq\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}(t,x)\int_{1}^{B_{3}}J(y)ydy

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{J}_{0}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}(t,x)\int_{1}^{B_{3}}y^{-2s}dy.

Since
0<\Phi(t,\cdot)<\varepsilon
on
\left(x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t),+\infty\right)
, it follows from (
1.7
) that

III_{4}\geq-\mathcal{J}_{0}\Phi(t,x)\int_{B_{3}}^{+\infty}y^{-1-2s}dy\geq-\frac{\mathcal{J}_{0}}{2s}\frac{\varepsilon}{B_{3}^{2s}}.

As a result, collecting the above estimates, for all
t\geq\tilde{t}
and
x>x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)
, we have

\mathcal{D}[\Phi]\geq-\frac{\mathcal{J}_{0}}{2s}\frac{\varepsilon}{B_{3}^{2s}}+\mathcal{J}_{0}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}(t,x)\int_{1}^{B_{3}}y^{-2s}dy.

##

Case
2
:
s\in\left(\frac{1}{2},+\infty\right)
.
Let us estimate
III_{2}
. By (
4.27
), the convexity of
\varphi(t,\cdot)
and using the fundamental theorem of calculus, we get

III_{2}=\int_{1}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{1}J(y)y\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x}(t,x+\tau y)d\tau dy\geq\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}(t,x)\int_{1}^{+\infty}J(y)ydy.

Since
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}(t,x)\leq 0
, by (
1.7
), we have

III_{2}\geq\frac{\mathcal{J}_{0}}{2s-1}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}(t,x).

As a result, in this case, collecting the above estimates, for all
t\geq\tilde{t}
and
x>x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)
, we have

\mathcal{D}[\Phi]\geq\frac{\mathcal{J}_{0}}{2s-1}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}(t,x).

\Box

4.2.3
The function
\Phi
is a sub-solution to (
1.1
) at later times

In this subsubsection, we show that
\Phi
is a sub-solution to equation (
1.1
) for large times
t
.

Proposition 4.11
.

There exists
\underline{\varepsilon}>0
such that, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\underline{\varepsilon})
, there exists
\underline{t}(\varepsilon)
such that

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}-\mathcal{D}[\Phi]-f(\Phi)\leq 0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }t\geq\underline{t}\text{ and }x\in\mathbb{R}.

Proof of Proposition
4.11
.

To prove the proposition, we split into three space zones:
\left(-\infty,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right)
,
\left[x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t),x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)\right]
and
\left(x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t),+\infty\right)
.

Zone 1:

x\in\left(-\infty,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right).
In view of the definition of
\Phi
and Hypothesis
1.1
, for
x<x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)
, we have

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}=0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}\hskip 9.24994ptf(\Phi)\geq\frac{r\varepsilon}{(1-\ln\varepsilon)^{\alpha}}(1-K\varepsilon).

Now, by the part I of Lemma
4.10
and choosing
B_{1}=\left(\frac{2(1-\ln\varepsilon)^{\alpha}\mathcal{J}_{0}}{sr(1-K\varepsilon)}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2s}}
, it follows that

\mathcal{D}[\Phi]\geq-\frac{1}{4}\frac{r\varepsilon}{(1-\ln\varepsilon)^{\alpha}}(1-K\varepsilon)-C_{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{2}\left(t,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right)\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{1}^{2B_{1}}y^{1-2s}dy\right).

In view of (
4.24
), we have uniform convergence of
\left(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{2}\left(t,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right)
to
0
as
t
goes to infinity. It follows that there is a time
\underline{t}_{1}\geq\tilde{t}
such that for all
t\geq\underline{t}_{1}
and
x<x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)
, we have

C_{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{2}\left(t,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right)\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{1}^{2B_{1}}y^{1-2s}dy\right)\leq-\frac{1}{4}\frac{r\varepsilon}{(1-\ln\varepsilon)^{\alpha}}(1-K\varepsilon).

Thus, for all
t\geq\underline{t}_{1}
and
x<x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)
, we obtain

\mathcal{D}[\Phi]\geq-\frac{1}{2}\frac{r\varepsilon}{(1-\ln\varepsilon)^{\alpha}}(1-K\varepsilon).

Therefore, for all
t\geq\underline{t}_{1}
and
x<x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)
, we achieve

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}-\mathcal{D}[\Phi]-f(\Phi)\leq-\mathcal{D}[\Phi]-\frac{r\varepsilon}{(1-\ln\varepsilon)^{\alpha}}(1-K\varepsilon)\leq 0.

(4.29)

Zone 2:

x\in\left[x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t),x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)\right].

In view of part II of Lemma
4.10
, by (
4.21
) and taking
B_{2}:=x(1+2s\ln x)^{\frac{\alpha}{2s}}+1
, for all
t\geq\tilde{t}
and
x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\leq x\leq x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[\Phi](t,x)

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{\mathcal{J}_{0}}{2s}\frac{\varepsilon\frac{1}{x^{2s}}}{(1+2s\ln x)^{\alpha}}-C_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{1}^{B_{2}}y^{1-2s}dy\right)\left(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{2}\left(t,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right)

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{\mathcal{J}_{0}}{2s}\frac{\varepsilon\varphi(t,x)}{(1-\ln\varphi(t,x))^{\alpha}}-C_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{1}^{B_{2}}y^{1-2s}dy\right)\left(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{2}\left(t,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right).

By (
4.24
), we have uniform convergence of
\left(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{2}\left(t,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right)
to
0
as
t
goes to infinity, and thus, for
x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\leq x\leq x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)
, there is a time
t_{1}
such that for all
t\geq t_{1}
,

\displaystyle C_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{0}\int_{1}^{B_{2}}y^{1-2s}dy\right)\left(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{2}\left(t,x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\right)\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{(1-\ln\varepsilon)^{\alpha}}.

It follows from
\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\leq\varphi\leq\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon
for all
x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\leq x\leq x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)
that

\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{(1-\ln\varepsilon)^{\alpha}}\leq\mathcal{C}_{1}\frac{\varphi^{2}}{(1-\ln\varphi)^{\alpha}},

for some
\mathcal{C}_{1}>0
.
As a result, since
\Phi\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\geq\frac{\varphi}{6}
for all
x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)\leq x\leq x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)
, we achieve, for all
t\geq\underline{t}_{1}:=\max\{t_{1},\tilde{t}\}
and
x\in\left[x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t),x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)\right]
,

\mathcal{D}[\Phi](t,x)\geq-\left(\frac{2\mathcal{J}_{0}}{s}+\mathcal{C}_{1}\right)\frac{\varphi^{2}}{(1-\ln\varphi)^{\alpha}}\geq-r\mathcal{C}_{2}\frac{\Phi\varphi}{(1-\ln\Phi)^{\alpha}},

for some
\mathcal{C}_{2}>0
.

In view of Hypothesis
1.1
, since
0<\Phi\leq\varepsilon\leq\xi_{0}
and
\Phi\leq\varphi
for any
x>x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t)
, we have for all
x\in\left[x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t),x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)\right]
,

f(\Phi)\geq\frac{r\Phi}{(1-\ln\Phi)^{\alpha}}(1-K\varphi).

By (
4.25
) and (
4.26
), we have

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}=rg_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\varphi)\frac{\varphi}{(1-\ln\varphi)^{\alpha}}\left(1-\tilde{\gamma}_{1}\kappa\varphi^{p}\right)\leq\frac{r\Phi}{(1-\ln\Phi)^{\alpha}}\left(1-\tilde{\gamma}_{1}\kappa\varphi^{p}\right)

Therefore, we have, for all
t\geq\underline{t}_{1}
and
x\in\left[x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t),x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)\right]
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}-\mathcal{D}[\Phi]-f(\Phi)\leq

\displaystyle-\frac{r\Phi}{(1-\ln\Phi)^{\alpha}}\left(\tilde{\gamma}_{1}\kappa-\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}(K+\mathcal{C}_{2})\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\varphi^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq 0,

(4.30)

as long as
\varepsilon\leq\tilde{\varepsilon}:=\frac{2}{3}\left(\frac{\tilde{\gamma}_{1}\kappa}{K+\mathcal{C}_{2}}\right)^{2}
.

Zone 3:

x\in\left(x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t),+\infty\right).
Let us show that
\Phi
is a sub-solution for all
x>x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)
.
In view of Hypothesis
1.1
, since
\Phi=\varphi
for all
x>x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)
, we have, for all
t\geq\tilde{t}
and
x>x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)
,

f(\Phi)\geq\frac{r\varphi}{(1-\ln\varphi)^{\alpha}}(1-K\varphi).

#

Case 1:
s>\frac{1}{2}
.

In this case,
p=\frac{1}{2}\min\left\{\frac{1}{2s},2s\right\}=\frac{1}{4s}
.
In view of part III of Lemma
4.10
, by (
4.23
) and letting
\varepsilon<e^{\frac{1}{2s}-\alpha}
, we have for all
t\geq\tilde{t}
and
x>x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)
,

\mathcal{D}[\Phi]\geq\frac{\mathcal{J}_{0}}{2s-1}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}(t,x)\geq-\frac{2s\mathcal{J}_{0}}{2s-1}\varphi^{1+\frac{1}{2s}}.

Since
y\mapsto y^{\frac{1}{4s}}(1-\ln y)^{\alpha}
is non-decreasing for all
y\in\left(0,e^{1-4s\alpha}\right)
and
0<\varphi<\varepsilon
for all
x>x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)
, by (
4.26
) and letting
\varepsilon<\min\left\{e^{\frac{1}{2s}-\alpha},e^{1-4s\alpha},\xi_{0}\right\}
, we obtain for all
t\geq\tilde{t}
and
x>x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}-\mathcal{D}[\Phi]-f(\Phi)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi^{1+\frac{1}{4s}}}{(1-\ln\varphi)^{\alpha}}\left\{-r\tilde{\gamma}_{1}\kappa+\mathcal{J}_{0}\frac{2s}{2s-1}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{4s}}(1-\ln\varepsilon)^{\alpha}+rK\varepsilon^{1-\frac{1}{4s}}\right\}.

Notice that
\mathcal{J}_{0}\frac{2s}{2s-1}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{4s}}(1-\ln\varepsilon)^{\alpha}+rK\varepsilon^{1-\frac{1}{4s}}
goes to
0
as
\varepsilon
goes to
0^{+}
. Therefore, there is
\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\in\left(0,\min\left\{e^{1-4s\alpha},e^{\frac{1}{2s}-\alpha},\xi_{0}\right\}\right)
such that for any
\varepsilon\leq\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}
, we have

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}-\mathcal{D}[\Phi]-f(\Phi)\leq 0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ for all }t\geq\tilde{t}\text{ and }x>x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t).

#

Case 2:
s=\frac{1}{2}
.

By the definition of
p
, one may notice
p=\frac{1}{2}\min\left\{\frac{1}{2s},2s\right\}=\frac{1}{2}
. In view of (
4.23
), by letting
\varepsilon\leq e^{\frac{1}{2s}-\alpha}
, we have

\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\geq-\varphi^{2}\geq-\varepsilon^{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }t\geq\tilde{t}\text{ and }x>x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t).

In view of part III of Lemma
4.10
, since the map
y\mapsto y(\ln\varepsilon-\ln y)
is non-decreasing for
y\in\left(0,\frac{\varepsilon}{e}\right)
, by letting
\varepsilon\leq\min\left\{e^{-1},e^{\frac{1}{2s}-\alpha}\right\}
and taking
B_{3}=\varepsilon\left(-\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{-1}\left[\ln\varepsilon-\ln\left(-\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)\right]^{-1}
, we get

\mathcal{D}[\Phi]\geq-\mathcal{J}_{0}\frac{\varepsilon}{B_{3}}+\mathcal{J}_{0}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}(t,x)\ln B_{3}\geq 2\mathcal{J}_{0}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\left[\ln\varepsilon-\ln\left(-\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)\right]\geq-2\mathcal{J}_{0}\varphi^{2}(\ln\varepsilon-2\ln\varphi).

Hence, by using (
4.26
), since
y\mapsto y^{\frac{1}{2}}(1-2\ln y)^{\alpha+1}
is non-decreasing for all
y\in\left(0,e^{-\frac{3}{2}-2\alpha}\right)
and
0<\varphi<\varepsilon
for all
x>x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)
, we arrive at, for any
0<\varepsilon\leq\min\left\{e^{-1},e^{\frac{1}{2s}-\alpha},e^{-\frac{3}{2}-2\alpha}\right\}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}-\mathcal{D}[\Phi]-f(\Phi)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi^{\frac{3}{2}}}{(1-\ln\varphi)^{\alpha}}\left[-r\tilde{\gamma}_{1}\kappa+2\mathcal{J}_{0}\varphi^{\frac{1}{2}}(\ln\varepsilon-2\ln\varphi)^{\alpha+1}+rK\varphi^{\frac{1}{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle-\frac{\varphi^{\frac{3}{2}}}{(1-\ln\varphi)^{\alpha}}\left(r\kappa\tilde{\gamma}_{1}-2\mathcal{J}_{0}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\lvert\ln\varepsilon\rvert^{\alpha+1}-rK\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\right).

It therefore follows from the fact that
2\mathcal{J}_{0}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\lvert\ln\varepsilon\rvert^{\alpha+1}+rK\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}
goes to
0
as
\varepsilon
goes to
0^{+}
that there is
\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\in\left(0,\min\left\{e^{-1},e^{-\frac{1}{2}-\alpha},\xi_{0}\right\}\right)
such that for all
0<\varepsilon\leq\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}
, we have

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}-\mathcal{D}[\Phi]-f(\Phi)\leq 0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ for all }t\geq\tilde{t}\text{ and }x\geq x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t).

#

Case 3:
s<\frac{1}{2}
.

In this case,
p=\frac{1}{2}\min\left\{\frac{1}{2s},2s\right\}=s
. In view of part III of Lemma
4.10
, by using (
4.23
), taking
B_{3}=\varepsilon\left(-\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{-1}>1
and letting
\varepsilon\leq e^{\frac{1}{2s}-\alpha}
, we have for all
t\geq\tilde{t}
and
x>x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[\Phi]\geq-\frac{\mathcal{J}_{0}}{2s}\frac{\varepsilon}{B_{3}^{2s}}+\frac{1}{1-2s}\mathcal{J}_{0}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}(t,x)B_{3}^{1-2s}

\displaystyle=-(2s)^{-2s}\mathcal{C}_{3}\varepsilon^{1-2s}\left(-\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right)^{2s}\geq-\mathcal{C}_{3}\varepsilon^{1-2s}\varphi^{2s+1},

for some positive constant
\mathcal{C}_{3}
.
Thus, since
y\mapsto y^{s}(1-\ln y)^{\alpha}
is non-decreasing for all
y\in\left(0,e^{1-\frac{\alpha}{s}}\right)
and
0<\varphi<\varepsilon
for all
x>x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)
, by letting
\varepsilon\leq\min\left\{e^{\frac{1}{2s}-\alpha},e^{1-\frac{\alpha}{s}},\xi_{0}\right\}
, we arrive at for all
t\geq\tilde{t}
and
x>x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t)
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}-\mathcal{D}[\Phi]-f(\Phi)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi^{1+s}}{(1-\ln\varphi)^{\alpha}}\left[-r\tilde{\gamma}_{1}\kappa+\mathcal{C}_{3}\varepsilon^{1-2s}\varphi^{s}(1-\ln\varphi)^{\alpha}+rK\varphi^{1-s}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle-\frac{\varphi^{1+s}}{(1-\ln\varphi)^{\alpha}}\left(r\kappa\tilde{\gamma}_{1}-\mathcal{C}_{3}\varepsilon^{1-s}(1-\ln\varepsilon)^{\alpha}-rK\varepsilon^{1-s}\right).

It therefore follows from
\mathcal{C}_{3}\varepsilon^{1-s}(1-\ln\varepsilon)^{\alpha}-rK\varepsilon^{1-s}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
that there is
\bar{\varepsilon}_{3}\in\left(0,\min\left\{e^{\frac{1}{2s}-\alpha},e^{1-\frac{\alpha}{s}},\xi_{0}\right\}\right)
, for any
0<\varepsilon\leq\bar{\varepsilon}_{3}
,

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}-\mathcal{D}[\Phi]-f(\Phi)\leq 0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ for all }t\geq\tilde{t}\text{ and }x\geq x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t).

Let
\bar{\varepsilon}:=\min\{\bar{\varepsilon}_{1},\bar{\varepsilon}_{2},\bar{\varepsilon}_{3}\}
. As a result, collecting results in three cases, for any
0<\varepsilon\leq\bar{\varepsilon}
, we have

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}-\mathcal{D}[\Phi]-f(\Phi)\leq 0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ for all }t\geq\tilde{t}\text{ and }x\geq x_{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}(t).

(4.31)

Therefore, combining (
4.29
), (
4.30
) and (
4.31
), by letting
\underline{t}:=\max\{\underline{t}_{1},\underline{t}_{2},\tilde{t}\}
and
\varepsilon^{*}:=\min\{\tilde{\varepsilon},\bar{\varepsilon}\}
, we conclude for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon^{*})
,

\Phi
is a sub-solution for all
t\geq\underline{t}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

\Box

4.2.4
Proof of the lower bound in Theorem
1.7

To get the lower bound on the level set
E_{\lambda}(t)
using the sub-solution
\Phi
, we only need to find two times
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
and a position
R^{\star}
, such that

u(T_{1},x-R^{\star})\geq\Phi(T_{2},x)\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }x\in\mathbb{R}.

(4.32)

If so, by using the parabolic comparison principle, we can achieve

u(t+T_{1},x-R^{\star})\geq\Phi(t+T_{2},x)\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }t>0\text{ and }x\in\mathbb{R}.

It follows from the definition of
\Phi
that

u(t+T_{1},x-R^{\star})\geq\varepsilon\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }t>0\text{ and }x\leq x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t+T_{2}).

Thus, for a small height
\lambda\in(0,\varepsilon)
, by Lemma
4.8
, there is a time
T_{\lambda}>0
such that, for all
t\geq T_{\lambda}
,

X_{\lambda}(t)\geq x_{\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon}(t+T_{2}-T_{1})-R^{\star}\gtrsim e^{\frac{1}{2s}[r(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}.

Meanwhile, by using the argument in subsubsection
3.2.5
, for all
\lambda\in(0,1)
, we obtain

X_{\lambda}(t)\gtrsim e^{\frac{1}{2s}[r(\alpha+1)t]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for }t\text{ large enough}.

To complete this step, we need to proof the remaining claim (
4.32
). The comparison principle implies that
u(t,x)\geq\underline{u}(t,x)
, where
\underline{u}(t,x)
is given by

\begin{cases}\frac{\partial\underline{u}}{\partial t}(t,x)=\mathcal{D}[\underline{u}](t,x),&t>0,\ x\in\mathbb{R},\\
\underline{u}(0,x)=u_{0}(x),&x\in\mathbb{R}.\end{cases}

(4.33)

Lemma 4.12

(
[
8
]
)
.

Assume that the kernel
J
and the initial data
u_{0}
satisfy Hypothesis
1.2
and Hypothesis
1.5
, respectively.
Then, there exists a constant
\Gamma>0
, such that the solution to (
4.33
) has the following asymptotic flattening behaviour at infinity:

\forall t\in(0,+\infty),\hskip 9.24994pt\lim_{x\to+\infty}x^{2s}\underline{u}(t,x)\geq\Gamma t.

By the above lemma, for some
T_{1}>\frac{1}{\Gamma}e^{[r(\alpha+1)\underline{t}]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}
, there is
\xi_{1}>1
such that for all
x\geq\xi_{1}
, we have
u(T_{1},x)\geq\underline{u}(T_{1},x)\geq\Gamma\frac{T_{1}}{x^{2s}}
.
In view of the definition of
\Phi
, by (
4.21
), denoting
T_{2}=\underline{t}
, we have for all
x\geq\xi_{1}
,

\Phi(T_{2},x)\leq\frac{e^{[r(\alpha+1)T_{2}]^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}}}{x^{2s}}\leq\Gamma\frac{T_{1}}{x^{2s}}\leq u(T_{1},x).

On the other hand, since propagation for Allee effect occurs (see
[
2
]
), the comparison implies that there is
\xi_{2}
such that
u(t,\xi_{2})\geq\varepsilon
, then, since
u(t,\cdot)
is non-increasing, we have

u(t,x)\geq\Phi(T_{2},x)\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }t>0\text{ and }x\leq\xi_{2}.

As a result, by taking
R^{\star}=|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|
, we achieve

u(T_{1},x-R^{\star})\geq\Phi(T_{2},x)\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }x\in\mathbb{R}.

5
A numerical method based on an IMEX scheme

In this section, we present an efficient numerical method based on Implicit-explicit scheme to get approximations to the singular integro-differential equation to (
1.1
). It is worth mentioning that the combination of non-local operators and accelerated propagation makes the numerical approximation very difficult and costly.

To do so, we truncate the real line to work on a bounded interval
\Omega:=[0,L]
for some
L>0
and impose an exterior boundary condition

g(x)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &1,&x<0,\\
&0,&x>L.\end{aligned}\right.

In other words, we consider the solution to the following initial value problem with Dirichlet boundary conditions, on a finite time interval
[0,T]
, for some
T>0
:

\begin{cases}\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}(t,x)-\mathcal{D}[u](t,x)-f(u(t,x))=0,&t\in(0,T),\ x\in\Omega,\\
u(t,x)=g(x),&t\in(0,T),\ x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\Omega,\\
u(0,x)=u_{0}(x),&x\in\Omega.\end{cases}

(5.1)

5.1
Discretisation of the singular integral operator
\mathcal{D}[\cdot]

Let us explain how we discretise the singular integral operator
\mathcal{D}[\cdot]
under Hypothesis
1.2
. We start from

\mathcal{D}[u](x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\Big(u(x-y)+u(x+y)-2u(x)\Big)J(y)dy,

that is obtained using the symmetry of
J
.
Without loss of generality, we assume that the closure of the interval
\Omega
belongs to
\mathbb{R}^{+}
and contains the singular point of the kernel dispersal
J
.
The integral operator
\mathcal{D}[\cdot]
involves a singular integrand, which is typically handled by decomposing it into a singular part and a smooth part, allowing standard quadrature methods to be applied. This strategy has been applied to both convolution (see
[
34
]
) and fractional Laplace operators (see
[
19
,
29
,
32
,
7
]
). Inspired by these works,
we write, for
x\in\Omega
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[u](x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\frac{u(x-y)+u(x+y)-2u(x)}{y^{\gamma}}y^{\gamma}J(y)dy+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus\Omega}\Big(u(x-y)+u(x+y)-2u(x)\Big)J(y)dy

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{D}_{1}[u](x)+\mathcal{D}_{2}[u](x),

where the parameter
\gamma
satisfies the following condition

\gamma\in[0,2]\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}\hskip 9.24994pty\mapsto y^{\gamma}J(y)\in L^{1}(\Omega).

(5.2)

For example, if
J
belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, then
\mathcal{D}[u]
is the convolution operator
J*u-u
with an integrable kernel. In this case, any
\gamma\in[0,2]
fits. One may notice that if one takes
J(y)=\frac{C_{1,s}}{|y|^{1+2s}}
with some
s\in(0,1)
and
C_{1,s}=\frac{2^{2s}\Gamma\left(\frac{1+2s}{2}\right)}{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma(1-s)}
, the operator
-\mathcal{D}[u]
is the fractional Laplace operator

\left(-\triangle\right)^{s}u(x)=C_{1,s}P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{u(x)-u(y)}{|x-y|^{1+2s}}dy,

and the condition (
5.2
) becomes
\gamma\in(2s,2].

Moreover, if
J
satisfies Hypothesis
1.2
, then
\gamma=2
is the only left choice.

To approximate
\mathcal{D}_{1}[u]
on
\Omega
, assuming
u
is
C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, for
x\in\Omega
, we define

U_{\gamma}(x,y)=\frac{u(x-y)+u(x+y)-2u(x)}{y^{\gamma}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }y\neq 0,

and

U_{\gamma}(x,0)=\begin{cases}0,&\gamma\neq 2,\\
u^{\prime\prime}(x),&\gamma=2.\end{cases}

The function
U(x,\cdot)
belongs to
C(\mathbb{R})
, since

\lim_{y\to 0^{+}}U_{\gamma}(x,y)=u^{\prime\prime}(x)\lim_{y\to 0^{+}}y^{2-\gamma}=U_{\gamma}(x,0),

(5.3)

using the L’Hôpital’s rule.
For some positive integer
N
, let
\{y_{n}\}_{n=0,1,\dots,N}
be a partition of
\Omega
such that
y_{0}<y_{1}<\cdots<y_{N-1}<y_{N}
where
y_{0}
and
y_{N}
are the endpoints of
\Omega
.
Then, denoting
I_{n}:=[y_{n-1},y_{n}]
and
{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{n}:=\int_{I_{n}}y^{\gamma}J(y)dy
for
n\in\{1,\dots,N\}
, we split
\mathcal{D}_{1}[u]
into
N
parts:

\mathcal{D}_{1}[u](x)=\int_{\Omega}U_{\gamma}(x,y)y^{\gamma}J(y)dy=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\int_{I_{n}}U_{\gamma}(x,y)y^{\gamma}J(y)dy.

To help readability, we denote by
{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{n}:=\int_{I_{n}}y^{\gamma}J(y)dy
for
n\in\{1,\dots,N\}
. On the one hand, for
n\in\{2,3,\dots,N\}
, by using the weighted trapezoidal rule, we obtain

\int_{I_{n}}U_{\gamma}(x,y)y^{\gamma}J(y)dy=\frac{{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{n}}{2}[U_{\gamma}(x,y_{n})+U_{\gamma}(x,y_{n-1})].

On the other hand, for
n=1
, since this part contains the singular integral, we apply (
5.3
) and the weighted trapezoidal rule to obtain

\int_{I_{1}}U_{\gamma}(x,y)y^{\gamma}J(y)dy=\frac{{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{1}}{2}[U_{\gamma}(x,y_{1})+U_{\gamma}(x,0)].

As a result, the numerical approximation of
\mathcal{D}_{2}[u]
on
\Omega
is given by:

\mathcal{D}_{1}[u](x)=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{n}}{2}[U_{\gamma}(x,y_{n})+U_{\gamma}(x,y_{n-1})].

(5.4)

Notice that for all
x\in\Omega
, if
y
belongs to
\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus\Omega
, then
x\pm y\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\Omega
. Thus, by use the boundary condition
g
, the numerical approximation of
\mathcal{D}_{2}[u]
on
\Omega
can be written as

\mathcal{D}_{2}[u](x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus\Omega}\Big(g(x-y)+g(x+y)\Big)J(y)dy-2u(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus\Omega}J(y)dy=(1-2u(x))\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus\Omega}J(y)dy.

(5.5)

Therefore, collecting (
5.4
) and (
5.5
), we achieve the numerical approximation of the singular integral operator
\mathcal{D}[\cdot]
as, for all
x\in\Omega
,

\mathcal{D}[u](x)\approx\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{n}}{2}[U_{\gamma}(x,y_{n})+U_{\gamma}(x,y_{n-1})]+(1-2u(x))\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus\Omega}J(y)dy.

(5.6)

In practical applications, the integral
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus\Omega}J(y)dy

can be achieved either through straightforward analytical calculations or by taking advantage of available software packages. For example, the
quad
function from the
scipy.integrate
library in Python can be used for numerical integration.

5.2
Toeplitz equation

In this subsection, we explain the discretisation of equation (
5.1
). More precisely, we use finite differences in space and an IMEX scheme in time. The diffusion term
\mathcal{D}[\cdot]
is dealt implicitly, while the reaction term
f
is integrated explicitly.

We work on the time interval
[0,T]
and the space
\Omega:=[0,L]
.
We use a uniform Cartesian grid
\{(t_{i},x_{j})=(i{\Delta}t,j{\Delta}x)|(i,j)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{Z}\}
6
6

6

In this paper, we use
\mathbb{N}
to denote the set of non-negative integers, i.e., {0,1,2,3,…}.
, with the time step
{\Delta}t=\frac{T}{M}
and the space step
{\Delta}x=\frac{L}{N}
for some positive integers
M
and
N
.
Some notations should be introduced:

u_{i,j}=u(t_{i},x_{j}),\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{D}_{i,j}=\mathcal{D}[u](t_{i},x_{j})\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}\hskip 9.24994ptf_{i,j}=f(u_{i,j}).

Denote
y_{n}:=n{\Delta}x
with
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{n}:=\int_{y_{n-1}}^{y_{n}}y^{\gamma}J(y)dy
for
n\in\{1,\dots,N\}
. In view of (
5.6
), if
x_{j}\in\Omega=[0,L]
and
y_{n}\in(L,+\infty)
, then
x_{j}-y_{n}\in(-\infty,0)
and
x_{j}+y_{n}\in(L,+\infty)
, whence by the extended boundary value, for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,

u_{i,j}=1\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for }j<0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}\hskip 9.24994ptu_{i,j}=0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for }j>N.

Thus, in view of (
5.6
), denoting

U^{i}_{j,n}=\frac{u_{i,j-n}+u_{i,j+n}-2u_{i,j}}{(n{\Delta}x)^{\gamma}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for }n\neq 0,

and

U^{i}_{j,0}=\begin{cases}0,&\gamma\neq 2,\\
\frac{u_{i,j-1}+u_{i,j+1}-2u_{i,j}}{({\Delta}x)^{2}},&\gamma=2,\end{cases}

the approximate formula of
\mathcal{D}_{i,j}
can be written as

\mathcal{D}_{i,j}\approx\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{n}}{2}[U^{i}_{j,n}+U^{i}_{j,n-1}]-2\mathcal{J}_{L}u_{i,j}+\mathcal{J}_{L},

where
\mathcal{J}_{L}:=\int_{L}^{+\infty}J(y)dy
.
Here, we use the central difference approximation for
u^{\prime\prime}(x)
to determine the value of
U^{i}_{j,0}
when
\gamma=2
, which equals to
U^{i}_{j,1}
.

Furthermore, by using the method of finite differences, the approximation of the first equation in (
5.1
) is:

\frac{u_{i+1,j}-u_{i,j}}{{\Delta}t}-\mathcal{D}_{i+1,j}=f_{i,j}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for each }(i,j)\in\{0,1,\dots,M-1\}\times\{0,1,\dots,N\}.

Rearranging the above discretized equation, we get

u_{i+1,j}-{\Delta}t\mathcal{D}_{i+1,j}=u_{i,j}+{\Delta}tf_{i,j},

which is, in matrix form,

(\mathcal{A}_{i+1}+\mathcal{B}(\gamma))X_{i+1}=\mathcal{E}_{i}+\mathcal{F}(\gamma),

(5.7)

where
X_{i}=(u_{i,0},u_{i,1},\dots,u_{i,N})^{T}
.
Using
E_{N+1}
to represent an
(N+1)
-dimensional identity matrix, the matrixes
\mathcal{A}_{i+1}
and
\mathcal{B}(\gamma)
are given by

\resizebox{22609920}{}{$\left(1+2{\Delta}t\mathcal{J}_{L}\right)E_{N+1}-{\Delta}t\begin{pmatrix}&-\frac{{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{1}}{({\Delta}x)^{\gamma}}-\sum_{n=2}^{N}{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{n}\left[\frac{1}{(n{\Delta}x)^{\gamma}}+\frac{1}{((n-1){\Delta}x)^{\gamma}}\right]&\frac{{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{1}+{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{2}}{2({\Delta}x)^{\gamma}}&\dots&\frac{{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{N}+{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{N+1}}{2(N{\Delta}x)^{\gamma}}\\
&\frac{{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{1}+{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{2}}{2({\Delta}x)^{\gamma}}&-\frac{{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{1}}{({\Delta}x)^{\gamma}}-\sum_{n=2}^{N}{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{n}\left[\frac{1}{(n{\Delta}x)^{\gamma}}+\frac{1}{((n-1){\Delta}x)^{\gamma}}\right]&\dots&\frac{{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{N-1}+{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{N}}{2((N-1){\Delta}x)^{\gamma}}\\
&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
&\frac{{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{N}+{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{N+1}}{2(N{\Delta}x)^{\gamma}}&\frac{{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{N-1}+{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{N}}{2((N-1){\Delta}x)^{\gamma}}&\dots&-\frac{{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{1}}{({\Delta}x)^{\gamma}}-\sum_{n=2}^{N}{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{n}\left[\frac{1}{(n{\Delta}x)^{\gamma}}+\frac{1}{((n-1){\Delta}x)^{\gamma}}\right]\end{pmatrix}$},

and

\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{B}(\gamma)=0_{(N+1)\times(N+1)}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for }\gamma\neq 2,\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{B}(2)=\frac{{\Delta}t{\Delta}_{1}^{\gamma}}{({\Delta}x)^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}&1&-\frac{1}{2}&&&\\
&-\frac{1}{2}&1&\ddots&&\\
&&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&&\ddots&1&-\frac{1}{2}\\
&&&&-\frac{1}{2}&1\\
\end{pmatrix},

where
{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{n}=\int_{y_{n-1}}^{y_{n}}y^{\gamma}J(y)dy
with
y_{n}=n{\Delta}x
.
Denoting
{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{N+1}=0
,
F_{i}=(f_{i,0},f_{i,1},\dots,f_{i,N})^{T}
and
e_{N+1}={\underbrace{(1,1,\dots,1)}_{N+1\ \text{ elements}}}^{T}
, the vectors
\mathcal{E}_{i}
and
\mathcal{F}(\gamma)
are given by

\mathcal{E}_{i}:=X_{i}+{\Delta}tF_{i}+{\Delta}t\mathcal{J}_{L}e_{N+1}+{\Delta}t\begin{pmatrix}&\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{n}+{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{n+1}}{2(n{\Delta}x)^{\gamma}}\\
&\sum_{n=2}^{N}\frac{{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{n}+{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{n+1}}{2(n{\Delta}x)^{\gamma}}\\
&\vdots\\
&\sum_{n=N}^{N}\frac{{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{n}+{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{n+1}}{2(n{\Delta}x)^{\gamma}}\\
&0\\
\end{pmatrix},

and

\mathcal{F}(\gamma)=0_{(N+1)\times 1}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for }\gamma\neq 2,\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{F}(2)=\left(\frac{{\Delta}t{\Delta}_{1}^{\gamma}}{2({\Delta}x)^{2}},0,\dots,0\right)^{T}_{N+1}.

One may observe that since the matrix
\mathcal{A}_{i+1}+\mathcal{B}(\gamma)
is a Toeplitz matrix, (
5.7
) is a Toeplitz system. See
[
28
, Chapter 5.2.6]
for a method of resolution of such systems.

5.3
Numerical experiments

In this subsection, we shall make some numerical experiments to illustrate our previous theoretical results. The influence of the tail of the dispersal kernel
J
on the rate of propagation is illustrated for some fixed
\alpha
in Figures
4
,
5
,
6
and
7
. We consider sub-exponential and algebraic kernels to illustrate our main results.

As the previous discussion about (
5.2
) in Section
5.1
, under Hypothesis
1.2
, the splitting parameter
\gamma
should be chosen to be equal to
2
.
In the following simulations, we always assume that
L>1
,
z^{2}J(z)=1
for
z\in[-1,1]
, so that
J
satisfies
\int_{|y|\leq 1}y^{2}J(y)dy=2
, and that the initial data
u_{0}:=\mathds{1}_{x\leq 0}
, and that
f(u)=\frac{u(1-u)}{(1-\ln u)^{\alpha}}
.

5.3.1
Sub-exponential kernel

To illustrate theoretical results given in Theorems
1.3
and
1.6
for a sub-exponential kernel, we take
J(|z|)=e^{1-|z|^{\beta}}
with
\beta>0
for
|z|\geq 1
. Thus, recalling
{\Delta}^{\gamma}_{n}=\int_{y_{n-1}}^{y_{n}}y^{\gamma}J(y)dy
with
y_{n}=n{\Delta}x
for
n\in\{1,\dots,N\}
, we have

{\Delta}^{2}_{n}=\begin{cases}\int_{y_{n-1}}^{y_{n}}y^{2}e^{1-y^{\beta}}dy,&\text{for }\left[\frac{N}{L}\right]+1\leq n\leq N,\\
{\Delta}x,&\text{for }1\leq n\leq\left[\frac{N}{L}\right],\end{cases}

where the integral
\int_{y_{n-1}}^{y_{n}}y^{2}e^{1-y^{\beta}}dy
can be numerically evaluated using the
quad
function from the
scipy.integrate
library in Python.

For this type of dispersal kernel
J
, Figures
4
and
5
demonstrate that acceleration occurs when
\beta
is small, and the time range over which this phenomenon can be observed becomes shorter as
\beta
decreases.
